The Account of the Precious Scriptures and the Lineage of the Śākyas
Author
Paltsek
Catalog
Toh 4357 (Tōhoku catalogue; also cited as D 4357 of the Derge edition) · Derge Tengyur · Miscellaneous (sna tshogs) · Commentary / Treatise (Śāstra)

About this text

The Account of the Precious Scriptures and the Lineage of the Śākyas, known in Tibetan as Sungrab Rinpoché Tamgyü dang Shakyé Rabgyü, is Toh 4357 in the Tengyur, where it stands among the miscellaneous compilations and anthologies rather than the commentaries on a single root text. According to its colophon it was assembled by the preceptor Paltsek, one of the great Tibetan translators of the imperial period, as a gathering of passages drawn from many sūtras. It strings together quotations and short narratives on themes such as the obstacles Māra raises for those who strive for liberation, the workings of karma, the faults of liquor and sense pleasure, the genealogy of the Śākya clan from which the Buddha was born, and the honoring of the Buddha's relics and the building of stūpas. It closes with extracts from the Perfection of Wisdom on the emptiness of beings and with the verses of the Gaṇḍavyūha in praise of the bodhisattva Avalokiteśvara, making the whole work a kind of reader's anthology of the scriptures for Tibetan students.

Translation

Herein are contained the Account of the Precious Scriptures and the Lineage of the Śākyas.

In the Heap of Jewels (Ratnakūṭa) it is said: "Māra creates obstacles for sentient beings who strive to emerge from saṃsāra, but not for those who do not strive."

In the Sūtra Teaching the Inconceivable Domain of the Buddhas, the noble Mañjuśrī taught the profound Dharma, and Subhūti said, "You teach a Dharma that does not look after beginners." Mañjuśrī said, "When a physician gives medicinal roots to cure an illness, it is not in order to bring comfort right away." And the Blessed One said, "In the future, whoever has faith in this profound Dharma, if he belongs to the śrāvaka vehicle, will be counted among the three assemblies of Maitreya; if he belongs to the bodhisattva vehicle, he will attain one or another of the forbearances."

In the Sūtra on Generating the Power of Faith: "Infinite wisdom is wisdom that is spontaneously present with regard to all worldly and world-transcending activities and crafts throughout all the countless world systems of the ten directions."

In the Teaching of Akṣayamati: "The inexhaustible moral discipline of bodhisattvas should be regarded as inexhaustible because its continuity is never exhausted. Why is that? The moral discipline of ordinary beings is exhausted with their places of rebirth. That of outsiders who possess the higher knowledges is exhausted along with those higher knowledges. That of humans is exhausted through the exhaustion of the ten virtuous paths of action. That of the gods of the desire realm, through the exhaustion of their merit. That of the form realm, through the exhaustion of the immeasurables and the meditative concentrations. The moral discipline of the gods of the formless realm is exhausted once what was produced by the absorptions they entered, whatever they were, is exhausted. That of those in training, those beyond training, and all śrāvakas ends at the limit of nirvāṇa. That of pratyekabuddhas is exhausted because they lack great compassion."

Furthermore, there are two vehicles: the vehicle of gods and the vehicle of humans.

Among these, loving-kindness that takes all sentient beings as its object belongs to bodhisattvas who have first generated the mind of awakening. That which takes phenomena as its object belongs to bodhisattvas engaged in the practice. Loving-kindness without an object belongs to those who have attained forbearance regarding the nonarising of phenomena.

Just as the seven kinds of precious things go before a wheel-turning king, exhalation and inhalation go before a person's faculty of life.

The bodhisattva's purified divine eye knows, throughout the infinite ten directions, the death and passing, the birth, and the sequence of actions and results from the beginning, of all sentient beings except those of the formless realm.

Giving is of the Dharma and of material things. Pleasant speech is gentle words. Purposeful conduct is fulfilling the wishes of others just as they intend. Consistency of aim is establishing others in that very vehicle which one oneself regards as excellent.

In brief, the teaching of the eighty-four thousand collections of Dharma is called "syllables." It teaches the meaning that cannot be expressed by the languages, letters, and syllables of any sentient being.

What abides in arising and cessation is consciousness. What abides in nonarising and noncessation is wisdom. Definitive and provisional are the teachings of the ultimate and of the conventional. Persons, from the ordinary person up to the buddha, are taught by way of convention, but clinging to them is not taught. The nature of reality is sameness.

There are twenty-one thousand who act out of attachment, and likewise those who act out of aversion, out of delusion, and out of all three in equal parts: in all, eighty-four thousand.

Four kinds of giving become the accumulation of wisdom: giving pens, paper, and the like to those who preach the Dharma; giving seats; giving gain, honor, and renown; and giving the words "Well spoken!"

Again, four kinds of patience become the accumulation of wisdom: patience with being reviled because of one's diligence in seeking the Dharma; the patience of being in accord with one's preceptor and teacher; and patience toward the three doors of liberation.

What is the meaning of impermanence? It is the meaning of nonexistence and the meaning of nonattachment.

In the Ratnākara: "According to the Vedas of the brahmins, one who possesses the thirty-two major marks has two destinies: a wheel-turning king or a buddha." "If the eyes are anointed with this yellow medicine, whatever forms one sees will all be seen as golden in color." "Even the incalculable hundreds of thousands of koṭis of niyutas of buddhas who have passed into parinirvāṇa were set upon awakening by this youthful Mañjuśrī."

In the Array of the Casket (Kāraṇḍavyūha), Avalokiteśvara gave a prophecy to Bali, the lord of the asuras. And it is said: "Not even the tathāgatas possess eloquence like that of Avalokiteśvara." "On those kings of mountains dwell those pratyekabuddhas, who discuss with one another many kinds of teaching, sūtras, verses in song, and the like." "A grave wrong arises when one who is unfit to perform the formal acts of the Saṅgha performs them."

In the Play of Mañjuśrī (Mañjuśrīvikrīḍita): "Wherever mind, mentation, and cognition are at work, even if it is thinking of nirvāṇa, all of that is affliction." "Color, the marks of form, and the manner of one's deportment are not a bodhisattva's going forth; generating loving-kindness and compassion toward other sentient beings and other persons is a bodhisattva's going forth."

In the Questions of King Vikurvāṇa: "If one relies on the nature of reality, all the sūtras are of definitive meaning. If one relies on the world, all are of provisional meaning." "The work of a bodhisattva is twofold: bringing sentient beings to maturity and upholding the true Dharma." For a bodhisattva who abides on the eighth ground and is advancing to the ninth, there are the ten powers of a bodhisattva, the four fearlessnesses, and the eighteen unshared qualities. These qualities also appear in this same sūtra. Among the unshared qualities: even when malicious beings do the bodhisattva harm, the bodhisattva is not crushed, and on account of that harm those beings do not go to the lower realms either, because of the force of the bodhisattva's aspiration of pure moral discipline.

In the Array of the Qualities of the Buddha Field of the Noble Mañjuśrī: "Going forth has ten qualities: one is not attached to sense pleasures; one delights in solitude; one attains the sphere of activity of the buddhas; one does not crave a wife and the like; one gives up the things that lead to the lower realms; one relies on the things that lead to the higher realms; one never falls away from one's former roots of virtue; one attains excellent roots of virtue; the gods rejoice; and nonhumans give protection."

In the Aspirations of the Seven Buddhas: "A sentient being's consciousness is led away by the men of Yama, the lord of death, and brought before Yama, while the god born together with that person follows behind. He writes down carefully everything good and bad that this being has done and presents it to Yama. Yama questions and examines, and then commands according to what was done." "If, as soon as a person has died, one makes offerings to the Buddha Bhaiṣajyaguru Vaiḍūryaprabha, then after seven days, or twenty-one, or thirty-five, or forty-nine, that person regains awareness and from then on does no more evil."

In the Array of the Qualities of the Buddha Field of the Noble Mañjuśrī: "Suppose householder bodhisattvas as numerous as the sands of the river Ganges were to honor the Tathāgata, together with his retinue, the Saṅgha of monks, with every comfort for eons as numerous as the sands of the river Ganges. Far greater in merit than that is a bodhisattva who has gone forth, longing for complete solitude, turning toward the wilderness and taking seven steps; and that bodhisattva comes nearer to buddhahood as well." "The factors of awakening abide in conscientiousness. Awakening abides in diligence. A buddha field is accomplished through aspiration." "If uttering the name of the noble Mañjuśrī is far greater in merit than uttering the names of hundreds of thousands of koṭis of niyutas of buddhas, what need is there even to mention uttering the name of the Tathāgata Samantadarśin?" When a certain bodhisattva asked the noble Mañjuśrī, "When will you attain buddhahood?" he answered with examples of never attaining buddhahood: "When the moon becomes day, when day becomes night, and when an arhat whose defilements are exhausted attains complete and perfect buddhahood, then I will attain buddhahood."

In the Array of the Tathāgata Akṣobhya: "Twenty intermediate eons make one eon. Ten of those eons make one great eon." "Suppose a person of the bodhisattva vehicle who has gone forth from home cannot obtain this sūtra to keep, to read, and so forth, and someone says to him, 'If you dare to live in a household among a retinue of women, I will give you this sūtra.' If he would thereby obtain the sūtra, then even if he lives in a household among a retinue of women, there is no transgression." "In that buddha field of Akṣobhya one becomes an arhat in a single life. The fruits called once-returner and non-returner exist there in name only, for no one is born there into those classes of beings."

In the Teaching on the Inconceivability of the Tathāgata: "That is to say, karma is inconceivable, nāgas are inconceivable, meditators are inconceivable, and buddhas are inconceivable." "He wrapped even his own body in cotton cloth, anointed it with sesame oil, and offered it as a lamp. At that time this Blessed One Śākyamuni was Śakra (Indra), lord of the gods, called Sunetra." "A king who possesses four qualities is called a Dharma king: he does not give up the awakening mind; he establishes others too in awakening; he dedicates all his roots of virtue to awakening; and, apart from aspiring to the greatness of the buddhas when he sees or hears of it, he does not aspire to any excellence whatever, whether of the gods and the like or of the śrāvakas and the like."

"Maitreya, Lion, Banner, Flower, Lotus, Flower, Star King, Good Conduct, Fine Arm, Light, Delighting in the Stars, Manifestly Exalted, Knowing, Possessor of Supreme Qualities, Glory of Wealth, Source of Wisdom, Source of Jewels, Universal Illumination, Renown of Infinite Qualities, and Roca." "The lifespan and the śrāvakas of the Tathāgata Roca equal those of all the nine hundred and ninety-nine buddhas of the Good Eon put together. All the deeds of that many are performed by the Buddha Roca alone." "The Brahmā Śikhin, the god who serves awakening, called Power of Splendor, always follows at the Tathāgata's side and holds the Tathāgata's food in a bowl." "The life of the gods of the formless realm is eighty-four thousand eons." "To those sentient beings who are attached to dwelling in the wilderness, one should give talks on making the mind secluded as a bodhisattva does."

Vajrapāṇi said: "Śāntamati, did I not explain earlier that the Tathāgata has not uttered even a single syllable? That is so; yet through the power of his former aspirations the Tathāgata speaks, and sentient beings perceive it." "Four summaries of the Dharma are set forth. To overcome the śramaṇas and brahmins who proclaim permanence, and the long-lived gods who perceive things as permanent, it is said, 'All formations are impermanent.' To overcome the gods and humans who perceive things as pleasant, it is said, 'All formations are suffering.' To overcome the non-Buddhists (tīrthikas) who proclaim a self, it is said, 'All phenomena are without self.' To overcome those with the conceit of attainment who engage in conceptual thought, it is said, 'Nirvāṇa is peace.'"

In the Teaching on Dreams: "On the ninth and tenth grounds there is no seeing of false dreams." "One should read the Three Heaps (Triskandhaka) in order to purify karmic obscurations." "A bodhisattva who in a dream obtains all three of the Tathāgata's Dharma robes should be regarded as being on the eighth ground."

In the Collection of Flowers: "In the north, in the land called Gandhāra, there are those who will occupy the hell of Avīci for a hundred thousand years: fifty thousand from the land of Kashmir; eight koṭis and five hundred thousand from the north; many hundreds of thousands from the east; two hundred thousand in the south; and a hundred thousand from the west." "Any son of good family who has no faith in the root of virtue of hearing the name of the Tathāgata Amitābha, does not believe it, and says aloud, 'How could that be so?' will live for sixty thousand years in the great hell called Raurava, the Howling."

"In whatever region the teaching has blazed forth, there the foolish people who reject the true Dharma have become very many." "A human body is hard to obtain": this is taught with the example of the turtle and the yoke.

In the Great Lion's Roar of the Noble Maitreya: "Until one has attained the fruit, one should not accept even so much as a vessel of water given in faith." "The renunciant of the śrāvaka vehicle who is given to evil has four wishes: wishing to see the future buddha, wishing for the kingdom of a wheel-turning king, wishing for the royal caste, and wishing for the brahmin caste." "Rather than giving every requisite to all the sentient beings of the four continents for more than an eon, it is of greater merit when one who has rightly entered the path, whose moral discipline is completely pure and whose livelihood is pure, accepts from any donor even so much as a vessel of water given in faith." "If a renunciant has these subtle afflictions, he goes to hell like a shot arrow: jealousy at the gains of others; disparaging moral discipline on hearing the sūtras connected with the vows; out of pride not confessing when he has transgressed the training laid down by the Tathāgata's command; and knowingly enjoying what patrons have given in faith." "If a renunciant has these four kinds of intoxication, he goes to hell. The four are: being intoxicated with great learning and so becoming heedless; being intoxicated with gain and honor; being intoxicated with friends, acquaintances, and households that give alms; and being intoxicated with the qualities of ascetic practice, so that he praises himself and disparages others." "One who has four qualities claims to be a bodhisattva: he wants gain, he wants honor, he is deceitful, and he lives by a base livelihood." "In the last five hundred years there will appear those who claim to be bodhisattvas while wholly engaged in the ways of dogs." "A monk who wishes to visit householders should send them no gift other than the gift of the Dharma. Having gone to a house, apart from the Dharma he should not engage in worldly talk." "I taught the Dharma discourse of the Three Heaps (Triskandhaka) in order to exhaust karma done in the past." "Some who pose as bodhisattvas, thinking that evil they have done is got rid of by confessing it, do not restrain themselves from then on. Even if it happens up to a hundred times in a single day, renouncing it is better; but to enjoy what is given in faith while one's moral discipline is impure is not like that." "If you had not yet brought about the liberation of the mind of even a single śrāvaka, then, after being made to exercise the kingship of a wheel-turning king as many times as there are grains of sand in the river Ganges, you would still attain complete and perfect buddhahood in unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening." "Even a householder can attain the fruit of the non-returner."

In the Questions of the Merchant Bhadrapāla: "Merchant, not even the comforts and requisites of Śakra, lord of the gods, can match in any way those of that Moon of Prosperity." "For example, just as with an arhat who has entered the absorption of cessation, the element of consciousness passes beyond cessation and goes on completely, so too this element of consciousness, when the one who is born dies, completely leaves the body and casts off the elements." "There is internal form and external form. Of internal form, that which is the appropriated eye is external form. That which is eye consciousness is form." "Through possessing the inner eye's knowing, one sees forms in a dream, not with the eye of flesh."

In the Irreversible Wheel: "Rather than these two accomplishing roots of virtue for ten thousand years, whoever does so there for a single morning: that one, I truly declare, has far greater merit."

In the Cloud of Jewels: "The eloquence of bodhisattvas is never devoid of meaning. Why is that? Because they have attained the nature of reality. Son of good family, those who have not attained the nature of reality become separated from the meaning, but not those who have attained the nature of reality. The eloquence of bodhisattvas is never devoid of words. Why is that? Because they are fearless with regard to all treatises. Son of good family, those who know only some part of the treatises become devoid of words, but they do not become bereft of all the treatises." "Bodhisattvas are not born among the long-lived gods, where, by dwelling there, the many buddhas who are supreme persons would be without fruit for them and the welfare of sentient beings would decline. Bodhisattvas are born in the desire realm itself, where there are sentient beings to be trained and buddhas who have appeared, to be pleased. Why is that? Because they are skilled in means. Son of good family, this is the Vinaya of the bodhisattvas."

In the Questions of Brahmā Viśeṣacinti: "Rather than someone dwelling in pure conduct in this buddha field for a hundred thousand eons, whoever dwells for a single morning without aversion in the Sahā world system has by far the greater merit." "A bodhisattva who has four qualities, while dwelling in meditative concentration, is born in the desire realm. What are the four? A workable mind, generating the strength of the roots of virtue, never abandoning sentient beings, and cultivating skillful means and wisdom." "Those who say that cessation through the destruction of things is the noble truth have entered a wrong path. I am not their teacher, and they are not my śrāvakas."

"If all sentient beings
were to wish for the supreme vehicle,
there would be room for them all.
Consider the distinctions of the vehicles."

"Things are void of the intrinsic nature of the eye. Not cultivating any phenomenon at all: this is cultivating the path." "When the eon is consumed by fire, the springs, ponds, lakes and the like dry up first, and later the great ocean dries up. Just so, when the true Dharma declines, the Dharma of beings engaged in partial practice declines first, and later the true Dharma declines from the hands of those holy persons with oceanlike minds who rightly uphold the vehicle of the buddhas."

It is praised in these words:

"Just as, from what comes forth from the ocean,
a jewel gathers jewels to itself."

"Even when the eon of burning comes, it does not burn;
when the world is destroyed, it too goes elsewhere.
Likewise, when the stainless Dharma is being destroyed,
those with supreme diligence strive to uphold it.
Knowing the world is no vessel and longs for what is not Dharma,
they go before the victors of other realms.
It is not from a single teaching that phenomena do not arise
that one becomes a victor all at once.
It is attained through the stages of reasoning and the stages of practice;
for countless koṭis of niyutas of eons
it is attained by donning the armor, and not otherwise."

"Son of good family, one who wishes to guard himself should not examine the conduct of anyone else, and should not judge, 'This one is like this, that one is like that.'"

"Better a householder attached to the five sense pleasures
who, having become human, hears this Dharma,
than one gone forth in this teaching
who prides himself on ascetic virtues with views that grasp at objects."

In the Questions of the Noble Gaganagañja: "The root of virtue is the first generation of the mind of awakening; it is the root of all the qualities of a buddha. Merit is that, as soon as that mind is generated, it outshines the merit of sentient beings and of the Lesser Vehicle. Wisdom is dedicating those roots of virtue to the unsurpassed. With the wisdom of discernment one completely casts away all the afflictions of all sentient beings; with the wisdom of skillful means one never abandons any sentient being. Because one's speech is like space, when reviled one does not revile in return. Because one's body is like space, when struck one does not strike back. Because one's mind is like space, when abused one does not abuse in return. Because one's intention is like space, when others are angry one does not become angry in return." "The root of virtue is finding a spiritual friend. Merit is never abandoning the spiritual friend. Wisdom is asking the spiritual friend about everything." "Learning is the cause of great discernment, because the intrinsic nature of the mind is luminous." "The wheel-turning king, adorned with merit, has eighty-four thousand queens and eighty-four thousand sons." "The root of virtue is confessing one's evil deeds one by one. Merit is rejoicing in all roots of virtue. Wisdom is supplicating all the buddhas." "Those who are satisfied in discernment are fully satisfied." "If the awakening mind is held together by two qualities, it abides as irreversible. What are the two? Intention and superior intention. Intention is held together by being without guile and without deceit. Superior intention is held together by attachment to it and by advancing to ever greater distinction." "The objects of the mind are the domain of Māra."

In the Questions of the Nāga King Sāgara: "One who has four qualities gives up the unfavorable states: proclaiming the praises of the Jewels, not distracting those who long for the Dharma, not giving rise to regret in oneself, and dispelling the regret of others." "One who has two qualities removes nonvirtuous phenomena and brings nonvirtuous phenomena to cessation. The two are possessing the dedication of the Three Heaps and dwelling in conscientiousness." "Again there are two: becoming virtuous by nature, and not hoping for the karmic maturation." "One who has eight qualities perfects the qualities of going forth. What are they? Being content with the lineages of the noble ones; the qualities of ascetic practice and keeping few requisites; great learning; the mind delighting with certainty in the mind; abiding with mindfulness in meditative equipoise, without confusion about the intention of awakening; striving in the cultivation of meditative absorption and wisdom; and making the guarding of all one's undertakings the heart of the matter." "Whoever, coming into an assembly, intersperses talk of the profound Dharma while discussing various topics is striving so that the true Dharma will not decline. Why is that? For as long as the profound Dharma is renowned in this Jambudvīpa, the Dharma will not decline." "This buddha field consists of a hundred koṭis of four-continent world systems; and for as many four-continent worlds as there are, there are a hundred koṭis of such. There, people do not die untimely deaths." "Some went forth in the well-spoken Dharma and Vinaya but did not perfect moral discipline; they were wholly given to degenerate ritual conduct, degenerate livelihood, and degenerate moral discipline, yet their view became upright. Therefore they are not born among the hell beings but are born in the birthplaces of the nāgas." "One who does not envy a beautiful face will come to have a great body." "By the four garuḍas, kings of the birds."

In the Samādhi of the Four Boys: "With his head toward the east, his face toward the north, and his back turned toward the south, he lay down on his right side."

In the Questions of the Sage Vyāsa: "The one whose wealth it is, is the patron. One who gives because he has been made to do so is a giver but not the patron. One who gives his own things himself is both a giver and a patron." "Giving poison, weapons, and the meat of slaughtered creatures; giving liquor to the heedless; gathering sentient beings while thinking, 'These will become my servants and those who honor me'; and giving to win people over in order to gain something: this is impure giving. Giving gifts to musicians for the sake of amusement and the like is impure. Giving gifts to the Buddha, the Saṅgha, those who preach the Dharma, the sick, and one's parents is unsurpassed giving." "Whether one makes offerings to the Tathāgata while he is present or after he has passed into parinirvāṇa, the karmic maturation is the same." "When one who has done virtue is born in a mother's womb, for seven days he remembers, 'I died and passed from that place and was born here, because of such and such a deed.' It is the same for one who does nonvirtue." "When two gods of the Garland Bearers unite, no sexual fluid is emitted. The child stays in the womb for seven days and comes out from the right side." "The gods of the realm of the Four Great Kings do not die before their time." "The gods of the Heaven of the Thirty-Three have short beards and short hair."

In the Teaching of the Great Miracle: "Realization gathered into one is expressed in full as the Four Truths." "Because they follow in the place of awakening, they are called bodhisattvas. Because they master great undertakings and perfect great wisdom, they are called great beings."

"One who, with words embraced by the buddhas, overcomes the ill-speaking of all sentient beings: such a one is a preacher of the Dharma." "To be well-disposed toward all sentient beings and to do nothing unwholesome is to be without pride." "To abide oneself in moral discipline and to teach the Dharma in order to dispel the remorse and the malice of all sentient beings is to be an expounder of the Vinaya."

In the Bodhisattva Piṭaka: "Self, sentient being, person, and the rest are unreal." "That childish, unlearned people conceive of a self and the rest is unreal conceptualization."

"Do not be proud, thinking, 'I possess moral discipline.'
Do not despise others, thinking, 'This one's discipline is bad.'
Whoever despises others on account of mere discipline
Is, because of that pride, called one of corrupt discipline."

"The unimpeded wisdom of the Tathāgata is such that, if all the trees and other things in as many world systems as there are grains of sand in the Ganges River were piled together into a single heap and burned, and the ashes stirred into the great ocean, and a particle were then taken out of it, he would know, 'This is from that,' and would meet no impediment in knowing it."

"It is impossible for a stream-enterer to bring about an eighth existence. It is possible for one to pass into complete nirvāṇa in that very life."

"Although the robe does not touch the Tathāgata's body, staying some four finger-widths away from it, even the world-scattering wind cannot stir it."

"The loving-kindness of bodhisattvas who have first generated the mind of awakening takes sentient beings as its object. That of those who have entered upon the practice takes phenomena as its object. That of those who have attained forbearance is without object."

"A skillful bodhisattva makes even a small gift into much. By the power of primordial wisdom it is made superior; by wisdom it is made vast; by dedication it is made immeasurable."

"There are persons whose application is meager but whose aspiration is vast, and there are also those whose aspiration is meager but whose application is vast."

"Woman has many faults, is skilled in deceit, light of mind, changeable of mind, unstable of mind; her mind wavers greatly and is unsteady, a monkey's mind like a monkey's, and she is skilled at displaying illusions. Therefore she is called woman."

"A skillful bodhisattva generates four kinds of turning away: turning away from desire, from existence, from ingratitude, and from the suffering that belongs to formations."

"When a son of good family who has entered the Great Vehicle sees a woman of radiant complexion, he should generate four perceptions: he generates the perception of her as decay, the perception of her as a precipice, the perception of her as a cesspit, and the perception of her as pus. If, even after examining in this way, desire should still arise, he should generate three perceptions: toward one like his mother he generates the perception of a mother, toward one like his sister the perception of a sister, and toward one like his daughter the perception of a daughter."

"Śāriputra, there is nothing other than evil monks that obstructs and destroys the teaching of the Tathāgata."

"If a householder bodhisattva possesses five things, that one does not please the buddhas who appear, does not rely on a spiritual friend, does not find leisure and endowment, wastes the virtuous qualities already accomplished, does not train in the footsteps of bodhisattvas who abide in the vows, and does not attain awakening. The five are these: one is empowered by the king and comes to be feared by many people, and then, having agreed to undertake the matters that many people entrust, saying, 'Please do this for us,' does not do them; when monks, nuns, laymen, and laywomen are dwelling in a village, a district, a country, or the precincts of a palace, one causes them to break their moral discipline; one obstructs father, mother, sons, daughters, sisters, friends, relatives, or any other beings from practicing the Dharma; on hearing the profound teaching of the Tathāgata, one has no faith in it, speaks against it, and turns others too away from interest in it; and, relying on ministers or other powerful members of the retinue and arrogant with power, one reviles many people, mocks them, and mistreats them."

"A renunciant too, if possessing five things, will become likewise. The five are: practicing corrupt discipline; disparaging the true Dharma; attachment to gain, honor, and praise; the thicket of the view of self; and jealousy and stinginess toward the households of others."

"The accumulation of wisdom is four kinds of giving: giving to a preacher of the Dharma service, pens, ink, and books; giving various seats; giving all honor and praise; and giving, without guile, the words 'Excellent!'"

In the Samādhi of Direct Encounter with the Buddhas of the Present: "Perceiving sons and daughters as enemies, perceiving one's wife as a sister." "Because of such disrespect, the true Dharma will disappear." "Whoever wishes to uphold this samādhi should abandon all fickleness." "After my complete nirvāṇa, this samādhi will circulate in Jambudvīpa for four thousand years. Then, in the last hundred years, in the final time, when the last five-hundred-year period of the final age has come, one who perceives entities will not see the Buddha; one who perceives phenomena will not see him; giving done in hope of karmic ripening will not be perfect; through delight in meditation, moral discipline will not be pure; through stinginess with the Dharma, one will not become learned; through reliance on a person, one will not pass into complete nirvāṇa; through delight in idle talk, one will not see the truths; through delight in a dwelling place, one will not attain the fruit; through attachment, one will not see faults; through delight in malice, one will not reach certainty; and through hatred and resentment, one will not see benefits." "One should follow behind the teacher by not disobeying the teacher's word, by constant affection, by abandoning fickleness, and by not looking for faults."

In the Avadāna of the Power of Merit:
"Those who, when it is very hard to give,
Still give much even from little,
Though they die, they are not dead,
Like a traveler whose provisions are spent.
For one without merit, beneficial medicine
Turns into something greatly harmful.
Those who, proud of their might, prowess, and vigor,
Are ever drunk on the wine of heedlessness
And do not make merit,
When they die, they die to the very core."

Aṅgulimāla said: "Mañjuśrī, if some person who seeks the Dharma, who has been eating at two times of day, is taught by someone and becomes one who eats a single meal, that person will kill eighty kinds of worms."

"If it came to that, the killing of those creatures would not be free of fault." "At the time when the true Dharma is disappearing, things will turn away from the foundations of monkhood." "All sentient beings possess the tathāgatagarbha, unmade, permanent, everlasting, and adorned with the excellent major and minor marks. Just as, for example, water mixed with milk is not perceived apart from it, so too the form of the Buddha is covered by the afflictions, yet there is no way for the two to become mixed. Once the afflictions are gone, it shines like the sun and moon set free from the clouds." "Those beings who were formerly cattle and have not yet passed beyond that state, whose teeth still go on grinding, do not believe in the tathāgatagarbha. Likewise, all those who formerly kept committing evil deeds do not believe, and faults arise in every one of them," so it is said. "Those who have merit will have faith, and it is said that in all their births every good thing will be accomplished for them." "The monks of the land of Kashmir, not understanding the meaning of the practice, will disparage the whole of the Great Vehicle. Thinking, 'What is to be done about those who denigrate me?', I too feel shame." "In the future, those beings who say that the body of the Buddha was born from the union of father and mother, a human body with bones, sinews, marrow, and blood, will in the world become ugly and blemished, lame, withered on one side, feeble, and lacking limbs, and will be destined for the suffering of the lower realms." "For example, if fire is put inside a heap of straw, the middle of the heap burns while its edges do not burn. Just so, Mañjuśrī, in the land where I was first born, earnest diligence will perish, burned away, while a remnant will remain in the regions of the south; and there, where bodhisattvas dwelling in the borderlands are born, the perfect Buddha is renowned to appear." "These two, asserting that all is solely empty and the view of a self, destroy and overturn the true Dharma." "'The Middle Way' is a synonym for the Great Vehicle. Not knowing the Middle Way, sentient beings conceive of the Middle Way as something else." It is said that the mother of Aṅgulimāla too was an emanation, and Aṅgulimāla and Mañjuśrī are both explained to be buddhas.

In the noble Compendium of the Dharma (Dharmasaṃgīti): "The bodhisattva does not keep company with neglect in ten matters. The ten are these: with neglect of upholding the true Dharma, of hearing the true Dharma, of bringing sentient beings to maturity, of giving the true Dharma, of serving all teachers, of the deeds of Māra, of beings in turmoil, of the utter destruction of the true Dharma, of the perfections, and of the dedication of the Three Heaps, the bodhisattva does not keep company." "Made of Jambū River gold, not hammered by a smith." "Giving is giving food and the like. Great giving is giving away one's sons, wife, kingdom, and the like. Extreme giving is giving away one's head, hands, and the like." "Blessed One, the bodhisattva is set free from nirvāṇa, but not from saṃsāra. Why is that? Because the bodhisattva fears nirvāṇa, but not saṃsāra." "A bodhisattva need not train in a great many dharmas. Blessed One, if a bodhisattva holds to a single dharma and fully realizes it, all the qualities of a buddha are in the palm of the hand. What is that single dharma? It is great compassion."

In the Accomplishment of the Fours (Catuṣkanirhāra): "Focusing on omniscience, on the liberation of sentient beings, on keeping the lineage of the Three Jewels unbroken, and on taking up the array of qualities of a buddha field, one should make offerings to the Tathāgata." "These four are the superior generation of the mind of awakening. The four are generating the mind in order to gather sentient beings, to bring them to maturity, to accumulate roots of virtue, and to realize the Dharma." "The four echo-like generations of the mind are these: freedom from anger toward one who asks; compassion for those who have gone astray; not falling away from wisdom; and a mind that has mastered effort." "The four exalted generations of the mind are being exalted in moral discipline, in learning, in loving-kindness, and in compassion." "The four vajra-like generations of the mind are not being parted from intention, from affection, from earnestness, or from the Great Vehicle." "The four hard-to-find generations of the mind are being unmixed with the afflictions; with gain, honor, and praise; with the Lesser Vehicle; and with foolish people." "The four unsurpassed generations of the mind are these: giving away everything one holds dear, not regretting it after giving, not hoping for its karmic ripening, and making it a mind of dedication to awakening." "The four dharmas that have reached the summit are the perfection of wisdom, skill in means, upholding the true Dharma, and bringing sentient beings to maturity." "The four excellent and supremely sublime dharmas are these: freedom from anger toward sentient beings; generating the mind to bring to complete liberation the very ones who oppose one; remaining heedful even when endowed with vast possessions; and practicing the Dharma even when afflicted by poverty." "The four virtuous dharmas are these: applying oneself to all virtue; not despising the untrained; being a friend to sentient beings without being asked; and expecting nothing in return for the good one has done."

In the Questions of Brahmadatta: "A bodhisattva who possesses four things is a ruffian. What are they? Cawing like a crow and talking nonsense, out of tune with the minds of others; praising oneself and disparaging others; holding this life to be of the greatest importance; and dividing those who are in harmony." "Wherever there are persons who are jealous, lazy, fond of talk, given to idle chatter, improper in conduct, and lacking in application, there buddhas and bodhisattvas do not dwell." "These four are precipices for those who have gone forth in this teaching. What are they? Disrespect toward the Buddha, rejecting the Dharma, hostility toward preachers of the Dharma, and accepting, without diligence, what is given in faith."

In the Prophecy of Aśokadatta: "A bodhisattva should not be apprehended as a 'householder' or as a 'renunciant.'"

"Why? Because they are distinguished by their intention, their wisdom, and their primordial wisdom."

In the Questions of Pratibhānamati: "One who possesses five things will in all births obtain meeting with the Three Jewels, will hear the Dharma, and will obtain excellence just as wished. The five are these: having oneself gone for refuge to the Three Jewels, to establish others in it as well; to have images of the Tathāgata made in abundance; to hold one's preceptor and master with respect and in harmony; to regard all sentient beings as one's only child; and to practice without interruption by reciting the profound sūtras and so forth."

In the noble Purification of Karmic Obscurations: "A bodhisattva who delights in the five qualities of sense pleasure, who neither gives them up nor abandons them, and who sees even the qualities of a buddha as being of the very nature of desire, will thereby attain the purification of karmic obscurations."

In the Chapter of the Universal Gateway:
"Were my Dharma to remain
For inconceivable tens of millions of eons,
And were each and every day,
Just as on a single day,
All the sentient beings there are in a trichiliocosm
Brought to nirvāṇa,
Even if all were done in that way,
The realm of sentient beings would not be exhausted."

In Entering the Samādhi of Certain and Uncertain Destinies: "Suppose someone with a hostile mind were to destroy the five aggregates, moral discipline and the rest, of countless śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas, and another with a hostile mind were to destroy even a single precept of the moral discipline of a bodhisattva devoted to the Great Vehicle: the latter would produce incalculably more evil than the former. Compared with someone who threw all the sentient beings in the worlds of the ten directions into prison, someone who with a hostile mind is angry at a bodhisattva and remains thinking, 'May I never meet this one!' commits a far greater evil than the former. Compared with someone who tore out the eyes of all sentient beings in the ten directions, someone who looks at a bodhisattva in anger commits a far greater evil. If someone were to make all the pratyekabuddhas in all the worlds of the ten directions fall away from the state of a pratyekabuddha, and someone else were to make a single bodhisattva fall away from the state of a bodhisattva, the latter is by far the greater evil."

In the Perfection of Wisdom in One Hundred Thousand Lines it says: "The six perfections are both mundane and supramundane. All that have an objective reference are mundane, because they neither move away from the world nor rise above it." It says: "Those without objective reference are supramundane." And it says that both are to be called perfections.

In the Questions of the Girl Sumati: "Four things lead to becoming beautiful: not being angry even at an enemy, abiding in loving-kindness, longing for the Dharma, and having images of the Tathāgata made. One who possesses four things becomes rich: giving at the right time, giving without regret, giving what is pleasing, and giving without hoping for karmic ripening. One who possesses four things becomes unrivaled: relying without guile on spiritual friends, not being stingy with roots of virtue, rejoicing in the gains of others, and not being angry at any of the deeds and conduct of a bodhisattva. One who possesses four things will, at the time of death, have the Buddha present before them, will hear the Dharma, and will be without suffering: fulfilling the wishes of others, having great devotion, providing for bodhisattvas various arrays of gifts, and striving to make offerings to the Three Jewels."

In the Śrīgupta Sūtra: "This is growth in the Dharma and Vinaya: when one sees a fault as a fault, one confesses it, acknowledges it, and from then on restrains oneself."

In the Perfection of Wisdom in Five Hundred Lines: "Subhūti, if someone says, 'Whatever the Blessed One taught, that form is without intrinsic nature, unborn, at peace from the very beginning, and by its nature completely passed into nirvāṇa, none of that was meant with a hidden intention; it should be understood exactly as the words say,' that person falls behind even the non-Buddhists (tīrthikas), stands on the side of ordinary beings, and speaks wrongly." "Those who do not correctly see form as mere unreal conceptualization are those who apprehend form as form."

In the King of the Array of Qualities of All Dharmas: "One who possesses four things should dwell in the wilderness: abandoning life in a household, abandoning unwholesome companions, abandoning householders, and giving away all one's possessions." "Through two things one falls into hell: desire and aversion." "Through four things one goes to the higher realms: an even mind toward sentient beings, pure intention, giving away all one's possessions, and abiding in celibate conduct."

In the Questions of Sūrata: "For one who possesses four things, seeing the Tathāgata is meaningful. The four are trust, faith, cherishing, and respect." "A bodhisattva should wear such clothing that, wearing it, one abandons desire oneself, and others too do not give rise to thoughts of desire."

In the Teaching on the Miracle That Generates the Powers of the Buddha: "Those who, in order to make offerings to the Tathāgata, make a stūpa of clay, even in laughter or in jest, or while playing at children's games, will all put an end to suffering and attain awakening."

In the Vajramaṇḍa Dhāraṇī: "Those who view all dharmas as without entity neither arise nor cease. They see all dharmas as like the expanse of the sky, and they neither apprehend nor reject even the qualities of a buddha."

In the Questions of Ratnadatta, the Definitive Explanation of the Conduct of Bodhisattvas: "There are five purifications of a bodhisattva's gift. What are the five? Not apprehending the body, not clinging to the giving, not disparaging karmic ripening, and, toward the recipient, not hoping for karmic ripening." "A bodhisattva should always keep four things in mind. The four are the samādhi of emptiness, recollection of the Buddha, great compassion, and the karmic ripening of one's own actions."

In the Hastikakṣya:
"Than someone who filled with flowers
Buddha fields as many
As the grains of sand of the Ganges,
And offered them to the Protector of the World,
The unsurpassed, all-knowing one,
Whoever here, with palms joined,
Bows the mind toward awakening,
Has by far the greater merit,
And there is no measure to it.
There is no offering like it,
Nor anything like it even among vows.
One who wishes to attain the wisdom of a buddha
Should grasp no dharma whatsoever
And should reject no dharma whatsoever."

In the Questions of the Nāga King: "When one is adorned with calm abiding, all afflictions are vanquished. When one is adorned with insight, one comes to know things correctly, just as they are."

In the Illusion-like Samādhi:
"If, for the benefit of all sentient beings,
One generates the mind toward awakening,
It becomes an offering to the buddhas
Endowed with the thirty-two supreme marks."

In the Questions of Udayana, King of Vatsa:
"Just as a fly, on seeing a wound, rushes toward it,
Just as a pig, on seeing vomit, rushes toward it,
Just as a dog goes to the butcher for the sake of meat,
So do the unwise take delight in women.
Better to grasp a ball of blazing fire,
Better likewise to eat a blazing ball of iron,
Better to touch terrifying pits of fire,
Than ever to touch a woman with one's two hands."

"To fall under the power of a woman and indulge in sense pleasures, to despise one's father and mother, and to set out on the way to the lower realms: these are the two faults of a man who has been born."

"Having heard such words of the Great Sage,
Who became chief among humans and god of the gods,
Who would not care for a father and mother grown old, their faculties very weak,
Those supreme objects worthy of offering?"

"Those men who, being unwise,
Behave in the manner of camels and donkeys,
And, seeing a woman, rush toward her
Like pigs to a place of vomit,
Are like a multicolored vessel of filth:
Though it is seen to be full of color,
Within it lies hālahala poison,
Great and virulent.
So are adorned women, wherever they appear,
Like a skin bag filled
With urine and excrement and blown up with wind.
The buddhas have proclaimed women
To be as base as excrement.
Just as a pig, though for a moment
It may be startled and frightened,
Gives rise to craving
When it sees excrement,
So the deluded, having heard the Dharma,
Though they say, 'It is excellently spoken,'
Have minds that run after women,
As a cat runs after a mouse."

In the Compendium of Purity: "When the monk Dharma had died, because of having thus rejected the true Dharma, that monk was born in the hells for ten eons." "At the end of the time when the tathāgatas of this Good Eon appear, you will attain forbearance regarding the nonarising of phenomena. Having attained the four meditative concentrations, from then on, after three incalculable eons, you will fully awaken to unsurpassed awakening." "Foolish people speak thus: 'Awakening is attained by one single mode of practice.' Their conduct is not completely pure." "Māra seeks an opening against those who strive; what would Māra do to those who do not strive?"

In the Bodhisattva Prātimokṣa: "In bodhisattvas there is no guile, stinginess, jealousy, or slander, and no clinging mind that thinks, 'I cannot attain perfect, complete awakening.'" "Bodhisattvas make no gift that is untimely or unfitting, no gift of intoxicants, of poison, of implements for beating, of weapons, or of things that incite strife, no gift not given by their own hand, and no gift of sexual contact."

In the Questions of Akṣayamati: "The mind that goes along with all dharmas is the knowing of the awakening mind."

In the Chapter of the Heaven of the Thirty-Three: "Son of the gods, the three realms are nothing but mind, and that mind too is like an illusion: even when it is thoroughly sought by way of the nature of mind, it is not found. The mind by which it is sought is not found either. Since sentient beings are not apprehended, no dharma is apprehended."

"The more a bodhisattva skilled in means suffers and is unhappy, the more that bodhisattva strives to pacify the suffering of others." "One who possesses four things engages in the profound dharmas and yet does not actualize the limit of reality. The four are abiding in the essential through steadfastness in one's commitments, undaunted diligence, great compassion, and skill in means."

"Maudgalyāyana, if you think that I attained perfect, complete awakening only in this world of four great continents and not in other continents, do not view it so. Why? In every world of four great continents I teach the Dharma according to the inclinations of sentient beings. In some I attain perfect, complete awakening; in some I dwell in my mother's womb; in some I abide in the Tuṣita heaven; in some I pass into complete nirvāṇa: this I know fully, just as it is. Thus, in that place there is no classification of the four fruits of the ascetic life such as the one called stream-entry; rather, all of them, seated upon a single seat, actualize them through certain higher knowledges, attain the eight liberations, and, having attained great wisdom, rise into the sky, burn their own bodies with fire, and pass into complete nirvāṇa." "Maudgalyāyana, a bodhisattva who possesses four things will for a time take birth in the five destinies. The four are these: making a living from gain under the name of a bodhisattva while not training in the precepts of a bodhisattva; remaining indifferent to the rejection of the Dharma and not turning it back; obstructing the gain of others and speaking in dispraise of them; and not guarding the actions of body, speech, and mind."

In the Teaching on the Three Vows: "Those in whose minds there is remorse do not commit the deeds of immediate retribution or the ten unwholesome actions. They do not take a drunken person by the hand or quarrel with one." "One who has entered into a mind of loving-kindness gives up twenty situations. One does not teach the Dharma to women when they number fewer than twenty. One does not answer a question asked by a nun. One does not write letters to women. One does not go when invited as a guest by a neighbor. One should not invite in return a nun who has invited one. One does not wear a robe given by a nun. If a widow invites one, one does not accept without the Saṅgha. If a nun calls to one, one should cross one's arms, lower one's head, and slip away from that place. If a nun comes to a place where the Dharma is being taught, one should sit with one's face covered by one's hands."

"Two things should always be taught. What are they? One should thoroughly seek the nature of reality, which is the absence of entity in all dharmas, and yet one should not even generate a mind that thoroughly seeks. How is one to seek? In such a way that, however thoroughly one seeks, those two are not apprehended. Not apprehending, one should not even generate the thought of non-apprehension, for that is a wrong view, and so it is a mind of the three realms."

"It is better for a person to go without food for six days than, having gone forth in this teaching, to consume what is given in faith while holding views, from the view of a self and of a sentient being up to the view of nirvāṇa."

"In the future there will appear monks who are greedy, slanderous, coarse, harsh, and scowling, and who abide in three things: medical practice, trade, and women. For example, if bile is smeared on the nose of an angry dog, it grows angrier still. Likewise, in the future there will appear unholy persons like dogs and foxes who, having obtained the profound scriptures of the Tathāgata and the vows, grow all the angrier."

"Sentient beings who possess four things feel no disenchantment, whatever scripture they hear. The four are heedlessness, disbelief in karmic ripening, disbelief in the great hells, and disbelief that they themselves will die. Further, through four things one does not become disenchanted. The four are having youthful elements without impairment, being endowed with might and strength, being attached to the qualities of sense pleasure, and never having experienced yoga while being in the grip of longing."

"The one thing that makes an ascetic or a brahmin is the mind not abiding in any dharma." "What is called one-pointedness of mind is not apprehending even a single dharma when one searches thoroughly." "A nun's wisdom is two fingers wide." "In the future, people will say to one another, 'Empty, empty,' and by saying it will speak the words, 'I have fully realized, I have fully realized.' At that time, a monk who realizes things correctly as they are and is endowed with a training free of contradiction will be rare, even one within a saṅgha of two thousand monks."

"After this eon has passed,
For a full sixty eons
No buddha will appear;
How then will this joy arise?"

"A householder bodhisattva should not do three things: at another's offering of gifts, one should not give even the essence of butter when one has not been invited or there is no occasion for it; one should not obstruct anyone's going forth, but should encourage it; and if one is appointed overseer of the work on a stūpa, one should not, relying on that position, use its resources as one's own provisions. Again, three things should not be done: selling men, selling women, and giving what is not medicine. Again, three things should not be done: going to the abode of prostitutes, associating with men and women whose speech and mind are at odds, and going to places where cattle are slaughtered."

In the Pair Sūtra: "Then the brahmins and the other non-Buddhist practitioners, the wandering ascetics, the naked ascetics, the kings, and wicked Māra with his retinue all came, and in order to deceive, they said this to the Blessed One: 'Blessed One, we have come here into the presence of the Tathāgata. Having heard this Dharma discourse, may we attain such bliss of a buddha! May we attain such a state as that of a tathāgata, an arhat, a perfectly complete buddha in the world!' The Blessed One declared: 'Good sirs, so it is. Just as you have come into the presence of the Tathāgata and have heard this Dharma discourse of the Pair Sūtra, you have generated the mind toward unsurpassed awakening. Therefore all of you, before long, will fully awaken to unsurpassed, perfect, complete awakening.' As soon as the Blessed One had spoken these words, all those non-Buddhists attained forbearance regarding the nonarising of phenomena. All of them became bodhisattvas abiding on the tenth ground, and they rose up into the sky above."

In the Five Perfections: "When a bodhisattva gives a gift, if it is dedicated thus, 'May this gift of mine ripen into nothing but omniscience,' it counts as a perfection. Even a gift that a bodhisattva gave in beginningless saṃsāra without dedicating it to omniscience counts as a perfection if, through skill in means, it is directed and dedicated to omniscience. A bodhisattva should train in the perfection that is applied from the very beginning." "'Can one who has fallen be restored?' It was said: 'Yes, provided one has not actualized the limit of reality and provided one has not seen the truths.'" "If a bodhisattva dwells as a householder for as many eons as there are grains of sand in the Ganges River, relying on and enjoying the qualities of sense pleasure, yet does not set the mind on becoming a śrāvaka or a pratyekabuddha, that alone does not make the bodhisattva one of corrupt discipline, because the superior mind has not been disturbed." "Although a bodhisattva has gained mastery over the meditative attainment of cessation, the bodhisattva does not rest in equipoise in it."

If perception and feeling were suspended, that would be regarded as actualizing the fruit of an arhat.

In The Meeting of the Father and the Son it says: "From the single utterance spoken by the Blessed One (the Buddha), people each understand differently, according to their dispositions."

Countless, immeasurable eons ago, a Tathāgata called Indraketu appeared in the world.

Not attending mentally to any phenomenon:
This too is a gateway of dhāraṇī;
The Sugata taught it as dhāraṇī.

Without asking the Victor of the ten powers,
The supreme heir does not foretell awakening.

The saying "The Tathāgata has comprehended the two, the conventional and the ultimate," is also contained in this. Moreover, because the Blessed One has thoroughly seen emptiness, thoroughly known it, and fully actualized it, he is therefore called the Omniscient One.

As the antidote to attachment he declared calm abiding;
As the antidote to aversion he taught loving-kindness;
As the antidote to delusion, insight
Was taught to sentient beings by the Supreme Sage.

The Tathāgata is unborn, and all phenomena too are unborn. Therefore all phenomena are the Tathāgata.

The signs of one who has passed from the hells are a loud voice, a booming voice, and distrust of all beings. The signs of one who has died and passed from the birthplace of animals are much sleep, great laziness, and eating a great deal; speaking unclearly; sleeping all curled up; saying one thing while something else comes about, and being deceitful; and acting recklessly. The signs of one who has died and passed from the world of Yama are tawny hair, constant hunger and thirst, great miserliness and craving, a mind bent on stealing every kind of thing, and great jealousy at the gains of others. One who has died and passed from the asuras is conceited, quarrelsome, holds grudges, is proud, has a large body, shows prominent fangs, delights in causing division, and treats everyone with contempt. One who has died and passed from the human realm relies on noble ones, aspires toward noble ones, has a sense of conscience and shame, is devoted to friends, is generous, and has the ability to learn every language. One who has passed from the gods is beautiful and very clean, delights in the sound of music, has a deeply loving disposition, speaks gently, is eloquent, and is skilled at bringing benefit.

The ceasing of the last consciousness is called death; the arising of the first consciousness is called birth.

In the time of King Māndhātṛ, all the towns of Jambudvīpa were so close together that a rooster could cross between them in a single flight.

In The Samādhi That Gathers All Merit it says: "All acts of merit are included within, and follow upon, the generation of the mind set on awakening."

If the Tathāgatas did not bless the world system in which the bodhisattva dwells to be of the nature of vajra, then when he took seven steps immediately after his birth, it would sink downward sixty-eight thousand leagues, and from below streams of water as thick as a chariot yoke would burst forth.

The strength that the Tathāgata has from his birth of father and mother is also greater than the series that begins from the strength of ten men and increases tenfold at each step, through bulls, gods, bodhisattvas and so on, until it reaches sixty; it is said that the last of these is that of a bodhisattva bound to one more birth.

The bodhisattva's collection of moral discipline is compassion toward those who break the trainings and intense respect toward those who keep the trainings.

If one possesses four things, one's giving becomes exhausted: not dedicating it, lacking skill in means, aiming at a lowly birth, and relying on evil friends. The opposite of these is inexhaustible.

A bodhisattva should not keep servants and attendants, wealth, or a kingdom of such a kind that, when a beggar comes, he cannot bring himself to give them away.

All phenomena are the door on every side, because they show the doors of liberation on every side.

For those who seek the Dharma of the bodhisattvas, treasures of Dharma have been placed within mountains, mountain ravines, and trees, and dhāraṇīs and infinite gateways of the Dharma made into volumes will also come into their hands.

If one possesses four things, one forgets the awakening mind: overweening pride, disrespect for the Dharma, contempt for the spiritual friend, and speaking false words. If one possesses four things, one forgets the awakening mind: associating with yogins of the Lesser Vehicle, inclining toward the Lesser Vehicle, denigrating bodhisattvas, and keeping the teacher's closed fist over the Dharma.

Through hearing one comes to know the teachings;
Through hearing one does not commit evil;
Through hearing one abandons harm;
Through hearing one attains nirvāṇa.

A bodhisattva does not commit evil even for the sake of his life. Even to nourish his body he does not make a living by wrong means. Even for the sake of his life he does not beg from others importunately. For the sake of possessions he does not form nonvirtuous volitions.

Again, by four things it is forgotten: deceit, showing guile toward sentient beings, being double-tongued toward the spiritual friend, and attachment to gain and honor.

The eighty-four thousand kinds of conduct are twenty-one thousand each of conduct in attachment, in aversion, in delusion, and in these in equal measure.

If a bodhisattva clings to any view, even if he clings to the view of the qualities of a buddha and of nirvāṇa, that too is deceiving the Tathāgata himself.

A bodhisattva should engage even with the three poisons, and should also be free of them.

An even mind toward all sentient beings, and taking up existence in the five destinies, is loving-kindness. The thought, "I will free all sentient beings from suffering, I will free them from the path of saṃsāra," is compassion.

In The Questions of the God's Son Suvikrāntacinta it says: "When bodhisattvas understand thorough discipline itself, are free of transgressions and afflictions, also teach the Dharma in order to discipline transgressions and afflictions, thus understand the afflictions, and also seek awakening, to that extent they are holders of the Vinaya."

Mañjuśrī said: "Son of the gods, all sentient beings become buddhas through the absence of enumeration, not through enumeration."

"Son of the gods, since whatever is emptiness is itself awakening, the Blessed One also said, 'The realization of emptiness alone is awakening.'"

If one is equal to the noble ones in knowledge, yet through skillful means abides as one who is not a noble one, that bodhisattva is an arhat. One who proclaims the teaching without depending on others and is not conceited about what one has heard: that bodhisattva is a śrāvaka.

In The Teaching on the Conduct of Bodhisattvas, Mañjuśrī said: "Here, how should a beginner bodhisattva be given instruction? How should such a one be advised?" Ratnadatta said: "One should speak thus: 'Do not abandon attachment! Do not crush aversion! Do not dispel delusion! Do not move upward out of your own body! Practice what is nonvirtuous! Do not cut off views! Do not teach non-engagement! Seize the aggregates! Make the elements into one whole! Engage with the sense sources! Understand what is nonvirtuous! Abandon virtue! Do not think about the Dharma! Do not make offerings to the Saṅgha!' That is how one should teach."

In The Transformation of a Woman, the venerable Subhūti said this to the Blessed One: "Three nights ago, in my dream."

The knowing of others' minds by śrāvakas, pratyekabuddhas, bodhisattvas and sages is the power of the Tathāgata, just as, for example, those who have eyes see forms by means of the condition of the sun and so on.

"It is right to put out a fire that is blazing, but there is no need to do so with a fire that is not blazing. In the same way, it is right to ask forbearance of ordinary people, but not of noble ones."

"Is the very name 'Omniscient One' also imputed?" "It is so, because in other buddha fields there are different names."

"Had I not come to think as I do, I too would surely have generated the mind set on unsurpassed, perfectly complete awakening."

An unlearned person who wishes for awakening: the Śrāvaka Vehicle is not like that. A woman endowed with the five qualities of sense pleasure who is not parted from the mind of omniscience: an arhat exalted in the Dharma, a meditator in the eight liberations who abides in the truths, is not like that.

In this Dharma, the ten nonvirtues are presented as the conduct of a bodhisattva that applies itself to virtue. In meaning, giving up one's own life and the lives of others for the sake of awakening is killing. Taking up awakening as something known by oneself is taking what is not given. Knowing these desires to be wrong is sexual misconduct. Since every sound is false, it is lying. Separating those inclined toward the Lesser Vehicle from that vehicle and setting them in the supreme one, and separating non-Buddhists by divisive speech and placing them on the correct path, is divisive speech. Refuting opponents is harsh speech. Reconciling divisions is idle talk. Wishing to set all sentient beings in happiness is covetousness. When the Dharma is being destroyed, bringing people into the Dharma with great force is malice. Because one brings those with wrong views to what is correct, it is called wrong view.

In The Questions of the Householder Ugra it says: "A householder bodhisattva who possesses four things has gone for refuge to the Buddha: not forsaking the awakening mind, not breaking his commitment, not giving up great compassion, and not focusing on other vehicles. One who possesses four things has gone for refuge to the Dharma: listening to the Dharma with respect for those who teach it, properly reflecting on what one has heard, teaching it to others, and dedicating to great awakening the roots of virtue that come from the gift of Dharma. One who possesses four things has gone for refuge to the Saṅgha: bringing those of the Śrāvaka Vehicle to the mind of omniscience; leading those who gather others with material goods to gather them with the Dharma; relying on the Saṅgha of irreversible bodhisattvas and not on the Saṅgha of śrāvakas; and not aspiring to the liberation of the śrāvakas."

"If one thinks, 'I will give these people whatever they want; having given them even liquor, I will afterward have them take up conduct free of confusion, and vigilance,' then even though a householder bodhisattva gives liquor to others, the Tathāgata has not declared it a fault, because fulfilling every wish is the purification of the perfection of generosity."

"A householder bodhisattva should generate three perceptions toward his wife: that she is a companion in joy and affection, not a companion for the world beyond; that she is a companion in food and drink, not a companion in karmic maturation; and that she is a companion in happiness, not a companion in suffering."

"One should recite the Dharma discourse of the Three Heaps (Triskandhaka)."

"Then the Blessed One said this to the bodhisattvas Maitreya and Viśuddhacāritra: 'You two: these householders go to what is called the Supreme Puṇḍarīka Flower.'" It is stated that, having experienced suffering, they attain nirvāṇa in this very life.

In The Noble Ratnolkā it says: "The categories of bodhisattva are these ten: one who has first generated the mind, a beginner, a practitioner of yoga, one born into the family, one perfect in application, one perfect in intention, one who is irreversible, one who has become a youth, a regent, and one who has received consecration." "Those sentient beings who abandon these teachings will mostly appear as those who have gone forth."

Resting in equipoise in the body of those in training and those beyond training,
They make the body of a pratyekabuddha appear;
Resting in equipoise in the body of a pratyekabuddha,
They make the supreme body of a buddha fully appear.

In The Instruction on the Great Vehicle it says: "A teacher of the Dharma is not a piśāca." "The eighteen unshared qualities have come forth as peace." Among the thirty-two things that obstruct the Great Vehicle are not seeking learning, lack of investigation, and the teacher's closed fist. By taking care of teachers of the Dharma, one upholds the true Dharma. By upholding the true Dharma, one takes care of all sentient beings.

In The Teaching on the Non-Arising of All Phenomena it says:

Whoever sees that, where discipline is lacking,
There is in fact no discipline,
And knows that broken discipline and discipline have one characteristic,
Knowing it thus, becomes a protector.

Those who are given to anger toward one another
Yet pray to be born in the field of Amitāyus,
As many times as there are grains of sand in the Ganges River,
May well come to a place of beatings;
But without fully cultivating supreme patience,
They will not go to the fields of those Victors.

Whoever knows fields to be merely the name "field,"
Knows all fields to have the nature of space,
And has no conceit about the qualities of a field,
That one goes to the field of the Bull among Men.

In The Noble Array of Amitābha it says: "Because that Tathāgata's lifespan is immeasurable eons, he is in general also called Amitāyus. Ten eons have passed since he attained perfect awakening. Āryapalo (Avalokiteśvara) and Mahāsthāmaprāpta too died and passed from this world system and were born there. Even now, seventy-two koṭis of niyutas of bodhisattvas from here will be born there." It is said that when the Buddha Amitābha was formerly the monk Dharmākara, he made the aspiration for a completely pure field. "For example, the great ocean, which is eighty-four thousand leagues deep." "Those in whom faith has arisen on hearing the name of Amitābha, but who still have doubts about the Tathāgata's wisdom and his vast array, are born there dwelling in the womb of a lotus; for five hundred years they do not see the Buddha and do not hear the Dharma. Those without doubt are born from a lotus seated with legs crossed."

In The Sūtra of the Ornament of the Seat of Awakening it is stated that tally sticks are distributed to those invited to an offering feast.

In The Noble Upholding of the Roots of Virtue it says: "The Blessed One offered half of his seat to the noble Kāśyapa."

"Thinking, 'I am content and have few desires,
I dwell in complete solitude,
And I wear a poor Dharma robe,' and being proud of it:
Kāśyapa, such people are non-Buddhists (tīrthikas)."

In The Verses of the Compendium of Trainings it says:

Be patient, and seek learning;
After that, go to the forest.

From The Ratnolkā:

Those who honor the buddhas by keeping the precepts
Do not recollect the buddhas;
Those who do not recollect the buddhas
Always see inconceivable buddhas.
Those who always see inconceivable buddhas,
For them the Buddha is never not present;
Those for whom the Buddha is never not present
Do not fall away from the Dharma in the meantime.

In The Application of Mindfulness of the True Dharma it says: "If one does not give even a little of what one intended to give, one is born as a preta. If one does not give what one promised, one goes to hell as a hell being."

In The Bodhisattva Prātimokṣa it says: "What is given is praised by the buddhas. What is hoarded is praised by the childish."

In The Questions of Nārāyaṇa it says: "One should not keep any thing toward which no thought of giving it away arises and to which the mind does not incline in that way. One should not hold on to any possession toward which no thought of letting it go arises."

In The Questions of Ugra it says: "Suppose a bodhisattva, for the sake of receiving the transmission and of recitation, has listened to, received the transmission of, or learned from someone a four-line verse connected with generosity, discipline, patience, diligence and meditative concentration, or connected with the accumulations of the bodhisattva path and so on. If, for as many eons as there are names, words and letters in the verse that teacher taught, he were to honor that teacher with every unfeigned gain and mark of respect, he would still not have repaid the teacher's kindness. What need is there to speak of respect that is not in keeping with the Dharma?"

In The White Lotus of the True Dharma it says:

Guard your proper conduct and your sphere of activity,
Stay free of bustle, and keep yourself pure.
Always avoid familiarity
With kings and with princes.
Whoever are the servants of kings,
Caṇḍālas, those of low birth,
And non-Buddhists:
Avoid familiarity with them in every way.
Monks who dwell in the Vinaya and the scriptures
Yet are puffed up, thinking themselves arhats,
And those who are arrogant: do not attend on them.
Nuns whose sphere is laughter and chatter
You should always avoid.
Laymen as well who are plainly unsteady
Should be avoided.

It occurs in The Account of Saṅgharakṣita: "When the turn fell to a certain novice who was an arhat to be assigned the duty."

From The Ākāśagarbha: "For kings, the four are: stealing what belongs to a stūpa, to the Saṅgha, or to the Saṅgha of the four directions; abandoning and obstructing the Dharma; beating, flogging, killing, binding and so on anyone at all who has gone forth, stealing their Dharma robes and making them live in a household; committing any one of the deeds of immediate retribution; and, being heedless of the world beyond, taking up the ten nonvirtues and causing others to take them up as well."

From The Kṣitigarbha: "Those who have gone forth for my sake, whether they possess discipline or have broken it, a universal monarch is not permitted to punish even in accordance with the Dharma. What need is there to speak of punishing them in a way that is not in accordance with the Dharma?"

In The Exhortation to Superior Intention it says: "Maitreya, those who have no diligence, and no meditative concentration, renunciation, recitation, or pursuit of great learning, have not gone forth in the teaching of the Tathāgata. Why is that? The teaching of the Tathāgata is distinguished by meditative concentration and the like, not by such things as doing service work and by the bounds of business. Maitreya, even if a bodhisattva devoted to service work were to fill a great trichiliocosm with stūpas of the seven precious substances, that would not please me, nor would it be service or honor done to me."

In The Application of Mindfulness of the True Dharma it says: "If one secretly sets things aside as leftovers, that is, gives to oneself so as not to eat leftovers, one is born as a preta."

In The Sūtra of Mañjuśrī's Play it says: "As for what is called 'anger, anger,' because it destroys the roots of virtue accumulated over a hundred eons, it is therefore called anger."

In The Akṣayamati it says: "Roots of virtue fully dedicated to awakening are not exhausted in the meantime, until one sits at the seat of awakening."

"Venerable Śāradvatīputra, it is like this. For example, a drop of water that falls into the great ocean is not exhausted in the meantime, until the end of the eon."

In The Questions of Upāli it says: "Upāli, suppose that a bodhisattva who has entered the Great Vehicle were to commit transgressions connected with attachment as many as the grains of sand in the Ganges River, and were to commit a single transgression connected with aversion. If one weighs them by the standard of the Bodhisattva Vehicle, then of those two, the one connected with aversion is by far the heavier. Why is that? Because aversion makes one abandon sentient beings, while attachment makes one gather sentient beings in. Upāli, in an affliction that makes one gather sentient beings in, the bodhisattva has nothing to shrink from and nothing to fear. Therefore, Upāli, I have explained to you that all transgressions connected with attachment are not transgressions. Upāli, those bodhisattvas who are unskilled in means are afraid of what is connected with attachment, not of what is connected with aversion. Those who are skilled in means are afraid of what is connected with aversion, not of what is connected with attachment."

In The Sūtra of the Treasury of the Tathāgata it says: "Distracting those who desire the Dharma is the most vicious of all idle speech."

In The Moon Lamp Sūtra it says:

However many discourses on the Dharma one has mastered,
If, drunk with learning, one does not guard one's discipline,
Learning will not be able to protect one;
Because of that broken discipline, one will go to the lower realms.

In The Noble Akṣayamati Sūtra it says: "Here, loving-kindness focused on sentient beings is this. First one makes habitual the intention to bring benefit and happiness to the beings who are dear to one's heart, and one cultivates loving-kindness in keeping with that. After that, one does so toward one's acquaintances. After that, toward those around one. After that, toward those who live in one's own village. After that, from other villages up to one whole direction, one directs one's resolve, pervades it, fills it completely, and so abides. It is the same for the ten directions."

In The Questions of Śrīdatta it says:

For example, a child takes a lump of charcoal
And rubs it, saying, "I will make this white";
Yet it does not become something white; it is only worn away.
Such is the childish mind, perverse and full of conceit.
Likewise, a person whose mind is set on cleanliness
Thinks, "I will make this body clean,"
And anoints it, wipes it, and bathes at a hundred bathing steps;
Yet it is worn away by the power of the Lord of Death, and never becomes clean.
