In The Questions of Ugra it is stated that even liquor may be given. Weapons and the like are covered by implication as well; whether giving them after due examination is a transgression is made clear by this very passage.

From The Ratnacūḍa: "What is the emptiness endowed with the supreme of all aspects? It is that in which generosity is not lacking, and so on up to that in which calm abiding and insight are not lacking, together with constant effort to bring sentient beings to maturity. This is the emptiness endowed with the supreme of all aspects." This is stated from there up to, "Son of noble family, this is the pure conduct of the bodhisattva's perfection of meditative concentration."

In The Noble Perfection of Wisdom it says: "Bodhisattvas do nothing at all without compassion."

From The Compendium of Trainings: "Here, the Three Heaps are called the confession of misdeeds, rejoicing in merit, and requesting the buddhas, because they are heaps of merit."

In The Descent into Laṅkā it says: "Mahāmati, how is it that the icchantikas do not desire liberation? It is through the abandonment of all roots of virtue, and through the aspiration for all sentient beings made from beginningless time. What is the abandonment of all roots of virtue? It is to reject and denigrate the Bodhisattva Piṭaka by saying, 'These are not sūtras and Vinaya that accord with liberation.' Because they have abandoned all roots of virtue, they do not pass into nirvāṇa. Mahāmati, the second is this: the bodhisattva great being makes the aspiration, 'As long as all sentient beings have not passed completely into nirvāṇa, I too will not pass into nirvāṇa,' and fully commits himself to the aspiration and the means to remain in existence; for that reason he does not pass into nirvāṇa. Mahāmati, that is the defining characteristic of those who do not pass completely into nirvāṇa; through it, they come to follow the way of the icchantikas."

Mahāmati asked again: "Blessed One, which of these is it that never passes into nirvāṇa?" The Blessed One said: "Mahāmati, it is the bodhisattva icchantika who, knowing that all phenomena have been in nirvāṇa from the very beginning, never passes into nirvāṇa; it is not the icchantika who abandons all roots of virtue. Mahāmati, even the icchantika who abandons all roots of virtue may, perchance, through the blessing of the Tathāgata, come to take up the roots of virtue fully again. Why is that? Mahāmati, it is because the Tathāgatas never abandon any sentient being. Mahāmati, for this reason, then, it is the bodhisattva icchantikas who do not pass completely into nirvāṇa."

"Mahāmati, words are neither different from conceptual thought nor the same as it. Why is that? Because the conceptual thought of words arises by way of the characteristic of arising from that as its cause. Mahāmati, if words were something different from conceptual thought, they would not arise from conceptual thought as their cause. And if they were one and the same, words would not make meaning clear; yet they do. Therefore they are neither different nor the same."

This cognition is mere name;
It does not exist by its own characteristics.

"Mahāmati, in some buddha fields the Dharma is taught by not blinking the eyes, and in some by moving the eyebrows and the like."

"Mahāmati, that confusion too, being conceived as inverted or as not inverted, brings about the attainment of two lineages, namely the lineage of the noble ones or the lineage of childish ordinary beings. Mahāmati, the lineage of the noble ones is in turn divided into three kinds: the śrāvakas, the pratyekabuddhas, and the buddhas."

"Here, Mahāmati, how is it that the childish, by imagining confusion, produce the lineage of the Śrāvaka Vehicle? It is like this, Mahāmati: through clinging to specific and general characteristics, that clinging becomes the lineage of the Śrāvaka Vehicle. In this way, Mahāmati, that confusion brings about the attainment of the lineage of the Śrāvaka Vehicle.

"Here, Mahāmati, how is it that imagining that very confusion brings about the attainment of the lineage of the Pratyekabuddha Vehicle? It is like this, Mahāmati: through that very confusion, by clinging to specific and general characteristics, and by keeping free of bustle, one is brought to attain the lineage of the Pratyekabuddha Vehicle.

"Here, Mahāmati, how is it that the wise, by imagining that very confusion, attain the lineage of the Buddha Vehicle? It is like this, Mahāmati: because they comprehend that it is merely the appearance of their own mind, when they imagine it without conceiving of external things as existent or nonexistent, they attain the lineage of the Buddha Vehicle. Therefore, Mahāmati, this is lineage, the meaning of lineage.

"Mahāmati, when the childish imagine, with regard to confusion as the nature of diverse things, 'This is just so and not otherwise,' they attain the lineage of the vehicle of saṃsāra.

"Mahāmati, nirvāṇa is of four kinds: the nirvāṇa that is the absence of an intrinsic nature of things; the nirvāṇa that is the absence of things with diverse characteristics; the nirvāṇa that is the conception of the absence of things with their own characteristics; and the nirvāṇa that is the severing of the continuum of the specific and general characteristics of the aggregates. These, Mahāmati, are the nirvāṇa of the non-Buddhists (tīrthikas); they are not my teaching. Mahāmati, in my teaching, the reversal of the mental consciousness that is accompanied by conceptual thought is called nirvāṇa."

The imputed has an intrinsic nature;
The dependent does not exist.
What confusion imputes is what appears;
The dependent is not conceived.

Conventional and ultimate:
A third, arising from causes, does not exist.
The imputed are said to be conventional;
Their cutting off is the domain of the noble ones.

"Mahāmati, wisdom is of three kinds: mundane, supramundane, and supreme supramundane. Here, what arises and perishes is consciousness; what neither arises nor perishes is wisdom. Furthermore, Mahāmati, that whose characteristic is to be bound up with arising, as the activity of the coming together of three, is consciousness; that whose nature is not bound up with it is wisdom.

"Mahāmati, a bodhisattva who seeks distinction should purify the buddha-nature that is proclaimed as the store consciousness. Here, name and sign should be known as the imputed nature. Mahāmati, the conceptual thought that rests and abides in that, proclaimed as mind and mental factors, arising all at once like the sun together with its rays, the nature by which diverse characteristics are understood as one's own conceptual thought: this is called the dependent. Mahāmati, correct wisdom and suchness, because they are imperishable, should be known as the consummate.

"Furthermore, Mahāmati, gold, vajra, the relics of the Victor, attainment and distinction are imperishable. Mahāmati, if realization, once attained, could perish, the noble ones would become ignoble; but the noble ones do not become ignoble. Mahāmati, gold and vajra, even when laid away, remain just as they are, neither diminishing nor increasing. How have the unskilled, with regard to what was spoken in words of hidden intent, imagined the meaning of the momentariness of all outer and inner phenomena!"

Mind is the store consciousness;
What thinks "I" is mentation;
That by which objects are apprehended
Is called consciousness.

Mind is always morally neutral;
Mentation is of both kinds;
And the consciousness that arises
Is virtuous and nonvirtuous.
The five conceive of what appears.

The five dharmas and the natures:
That is the domain of the buddhas.
The counterpart of existence is nonexistence,
And nonexistence too is the counterpart of existence.

The imputed and the dependent
Are not different from each other in characteristic;
Like the impermanence of form,
Each gives rise to the other.

If the imputed is seen as a mere trace,
The dependent does not arise;
If the dependent is clearly seen,
The imputed becomes suchness.

As the noble ones see in meditation,
Things have no thingness at all;
As the childish imagine them,
Things exist.

The mind free of appearance,
Having abandoned both natures too,
The transformation of the bases:
This I explain as non-arising.

Emptiness, being empty at all times,
Is the emptiness of non-arising.
For as long as the mind is active,
So long the world remains Lokāyata.
When the world sees its own mind,
Conceptual thought does not perish.

I and the Tathāgatas
Have explained nirvāṇa fully
In three thousand sūtras.

In the desire realm and the formless realm
The buddhas do not attain full awakening;
In Akaniṣṭha of the form realm,
Among those free of attachment, they awaken.

Through contact and through torment,
The bodies of gods and of hell beings
Have no intermediate existence;
They are produced by consciousness.
Those born from womb, egg, heat and moisture and so on,
For whom there is an intermediate existence,
Are like the many and various beings.

In The King of the Three Pledges it says: "I offer to those who dwell in the ten directions, who are without the making of 'mine' and without grasping, the mountains of jewels born on dry land, the mountains of jewels born in the water, the jewels born on dry land, and so on."

It occurs in The Ultimate Victory of the Dharma: "Some, while still dwelling in the womb, were eaten by worms."

"Great sage, moreover, it is like this: this burning by the eon is the skillful means of the tathāgatas. Great sage, it is like this, for example. Gold is not put into the fire in order to harm it; rather, one thinks, 'How may this unrefined gold become refined?' and for that reason it is put into the fire. Great sage, in just the same way the tathāgatas do not bring about an eon of burning without a cause, and here no sentient being whatsoever comes to harm. Seeing themselves burned amid the terror of the eon's burning, joy and supreme joy arise in them, and they make the aspiration, saying, 'May I too become like this.'"

"Please tell me, where do sentient beings come together?"

The Blessed One said, "Great sage, sentient beings come together through going forth by the one Great Vehicle. Sentient beings come together at the seat of awakening. Sentient beings come together in the sphere without any remainder of the aggregates. Great sage, moreover, it is like this, for example: the various rivers, and the streams both small and great, flow down into the great ocean and become of a single taste. In the same way, however many realms of sentient beings there are..."

From The Teaching of Vimalakīrti: "Wisdom not held by skillful means is bondage. Wisdom held by skillful means is liberation. Skillful means not held by wisdom is bondage. Skillful means held by wisdom is liberation.

"What, then, is wisdom not held by skillful means, which is bondage? It is this: to contemplate emptiness, signlessness and wishlessness with certainty, while not contemplating the major marks and the excellent minor marks, the adornment of a buddha field, and the bringing of sentient beings to maturity. That is wisdom not held by skillful means, and it is bondage.

"What, then, is wisdom held by skillful means, which is liberation? To contemplate the major marks and the rest with certainty, while familiarizing oneself with emptiness, signlessness and wishlessness: this is wisdom held by skillful means, and it is liberation.

"What, then, is skillful means not held by wisdom, which is bondage? It is this: while dwelling in all views, in the outbursts of the afflictions, in the latent tendencies, in attachment and in anger, to undertake every root of virtue and not dedicate it to awakening. That is skillful means not held by wisdom, and it is bondage.

"What, then, is skillful means held by wisdom, which is liberation? It is this: to undertake every root of virtue having abandoned all views, the afflictions and the rest, and to dedicate it fully to awakening, and yet not to be proud of that. That is skillful means held by wisdom, and it is liberation."

"Ānanda, there are also buddha fields where bodhisattvas do the work of a buddha. There are buddha fields where the work of a buddha is done by light, where by the tree of awakening, where by the marks of the Tathāgata, where by the Dharma, and where by food and the like."

From The Noble Peak of Jewels (Ratnakoṭi): "If one who has committed an act of immediate retribution has faith in this way in what he taught, and knows that the acts of immediate retribution too are of such a nature, he will not become thoroughly afflicted. Why is that? Mañjuśrī, because the acts of immediate retribution are alike and equal within the dharmadhātu, the sphere of reality. Thus the sphere of the acts of immediate retribution and the dharmadhātu are not two and cannot be made two."

From Rousing the Superior Intention: "Maitreya, if a person of the bodhisattva vehicle possesses four qualities, then in the last five hundred years, when the holy Dharma is being destroyed, he will remain unharmed and unhurt and will pass safely to complete liberation. What are the four? To recognize one's own mistakes; not to speak of the faults of other persons of the bodhisattva vehicle; not to look to the homes of friends or to the homes that give alms; and to give up unpleasant speech.

"There are four more: to keep away from beings of little learning; not to gather a following, and to rely on remote dwellings; to tame oneself, to be at peace, and to strive in the practice of calm abiding.

"Maitreya, I do not call a hypocrite a forest dweller. I do not call one of little merit a person of few desires. I do not call one who wants fine food an alms gatherer. I do not call one who wants soft garments a wearer of rags from the refuse heap. I do not call one who mingles with householders and renunciants unentangled. I do not call a deceitful person one who has met with the appearance of a buddha. I do not call one who looks for the mistakes of others one who strives in yoga. I do not call one with much anger pure in the aggregate of moral discipline. I do not call a proud person learned. I do not call a partisan a holder of the Vinaya. I do not call a frivolous person a preacher of the Dharma. I do not call one who mingles with householders pure in serving the community. I do not call one who looks to holy recipients of offerings one who gives everything away without thought of material gain. I do not call one who hopes for a favor in return a practitioner of the means of attraction. I do not call one attached to gain and honor pure in superior intention. I do not call one with many mothers and fathers a renunciant. I do not call one who instructs others one who wants the training. I do not call a disrespectful person a giver of the Dharma. I do not call one who is devoted to the secret words of the world one who delights in the Dharma. I do not call one who does not strive in emptiness one who has truly gone forth. I do not call one who has a conceptual reference one who has realized. I do not call a powerless person perfect in patience. I do not call one who has never been put to the test armored with the strength of patience. I do not call one who by nature has little desire one who dwells in the joy of pure conduct. I do not call one who does not abide in practice one who does as he says. I do not call one who possesses skillful means deceitful. I do not call a faint-hearted person free of pride."

From The Questions of Subāhu: "Subāhu, when a bodhisattva is begged by beggars, he does not cut off his limbs and lesser limbs and give them to the beggars. Why is that? Because those very beings would then become denizens of the great hells, and would experience great and vast unwholesome karma."

"Subāhu, when a bodhisattva great being would take all his possessions to beggars, he does not harm and abandon his father and mother, and he does not harm and abandon his children and wife, his male and female servants, his hired laborers and his workers, making them suffer, in order to give to beggars. Why is that? Because he has compassion equally for all sentient beings."

From The Questions of the Girl Candrottarā: "Unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening has no arising and no ceasing, no annihilation and no permanence, no single meaning and no different meanings, no coming and no going."

From The Analysis of Karma: "There are ten actions that make one ugly in complexion: obstructing the light of the lamps at a stūpa of the Tathāgata, disparaging persons who are ugly in complexion... There are ten factors that make one's family low: disparaging those born in low families... saying 'Excellent!' to preachers of the Dharma whose wisdom is corrupt... There are ten factors that make one become a hungry ghost: dying while hungry and thirsty, dying while attached to things...

"What is the action by which one is certain to be born there? It is to do a virtuous action and dedicate it fully, saying, 'By this, may I be born as such and such,' and it is the five acts of immediate retribution.

"What is the action such that a person who possesses it dies and passes on as soon as he is born in the hells? It is like King Ajātaśatru's killing of his father. As it is said:

When one has done exceedingly terrible deeds,
Through self-reproach, thorough confession,
And restraint, they are made slight;
But I do not say they are torn out entirely by the root.

"What is the action that brings happiness first and suffering later? Here, when some person gives a gift, his mind is filled with great delight, joy and gladness, but after giving he regrets it. When that person is born among humans, at first he is wealthy, a minister, one with many relatives and so forth, but later he declines.

"Which person's life is exhausted while his karma is not exhausted? It is a person who dies and passes on from the hells and is born again in the hells. And what is it for one's karma to be exhausted while one's life is not exhausted? It is to be happy first and to suffer later...

"Drinking alcohol has thirty-six drawbacks. In this life one's wealth is used up; it becomes a basis for disease; there is fighting; one's faults are exposed; quarrels increase; one reveals one's hiding places; it brings disrepute; it ruins wisdom; one does not gain the wealth one has not gained; one loses what one has gained; one tells secrets; one's work goes to ruin; it becomes the root of sorrow; one becomes feeble; one does not regard ascetics and brahmins as such; one does not serve the head of the family; one does not regard one's father and mother as such; one is disrespectful toward the Three Jewels; one is seized by bad companions; one is abandoned by spiritual friends; one has no self-control; one's senses are unrestrained; one is careless with women; one is displeasing to people; one is reviled by relatives and the venerable; one holds to what is not the Dharma; one rejects the Dharma; one has no confidence in the wise; one neglects carefulness; one is far from nirvāṇa; one becomes intoxicated, and so forth; and after death one is born in the lower realms, in the bad destinies, in the hells."

From The Teaching on Entering the Inconceivable Domain of the Qualities and Wisdom of the Tathāgata: "Mañjuśrī, from within this very assembly, some gods and humans know how many inexpressible eons have passed since the Blessed One Śākyamuni attained perfect and complete awakening, and some know that inexpressible eons have passed since his nirvāṇa. Mañjuśrī, those who see the Tathāgata, and if they see him, even painted as an image or made of clay, whether they see him with a hostile mind or a clear and faithful mind, by that root of virtue their eyes become purified.

"Mañjuśrī, although the disk of the moon appears to wane, to rise and to set on account of the sun, in the disk of the moon there is no rising, no waning and no setting. It is through the ripening of sentient beings' roots of virtue, and in order to establish the reckoning of moments and the like and the counting of years, that such events come about through the blessing of the Tathāgata.

"Mañjuśrī, for a bodhisattva who is held by a spiritual friend, the actions that would make him dwell in the hells, among animals, among hungry ghosts and in the world of Yama for hundreds of thousands of koṭis of niyutas of incalculable and immeasurable eons ripen in this very life. By headaches, or fevers, or pains in the side, or the upheaval of the kingdom, or raids by barbarians, or the upheaval of famine, or by harm to body, harm to speech or harm to mind through one upheaval or another, they are purified within a single life. They are also purified by being reviled, or even by a dream.

"Mañjuśrī, the tathāgatas, the arhats, the perfectly and completely awakened buddhas, are truly constant and everlasting. Mañjuśrī, whoever, with an angry mind or a clear and faithful mind, sees the form of the Tathāgata, painted as an image or made of clay, even if they go to birth as an animal, all their afflictions will come to an end."

From The Questions of Queen Śrīmālā: "Blessed One, the tathāgatagarbha is permanent, stable and constant. Blessed One, the tathāgatagarbha, existing at a limit that has neither beginning nor end, is of a nature that neither arises nor ceases."

From The Determination of the Vinaya: The Questions of Upāli: "A bodhisattva great being who is a householder living at home should abide in two kinds of giving: the giving of Dharma and the giving of material things. One who has gone forth should abide in four kinds of giving: the giving of pens, of ink, of books, and of the Dharma."

The Blessed One said, "Śāriputra, the faults of bodhisattvas that carry great wrongdoing are two: being possessed of aversion and being possessed of delusion."

"Of these, attachment carries little wrongdoing but is slow to be removed. Aversion carries great wrongdoing but is quick to be removed. Delusion carries great wrongdoing and is slow to be removed. Attachment is the seed that links one to rebirth. Aversion is the cause of going to the lower realms. Delusion is the cause of falling into the eight great hells.

"Upāli, what is pure moral discipline for one of the śrāvaka vehicle is impure moral discipline, utterly corrupt moral discipline, for a bodhisattva who has rightly entered the Great Vehicle; and what belongs to one who has rightly entered the Great Vehicle is, for a śrāvaka, impure and utterly corrupt. Why is that? A śrāvaka does not take up craving for any state of existence even for a single instant: that is the pure moral discipline of a śrāvaka, and that very thing is corrupt moral discipline in the Great Vehicle. A bodhisattva is never wearied by taking birth in existence for countless eons, and that very thing is corrupt moral discipline for a śrāvaka."

From The Noble Teaching on the Conventional and Ultimate Truths: "Like a stone that, once broken, cannot be joined again, is the Vinaya of the śrāvakas. Like gold or silver that, once broken, can be joined again, is the Vinaya of the bodhisattvas."

From The Great Secret of Skill in Means: "The bodhisattva who is skilled in means gathers up all those feelings of suffering of those sentient beings and takes them upon himself, and he dons the armor, saying, 'May whatever feelings of mental suffering there are all fall upon my own body, and may those sentient beings be happy.'

"Even if, once in a hundred times, a fault occurs, the bodhisattva great being who is skilled in means trains himself in this way: 'If one were to pass into nirvāṇa with these very elements, sense sources and aggregates, then I would have to discipline myself completely. Instead, I shall put on armor like this. I shall not lose heart at circling in saṃsāra until the very end of time in order to bring sentient beings to maturity; however long I circle in saṃsāra, for just so long I shall bring sentient beings to maturity. I shall make amends for this fault according to the Dharma, and from now on I shall not let a fault occur; and I shall teach all sentient beings the Dharma of emerging from faults.' Thus he puts on the armor.

"Son of good family, when this is carefully examined, even if all four root transgressions should occur in a bodhisattva who has gone forth, if he clears them away through his skill in means, then, son of good family, I declare him without fault, unlike other beings.

"Son of good family, through that arising of the mind endowed with great compassion, even though I acted with a little desire, I shortened and cast off ten thousand eons of saṃsāra. Son of good family, see how the very action that would lead other sentient beings to the hells causes a bodhisattva skilled in means to be born in the world of Brahmā.

Ānanda, the arousing of desire through which
Other beings go to the hells
Is that through which the heroes go to the higher realms:
See what kind of quality comes from it!

"Son of good family, a bodhisattva who has gained acceptance of the nonarising of phenomena could, if he wished, attain perfect and complete awakening even in seven days. Without moving from the abode of Tuṣita, the bodhisattva displays everywhere, by emanation, the descent, the birth and the rest."

The bodhisattva Jñānottara said this to the Blessed One: "Venerable Blessed One, the Blessed One has declared that a bodhisattva does not speak words by which himself and others would be tormented. Then why, Blessed One, when the bodhisattva who was bound to just one more birth was the brahmin youth called Garland of Light Rays, did he say this about the teaching of the Blessed One, the Tathāgata, the arhat, the perfectly and completely awakened buddha Dīpaṃkara: 'What use is it to me to see a shaven-headed ascetic? The mind of awakening is supremely hard to find, so how could a shaven-headed ascetic have awakening?' With what intention did the Blessed One say this?"

When he had asked this, the Blessed One said to the bodhisattva great being Jñānottara: "Son of good family, leave aside making fine distinctions about the measure of the conduct of tathāgatas and bodhisattvas; what is that to you? Why? Son of good family, because the bodhisattva great beings possess inconceivable skill in means, those holy beings abide in whatever way will bring sentient beings to maturity.

"Son of good family, listen further to why the bodhisattva practiced austerities for six years. The bodhisattva skilled in means did so in order to make sentient beings thoroughly weary of unwholesome deeds. Thus, when the bodhisattva said about the teaching of the perfectly and completely awakened buddha Kāśyapa, 'What use is it to me to see a shaven-headed ascetic?' and the rest, that should be seen as that bodhisattva's skill in means and as indirect speech. It is on account of that karmic obscuration that the bodhisattva practices austerities for six years.

"Son of good family, it was to bring those bodhisattvas to maturity, and to teach the ripening of karma, that through that ripening of karma I too practiced austerities for six years. This was to show that if other, ignorant sentient beings speak harsh words in secret against an ascetic or brahmin endowed with moral discipline, it will harm them for a long time. The Tathāgata merely displays that karma; the bodhisattva has not even the slightest karmic obscuration."

"Moreover, he displays that karma also in order to dispel the remorse of those beings who, having spoken harsh words to someone endowed with moral discipline, think, 'There is no chance of liberation for us,' and from then on make no effort. Even if one disparages the Tathāgata in a dream, upon waking that very thing becomes a stain. Because the Tathāgata possesses the mark of a great being that is the supreme sense of taste, even if he eats food that is not tasty, it takes on a flavor that far surpasses the food of the gods."

From The Transference of Existence: "Great king, no phenomenon whatsoever passes from this world to the world beyond, and yet death and birth do manifestly occur. Here, the cessation of every consciousness is called death, and what first arises is called birth.

The eye does not see forms,
And the mind does not know phenomena:
This is the supreme truth,
And the world does not fathom it.
All of these are mere names,
Abiding as mere perception;
Apart from the expression,
There is nothing to be expressed.
By whatever name
Whatever phenomena are expressed,
The world does not fathom it.
All of these are mere names,
And that is not present in them.
This is the true nature of phenomena:
A name is empty of a name,
And a name does not exist as a name."

From The Account of the Sow: "When a son of the gods is about to die and pass on, five omens appear. What are they? His garments, which never before grew old, grow old; his garlands of flowers, which never before grew old, grow old; sweat appears in his two armpits; a foul odor comes from his body; and he takes no delight in his own abode."

From Not Forsaking the Buddha: "In the world realm called Endowed with Mirages, at the time when the roar of the Buddha Vipaśyin arose, ten sons of good family each had five hundred monasteries built, and in each monastery they provided the livelihood of a thousand monks. At that time they accused the Dharma preacher called Pratibhānakūṭa of having broken his moral discipline. Through the ripening of that karma they were born in the hells for ninety thousand years. For five hundred lifetimes they were born among humans as eunuchs, as barbarians, and in households of wrong view. For six hundred years they were blind and without tongues. For seven hundred lifetimes they went forth, yet until the very end of their lives they died without ever hearing a dhāraṇī. That very karmic obscuration obscured those sons of good family.

"Son of good family, therefore you should have faith and understand this. Having heard this discourse on the Dharma, even if one sees with one's own eyes a Dharma preacher with corrupt moral discipline, one should not speak of it, let alone what one has only heard. Son of good family, if someone were to gouge out the eyes of all sentient beings, one who looks upon a Dharma preacher with the intention of reviling him commits a far more grievous act than that. If someone were to imprison all sentient beings, and another, on seeing a Dharma preacher, sits with his back turned, the former does not come to a hundredth, a thousandth, a hundred-thousandth part of the latter, nor to any number or reckoning. Why is that? Because whoever disparages a Dharma preacher disparages the Buddha. One who wishes to honor the Buddha should honor the Dharma preacher.

"Son of good family, therefore, however bodhisattvas may abide, a bodhisattva should not look for mistakes, even at the cost of his life. Why is that? Son of good family, because these four qualities purify the awakening of bodhisattvas. What are the four? Meditating on emptiness; a mind free of resentment toward all sentient beings; always bringing benefit to bodhisattvas; and giving the Dharma with a mind free of material motive.

syadyathedan adche adchabḍi anbile aśeṣo atrite anayudhi aphe abijarida bhabarudtra aṃgama yuttanadañaba prakṣibe ṇibaḍe agrahe huhume capale saḍesame samadeyoḍi nayobariśodhi

"When one recites these dhāraṇī-mantras for seven days, one should cultivate the recollection of the Buddha for seven days."

From the chapter teaching the superior intention: "The true nature of the buddhas, the blessed ones, is hard to realize. Why is that? Ānanda, because the mind must be purified even of all afflictions, and one must not be moved by any phenomenon whatsoever, nor be lifted up by it."

From The Way of the Dharma: "Sons of good family, be fearful! Be without delight! Be without affection! Be without compassion! Sons of good family, be conciliatory! Act constantly! Examine! Be wild of mind! Sons of good family, be attached as well! Be always resentful! Look on for a long time as well! Do not repay what has been done for you! Sons of good family, make the deeds of Māra blaze! Make the qualities of the Buddha subside! Do not disparage what is to be disparaged by the śrāvakas! Do not delight in what the śrāvakas delight in!"

From The Questions of Ratnacūḍa: "The purities of conduct of bodhisattvas are four. What are they? The purity of the conduct of the perfections, the purity of the factors of awakening, the purity of the conduct of accomplishing the higher knowledges, and the purity of the conduct of bringing sentient beings to maturity. The purities of moral discipline of a bodhisattva are five. What are they? Not exalting oneself; not exposing others; not speaking unkindly to those who are untrained; not touching the mind of a śrāvaka; and not wishing for the ground of a pratyekabuddha."

From The Questions of Śrīvasu: "If one is unable to train in the five bases of training, then one should take up and train in four, or three, or two, or even just one of them. If one is unable to train in even a single base of training, then whatever evil action one does, one should do it without clinging, not intensely, and unwillingly. One should rebuke it and censure it. With virtue one should do the opposite."

From Like Golden Sand: "In the time it takes the elder Śāriputra to speak a single word, a pratyekabuddha teaches countless discourses on the Dharma. In the time it takes a pratyekabuddha to speak a single word, a bodhisattva teaches countless, immeasurable, inexpressible discourses on the Dharma, and explains their meaning as well."

From The Questions of the God Susthitamati: "Venerable Śāriputra, there may be a son or daughter of good family who relies on the view of a self, or relies on the view of a sentient being, or relies on the view of a life force, and who, for as long as he lives, honors with every comfort tathāgatas, arhats, perfectly and completely awakened buddhas as numerous as the sands of the river Ganges, together with their saṅghas of monks. And there may be one who hears a Dharma such as this, profound, hard to understand, at odds with the whole world, who hears that phenomena are empty, signless, wishless, peaceful, secluded and unborn, without sentient being, without life force and without person, impermanent, suffering and selfless, and who, having heard it, rejects it and falls into the hells. Venerable Śāriputra, the son or daughter of good family who hears this profound Dharma, rejects it and falls into the hells will rise from them and be liberated far more swiftly. But it is not so for the son or daughter of good family who relies on the view of a self and, without hearing this Dharma, honors tathāgatas, arhats, perfectly and completely awakened buddhas as numerous as the sands of the river Ganges.

"Śāriputra, moreover, those sons or daughters of good family who lend an ear and listen to this profound Dharma but do not understand it, reject it and go to the hells will be liberated far more swiftly. It is not so for others, who hold conceptual references and have fallen into doubt."

From The Teaching on the Two Truths: The Blessed One said, "Mañjuśrī, it is like this, for example. It is better for a man weakened by hunger and starvation to endure it; it is not so to eat poisoned food unknowingly. In the same way, it is better for a bodhisattva without conceptual reference to be miserly, of corrupt moral discipline, malicious, lazy, forgetful and without wisdom; it is not so to apply oneself with the mind of śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas who hold conceptual references, with their moral discipline, patience, diligence, meditative concentration and wisdom."

The Blessed One said, "Son of the gods, it is like this, for example: the food of a poor man is poison for a universal monarch. In the same way, the moral discipline and the rest, up to the diligence, of śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas who hold conceptual references become, for a bodhisattva, corrupt moral discipline and the rest, up to laziness."

The son of the gods said this to Mañjuśrī, who had become a youth: "Mañjuśrī, did you train in the Vinaya of the bodhisattvas, or did you train in the Vinaya of the śrāvakas?"

Mañjuśrī said, "Son of the gods, what do you think? Is there any water that does not flow into the great ocean?"

The son of the gods said, "Of what is 'the sameness of all phenomena as utterly nonarising' a synonym?"

Mañjuśrī said, "Son of the gods, it is a synonym of the ultimate, which is without diversity."

He said, "Since all phenomena are the same in being utterly nonarising, why do they appear as diverse?"

"Son of the gods, when, within the sameness of all phenomena as utterly nonarising, one fully conceives them as ultimately substances, things and essences, then one treats them as ultimately diverse. When one treats them as ultimately diverse, views that hold conceptual references multiply. Through those many views that hold conceptual references, one wanders helplessly among the various destinies. Son of the gods, if, within the sameness of all phenomena as utterly nonarising, one does not conceive them as ultimately essences, substances and things, then one does not treat them as ultimately diverse. When one does not treat them as ultimately diverse, then, by way of going to the ultimate, utter absence of things, one comes to understand, evenly, the sameness of all phenomena as ultimately utterly unborn.

"Conventionally, conceptual thought is the door to the afflictions. Impermanence is the door to freedom from the afflictions. The awakening mind is the door to all the qualities of a buddha. The perfection of wisdom is the door to nirvāṇa.

"Conventionally, with the flesh eye one sees sentient beings and buddha fields. With the divine eye one sees the deaths and births of sentient beings. With the wisdom eye one knows the mental activity and the stirrings of sentient beings. With the Dharma eye one sees all phenomena correctly, just as they are. With the buddha eye, although one performs all the deeds of a buddha, ultimately one neither knows nor does anything at all."

It occurs in The King of Sūtras on Ascertainment: "Ānanda, it is like this, for example. The udumbara flower appears at the same time as the birth of a buddha. It is colorful and radiant and beautiful, and its fragrance pervades a whole league around. That flower clears away dimness of sight, purifies mindfulness, and fully soothes disease. If it does not appear even for a universal monarch, what need is there to speak of one whose moral discipline is corrupt?"

Where does such a flower grow? It occurs in the Avadāna: "To the north of the great lake Anavatapta is a Meru called Five-Peaked, and beyond it lies a grove of udumbara trees. When the Blessed One descends from Tuṣita and enters the womb, that grove comes into bud. When he is born, the buds open. When he attains full awakening, the flowers come into full bloom. When he relinquishes the formative forces of his life, they grow old. When he passes into nirvāṇa, the flowers, leaves and the rest fall away. Each flower is about the size of a chariot wheel. When a buddha is born into the kṣatriya caste, white flowers appear; when he is born into the brahmin caste, they become yellow."

In the Sūtra of the Fortunate Eon it says: "After this Fortunate Eon there will come sixty eons without a buddha. After that, in the eon called Great Renown, ten thousand buddhas will appear. After that, for ten thousand eons no buddha will appear. After that, in the eon called Like the Stars, eighty thousand buddhas will appear. After that, for three hundred eons no buddha will appear. After that, in the eon called Array of Qualities, eighty-four thousand buddhas will appear."

In the Connected Discourses (Saṃyuktāgama) there appears the simile of the yoke and the turtle, for how hard it is to obtain a human body.

In the Kāśyapa Chapter it says: "Kāśyapa, just as people bow to the new moon but do not do so to the full moon, in the same way those who have deep faith in me should bow to bodhisattvas, and not to the tathāgatas. Why is that? Because tathāgatas are born from bodhisattvas, and all śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas are born from tathāgatas."

In the Compendium of the Dharma (Dharmasaṃgīti) it is taught by way of a simile that a root of virtue dedicated to awakening does not run out until the eon comes to its end, like a drop of water that has fallen into the ocean.

In the Seal of Engaging in the Development of the Power of Faith it says: "Mañjuśrī, compared with someone who obstructs the roots of virtue of as many sentient beings as there are particles in the finest atoms of all the world systems, if another person obstructs a single root of virtue of a single layman who keeps the ten virtuous paths of action and does not look to any other teacher, that person generates incalculably more evil. Compared with obstructing the virtue of that many laymen, if one obstructs a single monk, one generates incalculably more evil than that."

In the Sūtra of the Application of Mindfulness of the True Dharma it says: "Daughter of good family, these ten things are obstacles to meditative concentration and to recitation. The ten are: listening to the talk of bustle and busyness; drawing pictures; fussing over seats and clothes; pondering the planets and the lunar mansions; treating the food one has received as one's own; dwelling on piśācīs and the magical transformations of gods; relying on kings; not asking questions of the learned; inviting such people in as guests; and living mingled with others."

In the Sūtra of the Unmixed it appears: "Two things obstruct the generation of the mind set on omniscience: deceit and guile, and so one must be without them. Its foundation, too, is straightforwardness and freedom from deceit."

One should also strive constantly in the ultimate offering. In the Lion's Roar of Maitreya it says: "When there is neither the perception of anything as a buddha nor even the seeing of a buddha, how much more does that become an offering to the Buddha! And what, then, is the offering to the Buddha? It is not to give rise to the sign of perception."

In the Sūtra on the Arising of the Tathāgatas it says: "O son of the Victors, this phrase 'the appearance of a Tathāgata' is a word meant to gladden sentient beings fully. The phrase 'complete nirvāṇa' is a word meant to stir weariness with saṃsāra in sentient beings. The tathāgatas have no birth, and they do not pass into complete nirvāṇa. Why is that? Because the Tathāgata abides in the dharmadhātu, and in order to fulfil the aspirations of sentient beings he appears as if passing into nirvāṇa." It also says that beings see the Buddha's nirvāṇa that way because of themselves, like the water of a broken vessel, and that his appearing is likewise like clear water in a sound vessel.

The noble Nāgārjuna said: "Sentient beings who are devoted to the One Vehicle are extremely hard to find, because it is taught in many sūtras that there is only the One Vehicle."

In the Noble Chapter of Truth it says: "Mañjuśrī, if the Tathāgata were to teach the Śrāvaka Vehicle to some, the Pratyekabuddha Vehicle to some, and the Great Vehicle to some, the Tathāgata's mind would be impure, and I would also have become tight-fisted with the Dharma. Mañjuśrī, all the teachings I have taught to sentient beings, whatever they are, are for the sake of attaining the wisdom of omniscience: they incline toward awakening, they flow down into the Great Vehicle, they accomplish omniscience, and they bring one to a single destination. Therefore with me there is no setting out of separate vehicles. As for what is called 'the setting out of vehicles': the setting out of grounds that the Tathāgata made is a setting out of persons, a setting out of their accumulations as lesser and as immeasurable. Since the dharmadhātu has no divisions, there are no different vehicles here. This is spoken merely as a gateway for teaching entry on the conventional level."

In the Perfection of Wisdom it says: "Son of the gods, those who have not yet generated the mind toward unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening should generate the mind toward unsurpassed awakening. Those who have definitively entered into the certainty of the right state are incapable of generating the mind toward awakening. Why is that? Because they have set a limit to the stream of saṃsāra. Yet if they were to generate the mind toward unsurpassed awakening, I too would rejoice, and I would not obstruct their roots of virtue."

One should direct one's attention above all to the teaching that is still more supremely exalted than the exalted. In the Questions of King Dhāraṇīśvara, the Great Assembly, the Wheel of Irreversibility, the Lion's Roar of Śrīmālā, and the Descent into Laṅkā, it is taught that the three vehicles converge into one. In the Lion's Roar of Śrīmālā it says: "'The true Dharma' is a designation for the Great Vehicle. Why is that? Because all the roots of virtue of the śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas, and all supramundane roots of virtue, are brought forth by the Great Vehicle."

In the Candragarbha Sūtra it says: "The ultimate truth is unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening, and it is not shared with any śrāvakas or pratyekabuddhas. Therefore no conventional truth whatsoever is able to accomplish the supreme accumulation of roots of virtue for unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening. Candragarbha, it is like this: no spark whatsoever is able to dry up the great ocean."

In the same way, if no conventional truth whatsoever is able to dry up even the ocean of one's own afflictions, what need is there even to speak of those of other sentient beings?

In the Bodhisattva Piṭaka it says: "There are two causes for the arising of the noble right view: hearing from others, and appropriate attention on one's own part."

In the Chapter on the Magical Display of Mañjuśrī it says: "Son of the gods, the teaching of a wisdom made of letters is the skillful means of the tathāgatas; that wisdom itself is beyond letters. Just as fire does not come from fire-sticks that are not rubbed, but fire does come from rubbing them, in the same way, if the Tathāgata were to teach the wisdom of a buddha extensively at the very outset, no sentient being whatsoever would understand it. Therefore the Tathāgata teaches that wisdom through the talk of the fire-stick, and that wisdom is beyond letters." The son of the gods asked: "Mañjuśrī, what is the talk of the fire-stick?" Mañjuśrī said: "Son of the gods, it is talk about engaging in vows, in having few requisites, in the perfections, and in the factors conducive to awakening."

The noble Nāgārjuna explained that one should not engage with the profound nature of reality without skill in means. In the Teaching on Entering the Inconceivable Domain of the Qualities and Wisdom of the Tathāgatas it says: "Mañjuśrī, some understand that ten years have passed since he attained full awakening; some, that ten years have passed since he passed into complete nirvāṇa; some, that he has gone to the seat of awakening; and some, that the teaching of the Blessed One Śākyamuni has disappeared. In the same way they understand things in all sorts of ways."

In the Great Nirvāṇa Sūtra it says: "All the sentient beings who dwell in the great trichiliocosm are loved, each one as if an only child, except for the icchantikas, those of great desire. Those who say that the Tathāgata is conditioned speak falsely; such beings will go to hell as if going to their own home. Whether one has disparaged the true Dharma or is an icchantika, one may not be killed. One would not dare to take life for any reason whatsoever. Just as a poisonous tree or a weed that grows in a field or a house must be pulled out by the root, so I permit those of corrupt moral discipline and those who disparage the true Dharma to be chastised like servants.

"Understand that the Tathāgata is permanent and stable. This world is not an emanated body; understand it with the thought: 'The Tathāgata has chosen to take on a body that depends on poisons and medicines.'

"Those who are of corrupt moral discipline are called shaven-headed householders. One should not take up the five precepts of a layman; one should take up weapons and protect the monks. After the Tathāgata has passed into nirvāṇa, in the evil time, because of the calamity of famine, most will go forth and become shaven-heads, and they will drive out and kill the monks who have moral discipline and good qualities. The Saṅgha is of three kinds: the saṅgha of the immoral, the saṅgha of the childish, and the saṅgha that is so by its intrinsic nature.

"The Buddha passed into nirvāṇa at daybreak on the full-moon day of the last month of spring. I have taught: 'From now on I do not permit my śrāvakas to eat meat. If they eat meat, great loving-kindness will be cut off.'

"Son of good family, there will also appear those who cook grain that is not allowable, accept meat and fish, prepare nasal remedies, keep vessels of oil and parasols, practise the cult of the gods, rely on kings, expound books of divination, busy themselves with medicine, accept servants, gold and the like, make paintings and images, plant trees, and devote themselves to secret mantras, medicines, children's games, the arts of dance, affected gaits, and the arts of seduction. One who has given these up is called a monk.

"In some parts of Jambudvīpa he shows himself attaining awakening in the form of a woman. In some parts of Jambudvīpa he transforms himself into the four elements, and in order to cure the illnesses of sentient beings he transforms himself into every medicine and every tree. The Tathāgata cures every illness except the incurable illness of the icchantika. Those who pass into nirvāṇa are the ones who are cured.

"The statement 'The Tathāgata has a body of flesh, and he passes into complete nirvāṇa like a fire whose fuel is used up' belongs to the Śrāvaka Vehicle; it is not authoritative.

"Monks should first, as the way of an ascetic, show compassion even to ants. As the way of an ascetic, they should completely give up any drink that tastes of alcohol. Even in a dream they should not tell a lie or give rise to the thought of meeting with a woman.

"Know that the Buddha's speech is the path of the Middle Way. The Buddha abandoned both extremes, permanence and annihilation, and taught the path of the Middle Way. Son of good family, call to mind what I taught in the Sūtra Comprising All Merit and in the Great Perfection of Wisdom Sūtra: the light of entering nonduality, and that self and selflessness are not two.

"Buddha-nature is not something fabricated from the beginning, while the afflictions are adventitious. Therefore one who wishes to become a buddha should destroy the afflictions. Even bodhisattvas who have attained the ten grounds, after investigating thoroughly, see only dimly that buddha-nature is present, unmade, in their own bodies. All śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas, too, should have faith, in the manner of the Great Nirvāṇa Sūtra, that buddha-nature is present in their own bodies.

"Understand that whatever is well spoken and accords with the Dharma, even if it is worldly, is supramundane. Know that every treatise, every knowledge mantra, and every secret mantra whatsoever was spoken by the perfect Buddha."

"How did the Blessed One speak of the alphabet?" He declared: "The alphabet and all phenomena are emanations. Moreover, I emanated it so that, by learning the alphabet in the way of the true Dharma, beings would come to tell Dharma from what is not Dharma; it was not for the sake of what is not Dharma."

"If one asks who the noble ones are: noble is whatever has come about as freedom from attachment, as contentment with what is allowable, and as the freeing of sentient beings from the ocean of existence; that is called noble. A son or daughter of good family who has entered the path is noble, and a monk endowed with moral discipline is also called noble."

"In the body of the Tathāgata there is not even a trace of a body built up from the elements."

"Son of good family, after I have passed away it will be practised in Jambudvīpa for four thousand years, and after that it will sink beneath the earth. The tail end of my Dharma will remain for eighty years. When the Dharma is on the point of disappearing, the great sūtra of the Great Parinirvāṇa will appear in Jambudvīpa, and after it has rained down the rain of Dharma for forty years, it will disappear.

"Whether one is old or whether young,
the one from whom you come to understand the Dharma
should be honoured and bowed to,
as the gods do for Mahendra (Indra).

"This I taught for the sake of the bodhisattvas of future times, not for the sake of the śrāvakas.

"Some take the moon to be half, yet the moon does not become half, nor does it become more; the moon merely appears that way because of Mount Meru.

"Furthermore, son of good family, it is like this. When the moon and Rāhu, the lord of the asuras, meet, people in the world think, 'The moon has been seized by the planet.' Yet Rāhu does not seize the moon, nor is he able to eclipse it; Rāhu's hand merely covers the moon so that its light is not seen. The moon has no wound and no affliction, yet people take it that the moon is suffering. While the moon remains just as it is by nature, it seems itself to be together with Rāhu; this is the way of the world. The Tathāgata appears perfectly in the three worlds; this is the way of the buddhas.

"Son of good family, it is like this. At the new moon, which belongs neither to the waxing nor to the waning half of the month, at that juncture between the two when there is neither sun nor moon, a comet rises and, unnoticed by anyone, vanishes in a single instant. In the same way, pratyekabuddhas too appear at times when no buddha is present, establish many sentient beings in the true Dharma, and then vanish.

"The icchantikas too have buddha-nature; nevertheless it lies utterly dormant, as in an abyss. Just as a silkworm wraps itself all around and, having sealed itself in, is unable to come out, so too buddha-nature is unable to come forth from within the icchantika, because of the fault of their karma.

"Furthermore, son of good family, listen to how insatiable women are. Even if a woman were to indulge in desire, clinging to it, with as many men as there are grains of sand in the river Ganges, or countless multitudes, or myriads beyond count, she would not be sated, just as the ocean is not wetted and filled by the urine of a fly. In the same way women are not sated.

"This great sūtra too will go to the south and rain down the rain of words that convey the intent of the Great Parinirvāṇa. Then the bodhisattva great beings of the south, knowing that the true Dharma is perishing, will go to the land of Kashmir like clouds when the winter season draws near, and it will sink into the earth. All of the true Dharma, the supreme ambrosia, the extensive sūtras of the Great Vehicle, will disappear."

He asked: "What is an icchantika like?" He declared: "Whether monk, nun, layman or laywoman, whoever rejects the sūtras with evil words and afterwards does not even ask forgiveness has entered the path of the icchantika. Those who commit the four defeats and the five acts of immediate retribution, who even though they know, 'We have entered a fearful path,' pay no regard to the fear, who do not think, 'The true Dharma will arise,' and who even speak in dispraise of it, have also entered that path. Those who say, 'The Three Jewels do not exist,' have also entered that path. What is given to one of supremely corrupt discipline bears not the slightest fruit. It is impossible for them to give rise to the mind that confesses and asks forgiveness, which bears great fruit.

"All are terrified of punishment;
to all, their own life is dear.
Taking one's own experience as the measure,
one should not strike, one should not kill."

In the Sublime Golden Light it says: "Because it is free of all obscurations and complete in all virtuous qualities, suchness together with correct wisdom is the Dharma body (dharmakāya). The transformed all-base consciousness is the Dharma body. The transformed afflicted mind is the enjoyment body (saṃbhogakāya). The transformed engaging consciousnesses are the emanation body (nirmāṇakāya)."

In the Conch Garland Sūtra it says: "When buddha-nature has not become manifest, it is called the all-base. When it has become manifest, it is called the Dharma body."

In the Dense Array it says:
"Mind, in two aspects, actually
appears as grasped and as grasper.
No body whatsoever,
such as a pot or a cloth, exists."

In the Former Practice of Golden Colour it says: "That a pratyekabuddha teaches the Dharma by means of the body is the nature of things; it is not by means of words. The Blessed One, the pratyekabuddha."

In the Dense Array it says:
"Whatever appears in this way,
from signs that appear as form,
is the all-base, which has habitual tendencies;
in all the grounds it abides as if it were form.
Just as milk, when it is transformed,
appears with the qualities of curd and of butter,
so too the all-base consciousness,
when it is transformed, appears as if it were form.
Form endowed with the qualities of form
is the all-base abiding as if it were form;
it is just as children imagine it:
form does not exist apart.
The forms that exist upon the earth,
and the mansions that stand in the realms of the gods,
whatever appears in its various aspects,
is the all-base transformed.
The all-base is hands on every side,
on every side it is mouth, eyes and head.
Whether gradually or else all at once,
it abides, inverted, in the grounds.
A view of self that has grown
as great as Mount Meru does no harm;
what harms the view of emptiness
is pride, a false imagining."

"Practitioners of yoga should not apply emptiness
where it does not belong.
If it is applied where it does not belong,
it is like nectar turning into poison.
However many views there may be,
whatever they are, they are found in embodied beings.
In order to do away with views,
the way of emptiness was taught.
Having heard the view of emptiness,
some, whose views are many,
become holders of an incurable view,
like a disease the physician has given up.
Just as the fire that burns up the fuel
does not remain once there is no fuel,
so when the fuel of views has been burnt,
the fire of emptiness too will cease.
At the time when views have ceased,
the fire of correct wisdom arises,
and having burnt up the afflictions, it shines in beauty.
Mind, in two aspects, actually
appears as grasped and as grasper.
No object whatsoever,
such as a pot or a cloth, exists.
Whatever is the domain of consciousness,
whatever appears in this way,
has become consciousness,
and is not a pot or the like.
Whatever mistaken appearance there is in this way,
pots and the like, whatever they may be,
is the all-base transformed;
it is the very character of mistaken appearance."

In the noble sūtra called Śrīgupta it says: "Fire burst out from that brahmin's whole body, and crying, 'I am burning, I am burning!' he fell into the great hell. Then the brahmin youth Dharmakāma said this: 'Master, do not be afraid. I will protect you.' Then the brahmin youth Dharmakāma said, 'By the truth of my practising for awakening for the sake of sentient beings, and by these words of truth, may this one's hell be stilled!' And at that very moment the brahmin was freed from hell."

In the Array of the Ornaments of Eloquence it says: "In the same way, from form up to the thirty-seven factors of awakening, there are countless different names in other buddha fields. As for this enumeration, one should understand that all names are false imagination."

The venerable Subhūti said: "If my mind had not been liberated, I too would generate the mind toward unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening; but now there is nothing at all to be done."

"Hunger does no harm, but eating food mixed with poison is not like that. In the same way, it is better for a bodhisattva not even to have heard of the Śrāvaka Vehicle.

"The ornaments of a bodhisattva are true ornaments: the ornament of never forgetting the awakening mind; the ornament of abiding in the intention of great compassion; the ornament of a mind unobstructed toward all sentient beings; the ornament of being insatiable in seeking to hear the Dharma; the ornament of mastering the teachings as they were heard and then properly investigating and analysing them; the ornament of not objectifying any phenomenon even while bringing sentient beings to maturity; the ornament of reflecting on the profound Dharma and on dependent origination, and of knowing which faculties of sentient beings are superior and which are not; and the ornament of skill in means with regard to the blessing of the buddhas."

In the Avataṃsaka it says: "The domain of the buddhas is born from samādhi. Then, through the blessing of the Buddha, all the oceans of world systems whose ground and core are adorned with ornaments of flowers shook violently.

"The Dharma body of the perfect Buddha
is like pure space.
The form body too is utterly stable,
and enters fully into this nature of reality.
With a single instant of mind he shows
all the ways the fields of the three times arise, and how they are entered.
The fields of the three times that the buddhas reveal
are Vairocana's inexhaustible ocean of fields.
The fields are long and short, in the shapes of continents, and of every kind;
equal to the dharmadhātu, vast and boundless, they are like mirages and illusions.
Magical displays, distinct, pure and stainless, brought forth entirely by mind,
the realms of space, the cores of manifold ornament, appear in this transformation.
Fields from which jewel light streams forth,
thoroughly covered with clouds of streaming light,
possessed of every jewel in all its variety,
are all the emanations of karma.
The fields that have arisen from the ocean of mind
abide right within the mind.
From the very beginning they do not abide, like mirages;
they are brought forth in full realization.
Through the ways of meditative concentration and the liberations, the grounds are purified,
and oceans of fields, manifold and vast, arise.
Just as the kings of wish-fulfilling jewels
appear in every colour,
so through the pure intentions of sentient beings
the fields appear in the same way.
Just as the abodes of clouds of the nāga kings
show themselves in the expanse of space,
so through the aspirations of the buddhas
the varieties of fields arise.
Just as the forms of sentient beings' bodies
are as their minds imagine,
so the forms of the fields as well
are all drawn by karma.
Just as the protectors appear
in this form or that,
according to the intentions of sentient beings,
so the fields appear.
Just as a master illusionist by his mantras
displays all kinds of gateways,
so through the karma of sentient beings
they arise inconceivably.
Just as the forms of a painting
are drawn by the painter's work,
so all these fields
are drawn as pictures by the mind.

"On that continent, Banner of Awakening, there is a town called Infinite Treasury, set within an expanse of tiered buildings and surrounded by as many towns as there are finest atoms in ten oceans of world systems. Because of the Tathāgata's intent, that town called Infinite Treasury is endowed with oceans of excellence as many as the finest atoms in ten oceans of world systems. Because of the Tathāgata's intent, each one of them is moreover encircled by oceans of moats of samādhi as many as the finest atoms in ten oceans of world systems. Each one of those oceans of moats of samādhi is made perfectly pure by oceans of the water of the Tathāgata's calm abiding, as many as the finest atoms in ten oceans of world systems. And each one of those oceans of the water of the Tathāgata's calm abiding is completely filled with flowers of the Tathāgata's wisdom and qualities, utpalas, lotuses, kumudas and puṇḍarīkas, as many as the finest atoms in ten oceans of world systems."

"That town Infinite Treasury is built of bricks of the utterly pure jewel of mind, oceans of them, as many as the finest atoms in ten oceans of world systems, and so forth. There too he is known as Śākyamuni; he is known as Vairocana.

"All those sentient beings
are described entirely in terms of the five aggregates.
They are a great heap of karma.
The root of karma is mind,
and mind is said to be like an illusion;
these beings have become illusions.
Mind is like a painter;
mind is what makes the aggregates.
However many world systems there are,
all these worlds are drawn by mind.
Whoever wishes to know
all the victors of all three times
should look upon all the buddhas
as the very substance of mind, the very substance of the Dharma."

"What are conditioned phenomena? They are the desire realm, the form realm, the formless realm, and the realms of sentient beings; these are conditioned phenomena. What are unconditioned phenomena? They are space, nirvāṇa, cessation through analysis, cessation without analysis, dependent origination, and the abiding of the Dharma; these are unconditioned phenomena."

"Fashioned by manifold karma, encompassed by the element of space and by all world systems, firmly established as they extend into the world systems of one direction after another: in all buddha fields, may countless jewel mountains, mountains of wisdom supremely lofty and pure, shine forth everywhere! May countless masses of jewel light, formed of masses of wisdom, abide on every side! May countless jewel lamps, perfected by the lamp of wisdom, abide on every side! May countless trees with jewel leaves that send out the light of wisdom everywhere abide on every side!"

In that text, the dedication too should be understood in keeping with the nature of the jewels: "Through the incense of wisdom, incense that culminates in non-attachment, may vast, perfectly pure and immeasurable activity of mind abide on every side!" In the same way, from "May inconceivable flower ornaments of every kind of flower abide everywhere" through "May flowers of wisdom come into being," through "flowers of wisdom that culminate in non-attachment," and garlands of wisdom, ointments, powders of wisdom, incense, garments of wisdom, ornaments of robes, ornaments of parasols of wisdom, victory banners of wisdom, banners, streamers of wisdom, and inexpressibly pure precious jewels of wisdom, "may they abide everywhere": when in this way a bodhisattva great being dedicates these roots of virtue, from the giving of the Dharma and the rest, to the buddha field accomplished by all roots of virtue, that is the dedication called "Rāhu, Lord of the Asuras." His natural body measures seven hundred leagues; his emanated body measures sixty-eight thousand leagues.

"It is like this: although space is unborn and unceasing, it holds the arising and ceasing of all the worlds. Whatever enlightened activity the buddhas, the blessed ones, accomplish through the sense base of the body, they bring to completion through the sense base of the mind."

The bodhisattva Samantabhadra said: "O sons of the Victors, I have not seen a single phenomenon as blameworthy as a bodhisattva giving rise to aversion toward others. Why is that? Because a bodhisattva who gives rise to anger toward others takes on these one million obscuring factors. What are they? Not seeing bodhisattvas, not hearing the Dharma, dwelling in the lower realms, mixing with the Lesser Vehicle, holding to evil friends, frequenting the domain of Māra, being far from the teaching of the Buddha, being devoted to the Lesser Vehicle, not trusting the Great Vehicle, rejecting the Dharma of the Tathāgata, uprooting the karma that brings association with bodhisattvas, siding with what is contrary to the Perfection of Wisdom, lying in wait for grounds for accusation, and coming to keep oneself far from the family of the Tathāgata, and so on. Thus one takes on a million gateways of obscuring factors."

"When a world system is destroyed, first a great sound like this arises: 'Ah, friends! The first meditative concentration is supremely blissful, because it is free of desire and malice; it becomes the cause for passing completely beyond the desire realm.' Hearing that sound, sentient beings attain the first meditative concentration and are born from the desire realm into the world of Brahmā. In the same way the sound is proclaimed for each concentration up to the fourth, and in the same way they attain it and are born there."

"From the abode of the nāga king Anavatapta four great rivers flow down. The great river Ganges flows from the mouth of an elephant; the Sītā flows from the mouth of a lion; the Sindhu flows from the mouth of an ox; the Pakṣu flows from the mouth of a horse. The Ganges flows from the front, carrying silver sand; the Sītā carries diamond sand; the Sindhu, golden sand; and the Pakṣu flows carrying sand of blue beryl. As they flow, each of them circles the great lake seven times to the right and then flows off in the four directions, and they are held to join the ocean. The islands lying between the seven circuits are adorned with utpala, lotus, kumuda and puṇḍarīka flowers, with petals of many kinds made of the substance of divine jewels, and with cores of many kinds. The round expanse of that lake measures fifty leagues, and its depth is also fifty leagues. Its shores are encircled on every side by many kinds of jewels laid like bricks."

"Just as the great lake Anavatapta is encircled by fields of jewels, in the same way, when a bodhisattva displays manifest awakening, he too is encircled and adorned on every side by buddha fields. All four great rivers flow out from mouths about a league across."

The great bodhisattva Glory Born in the Family of the Tathāgata said to the great bodhisattva Samantabhadra: "O son of the Victors, how should one understand the visible body of the tathāgatas?"

He said: "O son of the Victors, as for the visible body, it is like this. When the disc of the sun rises over Jambudvīpa, it first shines on Mount Meru, the king of mountains. After that it shines on the other great mountains; after that, on the black mountains; after that, on the high ground; and after that it shines on the low ground. Yet the disc of the sun has no thought such as, 'This I shall illuminate first.'

"O son of the Victors, in the same way, when the body of the tathāgatas, the sun disc of the wisdom body, comes to rise over the realm of the dharmadhātu, which has neither edge nor centre, it first rises upon the holy beings who are like supreme kings of mountains, in the manner of perfectly revealing the great light of the Dharma. After that it rises upon those who have entered the vehicles of the śrāvakas and the pratyekabuddhas; after that, upon sentient beings whose roots of virtue are certain, according to their capacity as vessels; and after that it rises upon all sentient beings, even down to those who are certain to go astray. Yet although it rises in this way, those sun rays of the Tathāgata's wisdom body have no thought such as, 'We shall first make the Great Vehicle shine for these sentient beings, and after that we shall shine on these others.' This is because the sun disc of the Tathāgata's wisdom body is free of conceptual thought.

"Again, it is like this. The disc of the sun has no thought, and its rays rise evenly, yet on the great mountains it appears in different ways. In the same way, the sun disc of the Tathāgata's wisdom body is free of conceptual thought and its rays rise evenly, yet because the roots of virtue of sentient beings differ and their intentions differ, different rays of the Tathāgata also appear and are present.

"O son of the Victors, it is like this. Even when the disc of the sun has risen, it does not appear to the blind, because they have no eyes. Yet even though it does not appear within their range of sight, it is not without benefit to the blind: it benefits them by digesting their food, by driving the cold from their bodies, and by dispelling bodily illnesses such as disorders of bile. In the same way, the sun disc of the Tathāgata's wisdom body does not appear within the range of sight of sentient beings whose eye of superior intention is blind, who have wrong views, wrong conduct and wrong livelihood, and who have become like the blind, because they do not have the eye of faith. Yet although it does not appear within their range of sight, through the power of the Tathāgata the discomfort in their bodies is blunted, and it is not without benefit, for it benefits them by removing what remains of the causes of future afflictions."

He said: "The expression of the speech of the tathāgatas is like this. Just as the sun disc of the Tathāgata's wisdom body is free of conceptual thought and its rays rise evenly, yet because the roots of virtue of sentient beings differ and their intentions differ, different rays of the Tathāgata also appear and are present: O son of the Victors, it is like this. Even when the disc of the sun has risen, it does not appear to the blind, because they have no eyes. Yet even though it does not appear within their range of sight, it is not without benefit to the blind: it benefits them by digesting their food, by driving the cold from their bodies, and by dispelling bodily illnesses such as disorders of bile.

"Sentient beings do not know the Tathāgata just as he truly is, and yet the wisdom of the Tathāgata is present within them; how very wonderful! Thinking, 'By whatever means, I shall untie, through the noble path, the great knots of these beings' own perceptions,' he teaches the noble path so that they may realize the wisdom of the Tathāgata. He clears away every form of perception. Because perception is cleared away in this way, he sustains all beings with immeasurable wisdom.

"O son of the Victors, because space is unborn, whether world systems are destroyed or come together, it remains just the same, neither diminishing nor increasing."

He asked: "What is the complete nirvāṇa of the tathāgatas like?"

He said: "O son of the Victors, the tathāgatas do not declare or show to bodhisattvas a complete nirvāṇa that is utterly final. If a water vessel is broken, or is carried away from there to some other place, or is left empty, and the reflection of the sun no longer appears in it, is that the fault of the sun's disc?"

He said: "It is not."

He said: "In the same way, to sentient beings who are like broken vessels and the like, the Tathāgata appears to pass into nirvāṇa.

"The sun's disc, equal in extent to the dharmadhātu,
appears as a reflection in a vessel, but does not appear in a broken one.
In this way the supreme lord of men appears everywhere,
yet beings without faith perceive him as having passed into nirvāṇa.

"O son of the Victors, a discourse on the Dharma such as this is utterly decisive in revealing the family of the Tathāgata; it is a dedication that follows the domain of the Tathāgata, gone to the furthest and final limit."

"O son of the Victors, these ten are the habitual tendencies of bodhisattvas: the habitual tendency of the bodhisattva, the habitual tendency of roots of virtue, the habitual tendency of bringing the mind to full maturity, the habitual tendency of beholding the buddhas, the habitual tendency of being born in purified world systems, the habitual tendency of virtue, the habitual tendency of aspiration, the habitual tendency of the perfections, the habitual tendency of definitively realizing the equality of phenomena, and the habitual tendency of diverse conduct. Abiding in those habitual tendencies, and having abandoned the afflictions without remainder, through the great wisdom of the Tathāgata they come to attain the unsurpassed, in which the habitual tendencies are fully understood.

"O son of the Victors, these ten are paths that stray from the Dharma of the bodhisattvas. What are the ten? Despising the spiritual friend; being afraid of saṃsāra; growing weary of the conduct of a bodhisattva; growing weary of dwelling in world systems; making meditative equipoise one's dwelling place; clinging to roots of virtue; rejecting the true Dharma; parting from the conduct of bodhisattvas through calculation; wishing to be a śrāvaka or a pratyekabuddha; and being angry and hostile toward bodhisattvas. These should be completely given up.

"O son of the Victors, these ten are the works of Māra for a bodhisattva: despising those whose minds are disturbed; reviling those of faulty wisdom; being tight-fisted with the Dharma; belittling those who are vessels for the Dharma; teaching the Dharma in connection with material gain; teaching the vast Dharma to those who are not fit vessels; forsaking the spiritual friend; wanting the vehicle of the śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas; being hostile toward bodhisattvas and speaking ill of them; praising oneself and disparaging others; concealing and rejecting profound discourse; delighting in one's own inventions, the various writings one has made up oneself; and attending upon and honouring those who are liberated and have found relief, but not doing so for those who are not liberated and have not found relief."

"Although those great śrāvakas were seated right there in the Jeta Grove, they did not see the pure buddha field and the bodhisattvas who had gathered from every side. It is like this. If some monk, in the midst of a great crowd of people, enters the samādhi pervaded by earth, or enters the samādhi pervaded by water, fire, wind, blue or the like, those many beings do not see the mass of water and so forth, except for those who have entered that samādhi and examine it. In the same way, when the inconceivable magical displays that are the domain of the Buddha's samādhi were revealed, those śrāvakas did not see them or come to know them. The bodhisattvas who had entered the path of the Tathāgata saw them and understood them.

"It is like a monk who has entered the absorption of cessation: although all the worldly dealings of the six senses are present in that place, through the blessing and the power of that very absorption he does not know them or see them. In the same way, those great śrāvakas in the Jeta Grove were examining, and they had their six senses, yet they did not see or come to know the samādhi of the Tathāgata, his magical displays, the arising of the power of the supreme bull among men, and those miracles.

"Like someone who owns everything
yet dies of a sickness of the stomach,
so it is with one who has not cultivated the Dharma.
However many designations of sound there are,
in the sounds and speech of all sentient beings,
though his knowledge of sound comprehends them all,
he has no conceptual thought whatsoever.
Though the buddhas are without concepts,
the Victors appear to have conceptual thought.
Just as a pure seed
will grow in a good field,
so in a pure mind and intention
the Dharma of the Buddha will grow.
The buddhas do not conceptualize.
Although the Teacher has no conceptual thought,
in measureless world systems
the Sage displays conceptual thought.
Those two bodies are inconceivable:
though they appear in world systems,
the body does not become two.
Just as when the moon has risen,
countless reflections
appear in vessels of water,
yet the moon does not become two."

From "Whatever buddha activity the buddhas, the blessed ones, perform through the sense base of the eye, they bring to completion through the sense base of the ear," the same is applied to all the senses.
