When the Buddha passes into nirvāṇa, the passage "all the sentient beings of every world weep and cry out in lament," and so on, appears; this is a skillful means so that they may gain merit.

"O sons of the Victors, among those who have entered the bodhisattva vehicle, I have not seen anything so gravely blameworthy as giving rise to thoughts of ill will toward one another."

"All the world is an aspect of mind; the aspects of the world are the minds of all sentient beings; they fall into the count of two kinds. The Tathāgata does not divide his body, nor does he make his body different, yet he displays bodies in accordance with the intentions and inclinations of the sentient beings belonging to all world systems. In the body of the Tathāgata there is no conceptual thought and no elaboration.

"The Tathāgata has no stinginess with regard to the sphere of reality; yet for sentient beings whose faculties are not fully matured, the sphere of the Tathāgata does not become audible.

"All the sentient beings gathered in the circle of the assembly think, 'The Tathāgata is teaching the Dharma for my sake alone.' The Tathāgata neither sends forth his speech nor draws it back, and yet those activities of the Tathāgata's speech are present and appear.

"It is like this. All the immeasurable masses of water there are in the great ocean have arisen from the special power of the minds of the nāgas. In the same way, all the immeasurable streams of Dharma there are in the ocean of omniscience have arisen from the special power of former intentions and aspirations. The bodies of the sentient beings of the four continents appear in the great ocean like reflections, and for that reason it is called the great ocean."

"These ten are the waves of Māra. The ten are: a dejected mind; a disturbed intention; being content with little scope; not tolerating more than one; not keeping one's fallen aspiration in focus; turning back the afflictions; peace; faith; cutting off the stream of saṃsāra; turning back from the conduct; not bringing sentient beings to maturity; and doubting the Dharma."

In the Teaching on the Magical Display in the Domain of Means within the Sphere of Activity of Bodhisattvas it says: "When one possesses twelve qualities, the unsurpassed awakening mind will arise. What are they? Being naturally inclined toward the vast; having compassion; being without artifice; having served the buddhas; purity of the three kinds of action; having given up evil companions; acting as one has spoken; having a generous disposition; being blessed by the Tathāgata; having no regard for any possessions; relying on a spiritual friend; and relying on one's commitment.

"Seeing twelve good qualities, one should abide in generosity. What are they? It shows the path of the awakening mind; it brings wealth; one is born into the family one wishes; it cuts off miserliness; the stream of giving increases; it shuts the door to the realm of hungry ghosts; one extracts the essence from wealth held in common; one's requisites become complete; one grows accustomed to giving away one's possessions; one comes to have no grasping mind; it accords with the word of the Tathāgata; and the perfection of generosity is brought to completion.

"Seeing twelve, one guards moral discipline: one's blessings become excellent; one enters the path of awakening; one's own bonds are loosened; one is freed from the lower realms; one abandons the path to the lower realms; one's three kinds of action are free of fault; one is not blamed by the wise; one relies on conscientiousness; one gives fearlessness to sentient beings; one obtains the vows of the three doors; one accomplishes the qualities of a buddha; and the perfection of moral discipline is brought to completion.

"The twelve of patience are: accepting that formations are momentary; accepting that no self is to be found; protecting oneself and others; seeing that there is no quarrelsome talk; dispelling the latent tendencies and the entanglements; dispelling attachment and anger; accomplishing the major and minor marks; being born in the world of Brahmā; passing beyond harm from others; attaining the knowledge of exhaustion and of non-arising; passing beyond the domain of Māra; and beholding the Tathāgata.

"Those of diligence are: fully awakening to all phenomena; going into the presence of the Tathāgata; making offerings to the Tathāgata; pleasing the Tathāgata; bringing sentient beings to maturity; establishing those to be trained in the qualities of a buddha; dispelling the unknowing of sentient beings; bringing about wisdom in those to be trained; purifying fields; working without end for the benefit of sentient beings; going to all fields in a single instant; and manifest awakening.

"Those of meditative concentration are: being unperturbed and at peace; not running after objects; the mind remaining settled; passing beyond the three realms; outshining worldly meditative attainments; becoming unattached to them; turning back the concentrations; being free of the perception of things; aspiring to a samādhi that has measure; being utterly at peace; being well trained; and restraining the senses.

"Those of wisdom are: dispelling delusion; dispelling the afflictions; being free of unknowing; uprooting ignorance; destroying craving; wisdom like a vajra; wisdom like the sun; wisdom like fire; wisdom like a jewel; signlessness; wishlessness; and emptiness."

"Suppose every sentient being possessed the merit of a universal monarch: a hundred times that merit accomplishes a single pore of a buddha's skin. All the merit of the pores multiplied a hundredfold accomplishes one minor mark; from that, a major mark; from that, the uṣṇīṣa; and from that, the svastika of the Dharma. Through it, even if all sentient beings were to ask questions, he would answer them with a single utterance."

"After I have passed into nirvāṇa, King Ajātaśatru will take one eighth of my relics, place them in a jewelled casket, and, outside the city, dig into the earth and deposit them there, to be kept until King Aśoka appears. King Aśoka will appear a hundred years after I have passed into nirvāṇa. Then my relics will be brought out, and after worshipping them for seven days he will build eighty-four thousand stūpas."

"Whoever listens to this discourse on the Dharma, reads it, and so forth, should be known as one who sees me, this pleasure grove, this teaching of the Dharma, and this assembled retinue as well."

In the Conformity with the World it says:
"Though they have no manifold bodies,
in keeping with the inclinations of sentient beings
they make emanated bodies, and so on:
this is conformity with the world.
Though Māra with a hundred koṭis of hosts
could not split the Saṅgha,
they display a schism in the Saṅgha:
this is conformity with the world."

In The Transference of Existence it says: "The ceasing of the last consciousness is called death and transference. The arising of the first consciousness is called birth. The eye does not see forms, and the mind does not know phenomena: that is the supreme truth, and for it the world is not needed."

In Maitreya's Entry it says: "Guarding one's wealth on every side and not sharing it out is a fault in a bodhisattva. Even though a bodhisattva has trained in the training of the Tathāgata exactly as it was laid down, if he joins it with the mind of a śrāvaka, the mind of a pratyekabuddha, or an objectifying view, he falls away from the moral discipline of a bodhisattva. Being unable to accept emptiness is a fault in a bodhisattva. Thinking, 'To this one I will give a gift; to that one I will not,' is a fault in a bodhisattva. To make distinctions, 'To this one I will teach the Dharma; this one has corrupt moral discipline; that one does not,' and to despise the one of corrupt discipline, is a fault in a bodhisattva."

"What is conscientiousness? Not giving up the training; not breaking one's pledges; loving-kindness toward all sentient beings; not abandoning sentient beings; a firm aspiration for awakening; not giving up diligence; relying on the spiritual friend and serving him; reverence for the Dharma; never being sated with much learning; sharing the Dharma with others; teaching the Dharma without material reward; not giving up the ascetic qualities and having few requisites; contentment; crushing pride; listening to the Dharma with an attentive ear; reverence for the teacher; abiding in patience; casting off the barb of malice; inclination toward the profound Dharma; giving up objectifying views; a strong aspiration for inner composure; disliking idle chatter; dwelling in solitude of body and mind; dwelling in the wilderness; a pliant mind; not holding moral discipline to be supreme; not praising oneself; not disparaging others; delighting in the ways of an ascetic; speaking openly; and having no angry frown on one's face. Moreover, not attending to the mind of a śrāvaka or to an objectifying mind, even in a dream, is conscientiousness."

In the Teaching on the Great Compassion of the Tathāgata it says: "With the divine eye of the Tathāgata he sees the death, transference and birth of sentient beings. Because he discerns each of them correctly, just as it is, among immeasurable objects, he sees that some who focus on what is afflicted are nonetheless placed in purification, and that some, grasping with overweening pride, are placed as focusing on what is afflicted even with regard to purification. The wisdom of the Tathāgata engages these through the permutations of the cross-classification.

"The cause of what is afflicted is improper attention, and its condition is ignorance. Ignorance is the cause, and formations are the condition; apply this in the same way to the rest. The causes and conditions for becoming purified are two: a concordant voice from another, and one's own proper attention. Again there are two: one-pointedness in calm abiding, and skill in insight.

"As for his knowledge of the paths that lead everywhere: he knows even application through bodily action that is not through speech; apply this in the same way to the rest. Again, desire may arise from attractive objects, it may arise from attachment, and it may arise from attachment to self. Aversion may have its cause in anger, it may arise from an intention not fully realized, and it may arise from habitual tendencies. Delusion may arise from the cause of ignorance, it may arise from the cause of the view of the perishing aggregates, and it may arise from the cause of doubt. There are persons of inferior intention whose application is vast; apply this in the same way to the rest.

"Toward sentient beings who are not fit vessels
the Buddha remains in equanimity;
the time at which one will be freed,
that very time he fully attends to.

"That through corrupt moral discipline one should be born a god or a human; that through malice one should become beautiful; that through laziness one should attain realization; that through distraction one should attain certainty; that with an objectifying mind one should obtain conforming patience; that in a woman's body one should become a universal monarch, Śakra or Brahmā, or appear in the world as a buddha; that the beings of Northern Kuru should fall into the lower realms; that one should know the movements of the Tathāgata's mind without realizing the Tathāgata's blessing; or that one should rest in equipoise upon the Tathāgata: for these there is no possibility.

"It may even be possible
to make space into the form of a person of five colours,
but this that the Buddha has declared impossible
can never become possible.

"The inner is peace; the outer is pacification. The four outflows are desire, existence, ignorance and views. The four appropriations are desire, views, the doctrine of a self, and holding moral discipline and ascetic observances to be supreme.

"Long ago, in the eon Supreme Fragrance, in the world system Fragrant, the Tathāgata Sandalwood House appeared in the world. When he was about to pass into nirvāṇa, he surveyed the world to see whom he should tame, and he saw a sentient being at the Peak of Existence who had produced roots of virtue and was inclined toward the vast, a single disciple to be tamed by a buddha. He saw that this being would remain there for eighty-four thousand eons and then be born as a human; that, having died and transmigrated and been born as a human, he would not indulge in sensual pleasures; and that if he heard the Great Vehicle, he would become irreversible from the great awakening.

"Then that buddha, out of compassion and skill in means, told the monks, 'Today I shall pass into complete nirvāṇa,' and displayed the great nirvāṇa. His relics also spread far and wide, and the true Dharma remained for eighty-four thousand years. By the power of samādhi, that Blessed One remained invisible for eighty-four eons. Then the being was born as a human, and when he was eight years old the Buddha went to him in the colour and form of a buddha, taught the Dharma to the boy, and gave him a prophecy. At that time no human being other than that boy saw that Tathāgata. Then he again displayed his nirvāṇa, and the gods worshipped his relics.

"Just as a single sun or moon dispels all darkness, so a single root of virtue endowed with insight dispels all the darkness of unknowing. The ornaments of a bodhisattva are four: moral discipline, samādhi, wisdom and dhāraṇī. Moral discipline is not harming any sentient being. Samādhi is loving-kindness. Wisdom is freedom from doubt. Dhāraṇī is questions beyond expression.

"The ground lies in an expanse of white beryl.

"The Buddha is always in meditative equipoise;
his samādhi never declines;
he is equally endowed with all phenomena.
Not in earth, water or fire,
nor in wind, the desire realm or the formless,
does the Victor abide; it is not a meditative concentration.

"Higher moral discipline is not seeing the mind of omniscience and giving up all deliberate formation. Higher mind is abiding firmly in samādhi and thereby gaining wisdom. Higher wisdom is abiding in the Perfection of Wisdom and thereby attaining the analytical knowledges. Listening to Dharma one has not heard is the root of wisdom. Teaching to others the Dharma one has heard is the work of wisdom."

In it the Dharma of the Buddha and the explanation of the letters appear in full.

In the Proclamation of Ambrosia it says: "A bodhisattva who possesses five qualities is not overcome by Māra, lives long, and swiftly attains buddhahood. The five are: always giving the Dharma to sentient beings; giving fearlessness to sentient beings; cultivating the four immeasurables; restoring old stūpas; and inspiring all sentient beings toward the awakening mind."

In the Questions of Mañjuśrī it says: "It is by two causes that the form body of the Tathāgata is supremely exalted: by the power of the Tathāgata's aspirations, and by the power of the full ripening of the virtue of the sentient beings to be trained. Just as with his body, so it is with his speech, his marks, his teaching of the Dharma and the rest; sentient beings understand them in that way."

In the Eight Maṇḍalas it says: "With that, mixing cow dung with earth, one should make a square maṇḍala, smooth and even. In common with all sentient beings, to whatever buddhas there were in the past, and those who are to come and who are present now, and whatever bodhisattvas, śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas there are, to all of them one dedicates it. Then all around the maṇḍala one should make the maṇḍalas of the retinue: Lokeśvara and Maitreya, Ākāśagarbha, sage and supreme being, Samantabhadra the wise, the Lord of Secrets (Vajrapāṇi), Mañjuśrī, who had become a youth, Sarvanīvaraṇaviṣkambhin, and likewise Kṣitigarbha: for their sake one should make eight maṇḍalas." If one does this, one obtains four benefits: in all one's lifetimes one is never parted from a mind endowed with the Dharma; one has a large retinue; one has excellent requisites; and one's body is firm and stable.

In the Dharmaskandha it says: "When eunuchs or those of neuter sex see a lovely woman and desire arises in them, most of them are struck by illness and their organ becomes rigid; this happens because their bodies are full of semen. Few eunuchs and neuters go to the higher realms; most of them go to hell. Those among the four assemblies who do not know the drawbacks of desire and impurity are tormented by lust, and so they are of poor complexion and unattractive. Those who do know them are the opposite.

"In women, unlike in men, there are five kinds of worms. They dwell in the woman's urinary passage. Of each kind there are eighty worms, and having sharp mouths with two lips they constantly feed on the woman's body. Therefore, whatever such women engage in, they never experience satisfaction in desire.

"The drawbacks of corrupt moral discipline are ten: torment at the time of death; coming to regret; fear of going to Avīci; casting disrepute on the law; being abandoned by the buddhas; being abandoned by the śrāvakas; being full of fear at the time of death; going to the lower realms; finding it hard to obtain a human body; and being abandoned by the gods.

"Inside women's noses there are swarms of worms called Complete Mouth. Because they feed on women's facial hair, women's facial hair does not grow. In women there is a swarm of worms called Abarophen, which feeds on the woman's mouth, and for this reason their necks are short. Whatever is put into a woman's mouth is first eaten by them, and they leave the residue near the breasts; for this reason the breasts hang down. When a woman is pregnant, those worms too become pregnant; for this reason breast milk is abundant. Because women cannot bear desire, additional points of training were laid down."

In the Casket of No Letters it says: "There is one thing a bodhisattva should always guard with diligence. What is it? Not doing to others what one does not want for oneself. Whoever listens to this discourse on the Dharma will, at the time of death, see me myself, together with these very bodhisattvas, dwelling on Vulture Peak."

In the Teaching on the Factors of Awakening it says: "Whoever sees all formations as unborn knows suffering. Seeing all phenomena as without arising, he has abandoned its origin. Seeing all phenomena as utterly passed into nirvāṇa, he has made cessation directly manifest. Seeing all phenomena as without entity, he has cultivated the path."

In the noble Sūtra of the Vast Wisdom of the Blessed One, the Jewel Brought to Perfection, it says: "It is impossible that in that very body of a woman one should become Śakra, Brahmā, a universal monarch, or a tathāgata, or that two tathāgatas should appear in a single world system."

"The quarter, the sixth, the eighth part, the sixteenth part, the triangular, the feminine sound, the feminine voice, the feminine demeanour, the feminine manner, and conduct in every respect: these are called the female faculty. The male faculty is the same."

"The lifespan of the beings of Northern Kuru lasts a full thousand years, and there is also untimely death." It appears that the six classes of gods of the desire realm, the seventeen abodes of the form realm, and the four formless realms also have untimely death. It appears that in the hell realms too there is death partway through.

It appears that at death, whichever of the five paths of rebirth one sees the sign of, one is born there. It appears that when the monks are called to a meal of barley flour, tally sticks are distributed. It appears that Maudgalyāyana, in order to finish Śāriputra's outer robe quickly so that he could go into the presence of the Blessed One, sewed it with five needles using his five fingers.

"A bodhisattva whose nature is to ask questions thoroughly will swiftly and fully awaken to unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening."

"By giving rise to pride one comes to meet with contempt;
by giving up pride one comes to receive honour."

"One who wishes to awaken fully to unsurpassed awakening should give up pride; should establish toward all sentient beings the attitude that they are worthy of offerings; should not give up the awakening mind; should please the spiritual friend; should be insatiable in seeking the Dharma; should strive for much learning; should also seek out treatises that have been thoroughly examined; and should also train in understanding the various philosophical systems. One should seek treatises that are meaningful, and give up meaningless treatises, namely the Lokāyata, treatises of sorcery, treatises on children's play, treatises on gambling, on trade, and on sexual intercourse, the treatises of the Vedas, and so on: everything that is incompatible with liberation should be given up.

"A bodhisattva should not associate with followers of the vehicles of the śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas. Why is that? Those of corrupt moral discipline and those of wrong view are not a bodhisattva's harmful friends; the followers of the vehicles of the śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas are. Corrupt moral discipline and wrong view cannot destroy the moral discipline of a bodhisattva, but the followers of the vehicles of the śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas can make a bodhisattva enter into their undefiled wisdom."

"Formerly this Jambudvīpa measured forty thousand."

"A bodhisattva should become skilled in all treatises. Why is that? Because the starting point of omniscience is this: skill in treatises."

"Through attachment one is established in the five destinies; through aversion, in three; through delusion, in five. Why is that? Because aversion is the ground of the lower realms alone. From an action, the affliction is inferred: from a past affliction this action was formed. From the affliction, the mind is inferred: from the affliction at work, a mind of attachment, aversion or delusion is inferred. From the mind, the arising of the person is inferred. The Tathāgata did this with the destinies of those beings in mind: because this person formed this action, this being goes on to that destiny."

"Formerly, when the Blessed One Śākyamuni was the brahmin called Moon Brow, the Buddha Teacher of the Three Worlds prophesied that in the future, after sixty koṭis of eons, he would become the buddha called Beauty Illuminating the World, and that his lifespan would be eighty koṭis of years."

"Formerly, in the presence of the Buddha Unripened Fame, when the Blessed One Śākyamuni was called Supreme in Bodhisattva Conduct, he made this vow: 'As long as the sentient beings in that buddha field of mine have not passed into nirvāṇa by the easy path of swift higher knowledge, as long as there is birth in the three lower realms, as long as there is the sound of suffering, and as long as all sentient beings have not reached their final existence, for that long I will not fully awaken.'"

"A bodhisattva should train in all the conventions of persons, of towns and of countries, and in all worldly knowledge."

"Formerly, when the Buddha Śākyamuni was the teacher called Good Eyes, he thought: 'Beings are harmed by the faults of the desire realm, and the other two realms are the same, so sentient beings have no refuge. I shall not abide in any formation at all, for where there is no abiding, there is no wavering.' He entered the meditative concentration of not abiding in any phenomenon, and gained a mind of compassion for sentient beings. Through his teaching of that Dharma, koṭis of niyutas of sentient beings passed into the nirvāṇa that does not abide."

"A bodhisattva who has undertaken diligence is able to conquer the five māras. What are the five? The māra of the afflictions, of karma, of the aggregates, of the Lord of Death, and of the son of the gods."

"Through the power of mind all phenomena arise; through the power of mind all phenomena cease. However the mind conceives, in just that way it displays the reflections of all phenomena."

In the Sūtra on the Measure of Life it says: "The lifespan of the people of Northern Kuru is a thousand years, and there is no death partway through."

In the Prophecy of Bhadra the Illusionist it says: "The paths that lead to the seat of awakening are four: never giving up the mind of omniscience; being insatiable in seeking all roots of virtue; and striving to accomplish the true Dharma.

"These four are beyond the measure of śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas: the turning back of meditative concentration; the discernment of minds; great compassion for sentient beings; and eloquence together with many languages.

"Seeing four benefits, one seeks learning: one brings oneself and others to knowledge; one cuts through doubts that come from the four directions; one upholds the true Dharma; and the praises of the Buddha become inexhaustible. Having heard, one knows how to distinguish phenomena; having heard, one gives up busy activity; having heard, one teaches others; and having heard, one dedicates it to awakening.

"Through these four, the words of a Dharma teacher become worthy of acceptance: having few desires; being content; having a good and gentle disposition; and practising one's own Dharma.

"One who possesses four qualities will meet the buddhas: one-pointed mindfulness; reciting the praises of the buddhas again and again; pure moral discipline; and making aspirations with superior intention.

"Through four qualities one gains eloquence: not contradicting what other Dharma teachers explain; respect for Dharma teachers; listening to the Dharma respectfully; not being puffed up by one's learning; and not looking down on those of little learning.

"One who possesses four qualities will see his aspirations fulfilled: moral discipline and purity of training; purifying the obscurations of karma; being without deceit and guile; and setting one's mind on producing roots of virtue."

In the Vinayavastu it says: "There are five things one never tires of looking at: the ocean, a noble bull elephant, Mount Meru, a universal monarch, and a buddha. A nāga remains in its natural form in five situations: at birth, at death, while eating, while asleep, and while having intercourse. Some women, being naturally wise, know which man is attracted to them, the time and the coming of their menses, that they have conceived, from whom the child entered the womb, and whether the child is a boy or a girl: a boy is on the right and a girl on the left."

"The neuter by nature is one who from the very beginning is not even a woman. The neuter by half-month is a woman for half of the month and a man for the other half. The neuter through attachment is one whose organ appears when attached to another. The neuter through jealousy is one whose organ changes on seeing another engaged in wrongful conduct. The neuter through impairment is one whose organ has been impaired by illness or cut off by a weapon."

In the Aṅgulimālīya it says: "The meaning of the statement 'Whatever is taught for faith in the Śrāvaka Vehicle is also buddha-nature' is this: what is called the mind that is pure by nature is buddha-nature. It is the chief of all phenomena; the import of all phenomena is the nature of buddha-nature. It is like this: just as one king, on hearing that another king has made laws, also governs his own subjects in the same way, so the assembly of the land of Kashmir and the people of the land of Bharukaccha, giving rise to shame and pride, and fearing and dreading the Great Vehicle, will teach it in secret and will not come to teach buddha-nature."

Long ago, countless eons in the past, when the Buddha Kosanta the Excellent appeared near Śrāvastī and the true Dharma was on the point of disappearing, someone gave a fine robe to the monk Buddhamati, who lived in the wilderness. One night someone else stole the robe, drove him out naked and tied him to a tree. Then a brahmin who gathered flowers saw this and thought, "Alas, look: the ascetic is not liberated by his saffron robe!" Saying, "If one goes naked and practises austerities, see how much better than saffron that is," he went naked and wandered about; and from that the naked ascetics arose.

Then the monk loosened his bonds, smeared his body with yellow earth and went on. He entered the water and washed the wound on his head, and covered his head with soft grass. He covered the wounds on his body with soft hair. He gathered cow dung and lit a fire, thinking that its warmth would reach his wounds. A brahmin, a woodcutter and some other destitute people saw this, and thinking as the first had, the strange ones arose: the wandering ascetics, the ascetics who wear garments of grass, the Paśupatas, those with balls of cow dung, and those who go about in mourning. That was the beginning: all ninety-six schools of strange ones arose from the deportment of that monk.

In the noble Questions of Śaṅkara it says: "Sons of good family, whoever does not accept and does not believe that all phenomena are like emanations in this way, but turns against it and reviles it, is called a non-ascetic, is called a tīrthika, is called an objectifier, is called a troublemaker, is called deceitful, is called corrupt in discipline.

"Deluded people overwhelmed by anger
are far from the teaching of the one wise in the world;
that is the inner fixation of wrong views.
Therefore the wise should not quarrel."

In the noble Questions of Mañjuśrī it says: "Mañjuśrī, it is by two causes that the form body of the Tathāgata is supremely exalted. What are the two causes? They are the power of the former aspirations of the Tathāgata and the power of the full ripening of the virtue of the sentient beings to be trained. Mañjuśrī, it is by these two causes that the form body of the Tathāgata is supremely exalted. Just as with his body, so it is with his speech, his marks, his teaching of the Dharma and the rest; sentient beings understand them in that way."

In the noble Dharmaskandha it says: "These ten are the drawbacks of corrupt moral discipline: one is tormented at the time of death; one comes to regret; one fears going to Avīci; one casts disrepute on those who keep moral discipline; one is abandoned by the buddhas; one is abandoned by the śrāvakas; one is abandoned by the gods; one is afraid at the time of death; one goes to the lower realms; and one finds it hard to obtain a human body."

In the noble Jewel's End it says: "In the dharmadhātu one cannot designate, 'These are the qualities of ordinary beings; these are the qualities of the noble ones.' In the dharmadhātu one cannot designate, 'This is here; it is in such and such a place.' Why is that? Because the dharmadhātu cannot be pointed out, cannot be designated, and has passed entirely beyond designation."

In the Extensive Account of the Former Aspirations of the Blessed One Bhaiṣajyaguru, Beryl Light, it says: "There are nine kinds of untimely death. What are they? A sick person lacking medicine and care, or taking what is not medicine; punishment by the king; having one's vitality stolen by nonhumans through great heedlessness; fire; water; lions, tigers, foxes, snakes and the like; falling into an abyss; the workings of poison, sorcery and vetālas; and hunger and thirst. These are given in brief; besides these there are countless, immeasurable others."

In the noble sūtra called Avalokiteśvara it says: "Just as the Tathāgata is infinite in all the qualities of a buddha, so too offerings made to the Tathāgata are immeasurable and infinite. In the time to come, in the last five hundred years, whoever makes offerings to and honours a relic of the Tathāgata even the size of a mustard seed will gain five benefits: he will be endowed with mindfulness; he will be endowed with realization; he will have shame and conscience; he will soon see a buddha; and, having seen him, faith will arise. He will also come to possess four qualities. What are the four? He will uphold the teaching of the Tathāgata; he will practise it just as it was taught; having practised it, he will come close to the buddhas; and he will soon be born in a completely pure buddha field." In this Dharma text the particular benefits of each act, up to offering ornaments to a stūpa, are set out in detail.

In the noble Like Golden Sand it says: "The venerable Ānanda said this to the Blessed One: 'Blessed One, how many world systems are there in which no buddha appears and in which bodhisattvas do not practise the conduct of awakening?' He declared: 'The appearance of a buddha and the practice of the conduct of a bodhisattva are as rare as golden sand among the sands of the great ocean and the sands of the Ganges. Just as when someone takes a single grain of sand from the sands of the ocean and the sands of the Ganges, so they are few.'"

In the noble sūtra called the Seal of the Dharma it says: "The venerable Śāradvatīputra (Śāriputra) said this to the Blessed One:

'Blessed One, to what extent is one called a person who has gone forth in the well-spoken Dharma and Vinaya?' He declared: 'Śāradvatīputra, when one has neither passed beyond worldly qualities nor attained the undefiled qualities, to that extent there is going forth and supramundane full ordination.'"

In the noble Accomplishment of the Four it says: "These four are the difficult deeds of a bodhisattva. What are they? Being patient with sentient beings of little strength; giving all one's possessions to the poor; regarding as a friend, without any anger, one who begs for one's head, the foremost of the limbs; and, by not conceptualizing birth, taking birth intentionally.

"The paired qualities of a bodhisattva are four. What are they? Intention paired with application; giving paired with dedication; loving-kindness paired with compassion; and skillful means paired with wisdom."

The noble Akṣayamati said: "What is the meaning of 'perfection'? The meaning of bringing the special features of conduct to completion is the meaning of perfection. So too is the meaning of bringing profound wisdom to completion; the meaning of linking the conditioned and the unconditioned as a path; the meaning of realizing the faults of saṃsāra just as they are; the meaning of bringing unskilled people to realization; the meaning of knowing the realms of sentient beings; the meaning of attaining the irreversible ground; the meaning of purifying buddha fields; and the meaning of perfecting all good qualities."

In the noble Accomplishment of the Infinite Gateways it says:
"Dwell in the wilderness that the Victors have praised.
Dwelling there, do not look down on others.
Arousing aspiration for the acceptance of the profound,
dwell in conscientiousness as though your head were on fire.
Since these are the grounds of hypocrisy,
do not dwell on renown, profit and gain.
Like a bird, be content with mere scraps,
and make your human birth bear fruit."

In the noble Teaching on the Conduct of Bodhisattvas it says: "Mañjuśrī said to the Blessed One: 'Blessed One, look: those monks were harmed by hearing this Dharma.' He declared: 'Do not say that! If they had not heard this, they would not be born in a good destiny even for an eon, let alone please the buddhas. Because they heard those discourses on the Dharma, even with doubt, they have only just now been freed from hell and born in Tuṣita.'"

In the noble Questions of Śrīvasu it says: "A beginner bodhisattva should give gifts from time to time, according to their wishes and as far as he is able, even to parents who do him harm, and to children, wife, male and female servants, workers, hired labourers, friends, ministers, relatives and elders who do him harm."

In the noble Questions of the Old Woman it says: "All phenomena are empty of essential nature, and yet phenomena are designated. The wise do not cling to them, and because they do not cling they do not dispute. Not disputing is the supreme quality of a monk."

Ānanda asked: "Why has she become so poor?" He declared: "Ānanda, when I was practising the conduct of a bodhisattva, she was my mother. She would not let me go forth in the teaching of the Tathāgata Krakucchanda, and so I went without food for a day; then she let me go as I wished. As the ripening of that, she has become like this."

In the noble Entering Nonconceptuality it says:
"In this true Dharma, the heirs of the Victor,
having contemplated nonconceptuality,
pass entirely beyond conceptuality, so hard to cross,
and gradually attain the nonconceptual."

In the noble Conformity with the World it says:
"The wisdom of the buddhas is immeasurable;
though it arises from the dharmadhātu,
they display buddha fields:
this is conformity with the world.
Though without destruction and without birth,
and equal to the dharmadhātu,
they display the eon of burning:
this is conformity with the world."

In the noble Analysis of the Dharma and Its Meaning it says: "Any woman who keeps or reads this discourse on the Dharma will change her woman's body and be born in the world system Sukhāvatī; that will be her last woman's body. At the time of her death, too, she will see the Tathāgata Amitābha."

In the noble Transference of Existence it says: "'Blessed One, how is it that an action that was done and accumulated, and ceased long ago, is close at hand at the time of death and becomes manifest to the mind? Since all formations are empty, how is it that actions are not lost?' When he had asked this, the Blessed One said this to Śreṇya Bimbisāra, king of the land of Magadha:

"'Great king, it is like this. Suppose a man, while asleep, dreams that he is enjoying himself with a lovely woman of the land, and, waking from sleep, he remembers that lovely woman of the land. Great king, what do you think: does that lovely woman of the land in the dream exist?'

"He said: 'Blessed One, she does not.'

"The Blessed One said: 'Great king, what do you think: is the man who clings to the lovely woman in the dream of a wise disposition?'

"He said: 'Blessed One, he is not. Why? Blessed One, since the lovely woman of the land in the dream does not exist at all and is not to be found, how could there be any enjoyment with her? So that man will be one whose lot is deprivation and weariness.'

"The Blessed One said: 'Great king, in the same way, when a childish ordinary person without learning sees forms with the eye, he clings to the forms that please the mind. Having clung to them, he becomes attached. Having become attached, he becomes thoroughly attached. Having become thoroughly attached, he forms with body, speech and mind actions born of desire, born of aversion and born of delusion. That action, once formed, ceases. When it ceases, it does not remain resting in the east, nor in the south, nor in the west, nor in the north, nor above, nor below; it does not remain resting in the intermediate directions.

"At another time, at the time of death, when the end is close at hand and the karma of similar lot is exhausted, at the moment the last consciousness ceases, that very action becomes manifest to the mind, just as the lovely woman of the land did to the man who woke from sleep. Great king, when in this way the last consciousness ceases, the first consciousness belonging to the phase of birth arises either among the gods, or among humans, or among the asuras, or among the hell beings, or among the animals, or among the hungry ghosts. Great king, immediately after that first consciousness has ceased, a mental continuum of similar lot manifests in which the ripening will be experienced.

"Great king, in this, no phenomenon whatsoever passes from this world to the next, and yet death, transference and birth do manifest. Great king, in this, the last consciousness that ceases is called death and transference, and the first consciousness that arises is called birth. Great king, when the last consciousness ceases, it goes nowhere at all; when the first consciousness arises, it comes from nowhere at all. Why is that? Because they are devoid of essential nature. Great king, in this, the last consciousness is empty of being last; death and transference are empty of death and transference; action is empty of action; the first consciousness is empty of being first; birth is empty of birth; and yet it is evident that actions are not wasted. Great king, immediately after the first consciousness belonging to the phase of birth has ceased, without interruption, the mental continuum in which the ripening will be experienced becomes manifest.'"

In the noble Discourse on the Dharma of Wonders it says: "Ānanda, suppose there is a great trichiliocosm, and some son or daughter of good family turns it into the substance of the seven precious things and offers it to the noble ones, from stream-enterers up to pratyekabuddhas, and to the Saṅgha of the four directions. Compared with that, if someone builds, after the manner of the stūpa of a tathāgata who has passed into nirvāṇa, a stūpa the size of an emblic myrobalan fruit, sets up in it a central pole the size of a needle, raises a parasol the size of a juniper leaf, makes an image the size of a barley grain, and places inside it a relic the size of a mustard seed, I say that this brings far greater merit than that."

In the noble Questions of the Brahmin Woman Śrī it says: "Glorious lady, ignorance arises in the aspect of what is not correct; it does not arise in the aspect of what is correct. Ignorance arises as error, but the ripening of error does not arise."

In the Perfection of Wisdom in Fifty it says: "He declared: 'Subhūti, one who wishes to train in the grounds of the śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas should listen to this Perfection of Wisdom, take it up, keep it, read it and master it. Why is that? Because the perfections and all the factors of awakening are gathered in it.'"

In the Perfection of Wisdom for Kauśika it says: "He declared: 'Kauśika, this is the meaning of the Perfection of Wisdom: the Perfection of Wisdom should not be viewed as two, nor as not two; not as a sign, nor as signless; not as something to take up, nor as something to discard; not as something added to, nor as something not added to; not as afflicted, nor as free of affliction'; and so on, up to 'not as the limit of reality, nor as not the limit of reality.'

"Like stars, a blur of the eyes, a lamp,
an illusion, dew, a bubble,
a dream, lightning and a cloud:
so should one view all that is conditioned."

namo bhagavate prajñāparamitaye oṃ dhī śī śruti smṛti gati vijaye svāhā

"Without ceasing, without arising,
without permanence and without annihilation,
not different in meaning, not one in meaning,
without coming and without going,
peace, through the stilling of elaboration:
to the one who taught dependent origination,
the perfect Buddha,
the best of speakers, I pay homage."

In the noble Teaching of the Four Qualities it says: "One who possesses four qualities overcomes the evil one has done and accumulated. The power of repudiation is great remorse whenever one engages in non-virtue. The power of applying the antidote is striving in virtue after one has done non-virtue. The power of restoration is taking a vow and not doing it again from then on. The power of the support is going for refuge to the Three Jewels and not giving up the awakening mind."

In the noble Dependent Origination it says: "Of those phenomena that arise from causes, the Tathāgata has declared the cause, and also what their cessation is. So spoke the great ascetic." If someone builds a stūpa in an uninhabited place and places this essence inside it, then on dying and passing on he will be born in the world of Brahmā, and on dying and passing on from there he will be born in the Pure Abodes.

In the noble Four Qualities it says: "There are four things a bodhisattva does not give up as long as he lives, even for the sake of his life: the awakening mind, the spiritual friend, patience, certainty, and dwelling in the wilderness."

To the noble Maitreya he declared: "Maitreya, compared with filling as many great trichiliocosms as there are grains of sand in the Ganges with the seven precious things and offering them to the Tathāgata, if someone out of compassion places a single verse in the mind of another, the former does not come close to even a fraction of this merit."

In the noble Questions of the Nāga King Sāgara it says: "If one reads this sūtra, one will have read the eighty-four thousand collections of the Dharma."

In the noble Sūtra of the Four Qualities it says: "There are four things a monk should not trust as long as he lives: women, the retinue of the royal palace, his own good looks, and the wealth and possessions of the powerful."

In the noble Intelligence of the Victor it says: "One who wants merit should make offerings to the Tathāgata. One who wants wisdom should strive at listening. One who wants wealth should increase his giving. One who wants beauty should cultivate patience. One who wants eloquence should show reverence to the teacher. One who wants dhāraṇī should be free of conceit. One who wants happiness should give up evil. One who wants to bring happiness to sentient beings should give rise to the mind set on awakening. One who wants a pleasing voice should speak the truth.

One who wants good qualities should rely on a spiritual friend. One who wants calm abiding should be free of bustle. One who wants to see with insight should examine phenomena as empty. One who wants the world of Brahmā should cultivate the immeasurables. One who wants the excellences of gods and humans should practise the ten virtues. One who wants to pass into nirvāṇa should strive at listening. One who wants every good quality should make offerings to the Three Jewels."

In the noble Teaching on the Benefits of Giving it says: "In order to give up miserliness, one gives with faith. One gives at the proper time. One gives what is allowable, as permitted by the Tathāgata, completely pure in its three aspects. In order to give up entirely all the faults of a disturbed mind, one gives with respect. In order to extract the essence from this body that has no essence, one gives with one's own hand." In this Dharma text the ripening of each kind of gift is set forth definitively and at length.

In the noble Sūtra on Impermanence it says:
"Health is impermanent, youth does not last,
wealth is impermanent, and life too does not last.
When people are beset by impermanence itself,
how can delight in the pleasures of the senses arise?"

In the noble Questions of the Wanderer Dīrghanakha it says: "The wanderer Dīrghanakha said to the Blessed One: 'As long as I live, I go for refuge to the Buddha, the Dharma and the Saṅgha. As long as I live, I shall observe the eight-branched fast. Killing any living being, stealing the wealth of others, unchaste conduct, lying of whatever kind, intoxicating drink, and eating at the wrong time, garlands, cosmetics, song and dance, amusements, and high beds and seats: from today I give these up, and with the eight qualities I shall act after the manner of the arhats.' Likewise a second and a third time. 'May this vow of the discipline of the poṣadha that I have properly taken up become an ornament of the mind, a requisite of the mind, and the accumulation for awakening; and may I come to abide in the supreme aim, the wisdom of omniscience, and in the unsurpassed bliss of nirvāṇa!' He declared: 'Great brahmin, it is excellent, excellent that you do so.'"

In the noble Questions of Siṃha it says:
"If in all one's births the awakening mind
is not given up even in a dream,
what need to speak of when one is not asleep?
By what does this come about?
In villages and in towns,
in whatever lands one dwells,
one establishes people properly in awakening;
by that, the mind will not be given up.
If here one gives the five essentials
and gives fearlessness to the frightened,
wherever one may be born,
by that one's strength will become great;
one will always be exalted in might,
and not even koṭis of māras can overcome one."

In the noble Teaching of Mañjuśrī it says: "'Mañjuśrī, with what in view should one make offerings to the Tathāgata?' 'Son of the gods, there are four things to keep in view. What are they? One makes offerings with a view to the awakening mind, to liberating all sentient beings, to not cutting off the lineage of the Jewels, and to purifying buddha fields.'"

In the noble Vajra Essence Dhāraṇī it says: "He declared: 'Mañjuśrī, what do you think: do the hells arise from one's own conceptions, or do they arise from an essential nature?' Mañjuśrī said: 'Blessed One, childish ordinary people, through their own conceptions, perceive the hells, the animals and the world of Yama. Because they superimpose these, which do not exist, they feel harm and experience suffering in the three lower realms. Blessed One, just as I do not see the hells, so too I do not see beings in the hells, and there is no suffering of the hells either. Blessed One, it is like this. Some man, asleep, dreams and thinks, "I have fallen into hell," and he perceives himself thrown into a fiercely blazing iron cauldron. There he experiences feelings of suffering that are intense, unbearable, hard to endure and unpleasant. There he experiences torment of mind. There he becomes frightened, terrified, utterly terrified'; and so on, up to: 'He comes to remember, "I had fallen asleep, and I imagined this unreal thing," and he regains his peace of mind.'"

In the noble Teaching of the Inconceivable Light it says: "One who possesses four qualities swiftly attains acceptance and fully awakens to unsurpassed awakening. The four are: seeing the Buddha as the nature of reality, not as form; seeing the Dharma as freedom from desire, not as letters and definitions; seeing the Saṅgha as unconditioned, not as a group; and the eye of wisdom becoming completely pure."

In the noble Ten Buddhas it says that there are ten buddhas by holding whose names one swiftly attains awakening. In the east, in the world system All Qualities Completely Pure, is the Buddha Qualities Beyond Immeasurable Eons. In the south, in the world system Array of Precious Qualities, is the Buddha Eon of the Mass of Glory and Splendour of the Array of Qualities. In the west, in the world system Free of All Darkness through Yoga, is the Buddha Glory of the Array of Eloquence in All Phenomena. In the north, in the world system Free of Darkness and Dust, is the Buddha Perfect Practice of Immeasurable Eloquence. In the southeast, in the world system Array of Long-Lasting Happiness, is the Buddha Praise of the Melody of the Thunder of Clouds. In the southwest, in the world system Array of Immeasurable Qualities, is the Buddha Source of the Glorious Splendour of the Supreme Light of the Sun. In the northwest, in the world system Free of the Lower Realms, is the Buddha Source of Glory, King of Splendour of Many Kinds. In the northeast, in the world system Free of All the Harm of Sorrow, is the Buddha Who Appears Everywhere for Countless Koṭis of Eons. Above, in the world system Array of Immeasurable Splendour, is the Buddha Source of the Glorious Splendour That Proclaims Virtue in a Golden Sky. Below, in the world system Gathering of All Dust, is the Buddha Source of the Splendour of the Magical Display of the Ways of All Phenomena.

In the Application of Mindfulness of the True Dharma it says: "Just as the current of a river flows on constantly without interruption, so it is for those persons even when they are asleep, intoxicated or deeply intoxicated." Thus non-revealing form is held to be indemonstrable and unobstructed.

What is that form like? It is simply that which exists as the nature of action.

The scripture of all the buddhas of the three times that teaches the true path is this: for attachment, the unattractive; for aversion, loving-kindness; for delusion, dependent origination.

Thus the ten factors of the great ground (mahābhūmika) are feeling, perception, volition, contact, attention, aspiration, resolve, mindfulness, meditative absorption, and wisdom. It is like this: with respect to a single object they are held to have characteristics that are each distinct from the others.

If one restores the whitewash of a stūpa or of a monastery of the Saṅgha that has been worn away by sun or rain, one is born among the white-bodied gods of the third outer terrace. When one dies and passes from there, then, as a result that corresponds to its cause, one is born in places such as the Tong region and China, with a complexion like the stalk of a white lotus.

The Four Great Kings circle around the faces of Mount Meru. Those that possess celestial mansions the non-Buddhists (tīrthikas) take to be planets, stars and lunar mansions; if one counts only the large ones among them, there are sixty million.

Whatever face of Mount Meru the sun is circling, in those human dwelling places on which the shadow of Mount Meru falls, it becomes night.

In the north there is what is called the wheel-shaped form of wind. It lets those northern stars neither rise nor set, and the non-Buddhists, seeing this, hold that the Seven Sages uphold the world.

In the many-storied palace of Śakra (Indra) there is written a register of the names of former Śakras who died and went to the hells and elsewhere: Good Fortune, Ānanda, Deathless and so on, thirty in all. Seeing it, Śakra grows weary of the world.

In the Cloud of Jewels it says: "How is it that a bodhisattva cultivates loving-kindness abundantly? Son of good family, here a bodhisattva stays alone in solitude, settles inwardly, and turns toward complete seclusion. With a mind deeply weary of the world, with his body upright and gradually relaxed, he sits cross-legged and sets mindfulness before him. Then, with a mind that has not strayed outward, he thinks: 'Whatever beings there may be, those full of anger and malice who do unwholesome deeds, those who attack me, and those who rise up against me as enemies without cause, whether they have arisen, are arising, or will arise, I shall completely pacify the anger and malice of them all, and set them upon the seat of awakening.' And he thinks this not superficially, but with his whole heart. That is how a bodhisattva cultivates loving-kindness abundantly."

"Blessed One, do the tathāgatas not reach complete accomplishment in three incalculable eons?" The Blessed One said: "No. Why is that? Because the domain of a tathāgata that is to be accomplished is inconceivable, it cannot be fully accomplished in three incalculable eons. The eons are to be counted from the time a bodhisattva has entered into the sameness of all phenomena, not from the first resolve."

As it appears in the Great Vehicle Abhidharma Sūtra: "A bodhisattva who possesses four qualities comes to understand that all representations are without objects. They are: knowing the characteristic that is the reason for discordant consciousnesses, as when hungry ghosts, animals, humans and gods see differing representations of one and the same thing; knowing the observation of representations that have no object, as when one observes the past, the future, dreams and reflections; knowing that freedom from error would come about without effort, for if consciousness observed real objects it would know truly without effort, and so would become unerring; and the knowledge that conforms to the three kinds of knowledge. That is: for bodhisattvas and meditators who have gained mastery over the mind, objects appear in whatever way they resolve by the power of their resolve; for yogins who have attained calm abiding and strive in insight into phenomena, objects appear through mere attention; and for those who have attained nonconceptual wisdom and abide in it, no objects appear at all."

In the Sūtra Unraveling the Intent it says: "Mañjuśrī, hold this as the teaching called 'The Definitive Meaning of the Accomplishment of a Tathāgata's Activity.'"

"Blessed One, the reflection that is the domain of the meditative absorption of discerning insight: should it be called different from the mind, or should it be called not different?" "Maitreya, it is called not different. Why is it not different? Because that reflection is nothing more than mere representation. Maitreya, I have explained that consciousness is not divided off by its object, which is mere representation."

"Blessed One, if the reflection that is the domain of meditative absorption is not different from the mind, how does that very mind examine that very mind?" He said: "Maitreya, no phenomenon examines any phenomenon at all; nevertheless, the mind that arises in that way appears in that way. Maitreya, it is like this: in dependence on a form, one sees in the perfectly clean disk of a mirror only that very form, yet one thinks, 'I see a reflection,' and there the form and the reflection that appears seem to be different things. In the same way, the mind that arises in that way appears as a thing different from what is called the reflection of meditative absorption."

"Blessed One, the reflections of beings that abide by nature, form and the rest: should they too be called not different from the mind?" "Maitreya, they are not different. But childish beings of inverted understanding do not know, as it truly is, that these reflections are mere representation, and so they think of them invertedly."

In the Perfection of Wisdom in Eighteen Thousand Lines it was said: "Maitreya, the names adventitiously imputed to things that are signs of formations, from 'form' up to 'the qualities of a buddha,' are they not nothing more than mere conceptual thought?" He replied: "They are mere conceptual thought alone."

In the Letterless Casket, the Essence of Vairocana, it says: "Son of good family, what is the one dharma that a bodhisattva must always guard? It is this: what one does not want for oneself, one should not do to others."

"If that one dharma is guarded, all the bases of a bodhisattva's training will be guarded. Why is that? Son of good family, one who holds his own life dear does not take life. One who holds his own wealth dear does not take what is not given. One who holds his own wife dear does not go to the wife of another. Son of good family, it is taught that whoever, among those and others, thinks, 'I shall act according to the word of the Tathāgata,' should guard this one dharma. Why is that? Son of good family, all who seek unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening seek it as a cause of their own happiness. This is the meaning of the words, 'No one wants suffering for himself; beings want happiness.' I say: what you yourselves do not want, do not do to others. Son of good family, this is the one dharma that a bodhisattva must always guard. Whoever hears this sūtra will, even at the time of death, see these very bodhisattvas together, dwelling on Vulture Peak, the king of mountains. Son of good family, as for what is spoken of as 'the one dharma to which the Tathāgata fully awakened,' there is no such one dharma at all. That in which no phenomenon whatsoever is conceived is what the Tathāgata fully awakened to. Son of good family, the Tathāgata realized that all phenomena are unborn."

In the Perfection of Wisdom in Ten Thousand Lines it says: "What is the gateway to liberation that is emptiness? It is the abiding of a mind that discerns all phenomena as empty of their own characteristics; that is called the gateway to liberation that is emptiness. What is the gateway to liberation that is signlessness? It is the abiding of a mind that discerns all phenomena as without signs by their own characteristics; that is called the gateway to liberation that is signlessness. What is the gateway to liberation that is wishlessness? It is the abiding of a mind that discerns all phenomena as without volitional formation.

"What is the faculty of coming to know what is not yet known? It is the faculties of faith, diligence, mindfulness, meditative absorption and wisdom of persons in training who have not yet attained clear realization. What is the faculty of complete knowledge? It is whatever belongs to persons in training who have not yet attained clear realization. What is the faculty of one who possesses complete knowledge? It is the faculty of faith and the rest belonging to persons beyond training, that is, the arhats, the pratyekabuddhas, the bodhisattvas abiding on the tenth ground, and the tathāgatas.

"What is the meditative absorption without initial thought and with mere analysis? It is whatever lies between the first meditative concentration and the second meditative concentration.

"These are called the ten spheres of totality. One resolves on everything as the earth element, and everything becomes a single element, the earth element alone. Encompassing everything in the same way, one resolves on each in turn up to the consciousness element, and everything becomes a single element, consciousness alone.

"What is knowledge of all? It is the partial knowledge that śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas have, with regard to the twelve sense sources, of the selflessness of the person; that is called knowledge of all. What is knowledge of the aspects of the path? It is this: although a bodhisattva knows the paths of the three vehicles, he does not consider purification to come through the vehicle of the śrāvakas or of the pratyekabuddhas, but goes forth by the path of the bodhisattva."

Being proportioned like a nyagrodha tree means this: his height is as great as his breadth, and his breadth as great as his height. The supreme sense of taste means this: what he swallows goes straight down his throat, straight and without deviation, so that it can be swallowed without obstruction.

He declared: "Śāriputra, what do you think? Do you think that these five aggregates, having trained in the perfection of wisdom, go forth into omniscience?" He replied: "Venerable Blessed One, that is not so. Why is that? Venerable Blessed One, because form is like an illusion, and likewise feeling, perception, formations and consciousness are like an illusion; and whatever is consciousness is the six faculties, and whatever are the six faculties are the five aggregates of appropriation."

The view of the transitory collection, doubt, and holding moral discipline and ascetic observances to be supreme: one who has abandoned these three fetters is called a stream-enterer. Having attained the path of meditation, he comes to make thin both attachment to sense pleasures and malice; that is a once-returner. Through that very cultivation of the path of meditation, he comes to abandon attachment to sense pleasures and malice without remainder. Having abandoned those two, he is a non-returner. Through that very cultivation of the path of meditation, cultivated extensively, he abandons the five fetters belonging to the higher realms, namely attachment to form, attachment to the formless, ignorance, pride and agitation, and so becomes an arhat.

One should cultivate the path of the pratyekabuddha thus: "All formations are dependently originated."

Those who have entered the faultless state of rightness are unable to generate the mind set on unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening. Why is that? Because they have cut off the boundary of saṃsāra.

One should not speak of "a person in whom the exhaustion of life and the exhaustion of the afflictions coincide."

The Blessed One spoke these words to the venerable Subhūti: "Subhūti, there is a way in which a bodhisattva rejects the perfection of wisdom and likewise puts it far away. Subhūti, if, while the great bodhisattva Maitreya is practicing the perfection of wisdom, he thinks, 'Alas, this perfection of wisdom is hollow, it is empty, it is without essence,'

or if he thinks, 'Alas, this perfection of wisdom is a plantain trunk,' then this is rejecting the perfection of wisdom and putting it far away.

"Subhūti, the great bodhisattva Maitreya, after he has fully awakened to unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening, will teach this perfection of wisdom in this very place. All the tathāgatas of this Good Eon, too, will appear in this very northern direction and will teach the perfection of wisdom.

"Subhūti, just as the sixty-two views are all included within the view of the transitory collection, so, Subhūti, all the perfections are included in this profound perfection of wisdom."

To know that whatever has the nature of arising all has the nature of cessation: that is solitary awakening.

The hairs of the head and the hairs of the body that grow from his body and head point upward, deep blue, curling all around in ringlets, soft and glossy, coiling to the right, beautifully grown.

If the Tathāgata had not blessed them, even these two, the moon and the sun, would not exist in the world, and months, half-months, seasons and the cycles of the years would not appear in the world.

He teaches: "All these phenomena are dependently originated, born from the four inversions, and appropriated by the maturation of karma. That being so, what other cause is there here for your perceiving phenomena that are not real things as real things?"

If a bodhisattva, on seeing a creature born in the animal realm, gives rise to the thought, "My field of giving is perfect and complete awakening, not the animal realm," that is not the way of a bodhisattva. Why is that? Because when one's own mind is completely pure, the bodhisattva's field of giving is completely pure. The great bodhisattva should give rise to such thoughts as, "When I have gathered these poor beings through giving, they will not steal the goods of others."

Form is not the sphere of phenomena, and yet apart from form there is no sphere of phenomena either.

This is because a bodhisattva should not train in anything other than emptiness, signlessness and wishlessness. Why is that? Because, Subhūti, all virtuous dharmas are included in these three gateways to liberation.

The venerable Subhūti said to the Blessed One: "Blessed One, if all phenomena are without signs, and signs arise from mere imputation and conceptual construction, how does a bodhisattva become distinguished through virtuous dharmas?"

"Venerable Blessed One, should the great bodhisattva who has first generated the mind of awakening be called 'certain,' or is it the one in his last existence?" The Blessed One said: "Subhūti, the great bodhisattva who has first generated the mind of awakening is also certain. The irreversible one and the one in his last existence are also certain."

On the ground of certainty in rightness, one is not born among the beings of the hells.

In the Noble Compendium of All Phenomena in Full it says: "Mañjuśrī, the karmic obscuration of rejecting the true Dharma is subtle. Mañjuśrī, whoever regards some of the Tathāgata's words as good and regards some as bad, that is rejecting the true Dharma. Whoever rejects the Dharma disparages the Tathāgata."

In the Establisher it says: "Establisher, one who wishes to keep the fast with its eight branches should rise early in the morning and go before a monk, a brahmin, or anyone at all. Arranging the upper robe over one shoulder, placing the right knee on the ground and joining the palms, one should speak these words:

"'I, named So-and-so, from this time onward, for this night tonight until the sun rises tomorrow, abandon the taking of life and turn back from the taking of life. Laying aside the rod and laying aside weapons, I shall be endowed with conscience and endowed with compassion, and I shall abandon taking the life of any sentient being, creature or living thing, down to the very smallest of tiny creatures such as ants and insects, and turn back from the taking of life. Just as the noble arhats, for as long as they live, abandon the taking of life, turn back from the taking of life, lay aside the rod and lay aside weapons, and abandon taking the life of any sentient being, creature or living thing, down to the very smallest of tiny creatures such as ants and insects, and turn back from the taking of life, so I too, So-and-so, from this time onward, for this night tonight until before the sun rises tomorrow, abandon the taking of life and turn back from the taking of life. Laying aside the rod and laying aside weapons, I shall be endowed with conscience and endowed with compassion, and I shall abandon taking the life even of any sentient being, creature or living thing, down to the very smallest of tiny creatures such as ants and insects, and turn back from the taking of life. By this first branch I follow after the training of those noble arhats, I practice after them, I act after them.

"'Just as with turning back from the taking of life, so too I abandon taking what is not given, unchaste conduct, speaking falsehood, grain liquor and prepared liquor that intoxicate and are a ground of heedlessness, song, dance and the sound of music, garlands, perfumes and unguents, ornaments and the wearing of colors, high seats and great seats, and food at the wrong time; and I shall turn back from food at the wrong time. Just as those noble arhats, for as long as they live, abandon food at the wrong time and turn back from food at the wrong time, so I too, So-and-so, from this time onward, for this night tonight until the sun rises tomorrow, abandon food at the wrong time and shall turn back from food at the wrong time.'

By this eightfold vow I follow after the training of those noble arhats, I practice after them, I act after them.' So it is said a second and a third time."

Thus the sixteen great countries are Aṅga, Magadha, Kāśī, Vṛji, Malla, Puntra, Srakpa, Kama, Avanti, Kuru, Pañcala, Vatsa, Śūrasena, Yavana and Kampo.

In the Perfection of Wisdom in One Hundred Thousand Lines it says: "Ignorance itself is omniscience, and omniscience too is ignorance."

"Sons of the gods, because the self is profound, this Dharma is profound. Because sentient being, life, migrator, nourisher, person and the rest are profound, this Dharma is profound. Because form is profound, this Dharma is profound. Likewise, because feeling and the rest are profound, this Dharma is profound."

Subhūti said: "Venerable Śāradvatīputra, beings who, through body, speech and mind, form actions that arise from the inversions bring about sense sources of this kind. Taking up the maturation of karma that is based on the aggregates and arises from conceptual thought, they bring about birth as hell beings and in the animal realm, in the world of Yama, and in the destinies of gods and humans."

"However many tathāgatas, arhats, perfectly complete buddhas appeared in times past, all of them were without conceptual thought and had abandoned conceptual thought. So it is too with those of the future and the present."

"Therefore, Nanda, I entrust this perfection of wisdom to you. Nanda, if, of all the Dharma I have taught, you were to take up all the teachings I have given apart from the perfection of wisdom, master them completely, and afterwards let them be lost or cast them away, Nanda, I would not count that much against you. But Nanda, if you were to take up this profound perfection of wisdom, master it completely, and afterwards let it be lost or cast it away, then, Nanda, I would count that against you."

The self should be understood as emptiness. Sentient being, life, person, individual, the one possessed of power, the master of power, the agent and the rest should be understood to be emptiness.

The Blessed One said: "This is the fruit of stream-entry, through abandoning the three fetters. Through thinning sensual attachment and malice, it is the fruit of once-returning. Through abandoning the five fetters belonging to the lower realm, it is the fruit of non-returning. Through abandoning the five fetters belonging to the higher realms, it is arhatship. Through knowing that whatever phenomena have the nature of arising all have the nature of cessation, it is solitary awakening. Through abandoning all the afflictions that are linked on by habitual tendencies, it is perfect and complete awakening."

All the conditioned things to which childish ordinary beings have become attached are mere names. When the great bodhisattva is practicing the perfection of wisdom, with skill in means he instructs them: "All names arise from imagining. Do not be attached to what is imagined, the emptiness of the essential nature of the nonexistence of things. One who is skilled in the emptiness of all phenomena does not become attached."

The Blessed One said: "Śāradvatīputra, is there, was there, or will there be any such thing of form to which childish ordinary beings cling?" He replied: "Apart from the inversions, there is not."

When the great bodhisattva is practicing the perfection of wisdom, he brings forth the divine eye. With that divine eye he sees all phenomena as empty, and abiding in those phenomena he accomplishes the path.

"Śāradvatīputra, do all the śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas think, 'We shall practice the six perfections, bring beings to maturity, and purify buddhafields; we shall perfect the ten powers of a tathāgata, the fearlessnesses, the discriminating knowledges, loving-kindness, great compassion and the unshared qualities of a buddha, and bring immeasurable beings to complete nirvāṇa'?" He replied: "They do not think so." He said: "A bodhisattva does think so."

The Blessed One said: "Subhūti, will the five aggregates, which are like a dream, an illusion, an echo, an optical illusion, the moon in water, a mirage and an emanation, having trained in the perfection of wisdom, go forth into omniscience?" He replied: "Blessed One, they will not. Why is that? Because, Blessed One, everything from a dream up to an emanation has the nature of a nonexistent thing, and that nature of a nonexistent thing is not apprehended."

The Blessed One said: "Subhūti, it is so. Form is like a dream, feeling is like a dream, perception is like a dream, formations are like a dream, consciousness is like a dream. Whatever is consciousness is the six faculties; that is the five aggregates. They are not apprehended because of inner emptiness, and so on up to: they are not apprehended because of the emptiness of the essential nature of the nonexistence of things. Subhūti, everything from form up to consciousness is like an illusion, an echo, an optical illusion, the moon in water, a mirage and an emanation. Whatever is consciousness is the six faculties; that is the five aggregates. They are not apprehended because of everything from inner emptiness up to the emptiness of the essential nature of the nonexistence of things."

He asked: "Blessed One, if that is so, will not a great bodhisattva who has newly entered the vehicle become afraid, averse and terrified on hearing this?" He said: "Subhūti, if he has newly entered the vehicle, and is also unskilled in means, and falls into the hands of a non-virtuous spiritual friend, then it will be so."

He asked: "What is that skill in means in practicing the perfection of wisdom, by which a great bodhisattva, on hearing this teaching, does not become afraid, averse or terrified?" He said: "Subhūti, when a bodhisattva is practicing the perfection of wisdom, with a mind endowed with omniscience he realizes that form is impermanent, and he does not apprehend it. He realizes that everything from feeling up to consciousness is impermanent, and he does not apprehend it. Subhūti, that is skill in means in practicing the perfection of wisdom. Everything from form up to consciousness is suffering, without self, peaceful, empty, signless and so on, and he does not apprehend it. Subhūti, that is skill in means in practicing the perfection of wisdom."

He asked: "Blessed One, what are conditioned phenomena?" He said: "Subhūti, what are called conditioned phenomena are the desire realm, the form realm and the formless realm, and also the other phenomena that belong to the conditioned element, and everything from the applications of mindfulness up to the eighteen unshared qualities of a buddha. Because these are known as conditioned, they are called conditioned phenomena."

He asked: "What are unconditioned phenomena?" He said: "Subhūti, what are called unconditioned phenomena are that basis in which there is no arising, no destruction, and no change while abiding: the exhaustion of attachment, the exhaustion of aversion, the exhaustion of delusion; suchness, unmistaken suchness, suchness that is not otherwise, the sphere of phenomena, the fixed lawfulness of phenomena, the inconceivable element, and the limit of reality. These are called unconditioned phenomena."

What is the bodhisattva's discernment in giving the Dharma without worldly gain? When he teaches the Dharma, he hopes for nothing at all through that gift of the Dharma, not even for awakening. This is giving the Dharma without worldly gain.

What is the great bodhisattva's producing and dedicating the roots of virtue for purifying a buddhafield? Whatever roots of virtue purify a buddhafield, when the field is being purified through the minds of oneself and others, he dedicates those roots of virtue. This is the bodhisattva's producing and dedicating the roots of virtue for purifying a buddhafield.

What is the great bodhisattva's complete avoidance of familiarity with nuns? He does not stay together with a nun even for the space of a finger-snap; he does not give rise to a craving mind on her account; and he does not let himself become tainted on her account. This is the complete avoidance of familiarity with nuns.

What are the five things to be known? The past, the future, the present, the inexpressible and the unconditioned.

What is the bodhisattva's turning away from views? It is his avoiding the grounds of the śrāvakas and the pratyekabuddhas.

What is the great bodhisattva's bringing about those buddhafields just as he has seen them? Abiding on the ground of a universal monarch, lord of a great trichiliocosm world system, he sees the whole great trichiliocosm world system, and yet he does not become conceited because of it.

What is the great bodhisattva's purifying of a buddhafield? It is purifying the minds of all beings by way of non-apprehension.

What is the great bodhisattva's perfect completion of all excellent qualities? It is purity in bringing beings to maturity and purity of the buddhafield.

What is it that a bodhisattva abiding on the tenth ground is called a tathāgata? It is that the great bodhisattva has brought to complete perfection all the perfections, everything from the ten powers up to the eighteen unshared qualities of a buddha, the wisdom of omniscience, the abandonment of all the afflictions that are linked on by habitual tendencies, great compassion, and all the qualities of a buddha as well. Subhūti, it is in this way that a bodhisattva abiding on the tenth ground is called a tathāgata.

Although no phenomenon whatsoever is apprehended, there is nonetheless the arrangement into three vehicles. In this perfection of wisdom the three vehicles are taught extensively. What are the three? They are explained thus: the vehicle of the śrāvakas, the vehicle of the pratyekabuddhas, and the vehicle of the perfectly complete buddhas.

"From here, with my unobstructed buddha eye, I see in the world systems to the east, as many as the sands of the river Ganges, immeasurable, countless beings who, though they practice for unsurpassed awakening, do not possess skill in means in the perfection of wisdom; and so, of them, only one or two come to abide on the irreversible ground, while most come to abide on the ground of the śrāvakas or the ground of the pratyekabuddhas. Why is that? Kauśika, unsurpassed awakening is hard to accomplish for those who are lazy, without diligence, weak in intention, low in aspiration, and corrupt in wisdom."
