The bodhisattva absorbed in this way gives rise to great compassion for sentient beings, and toward phenomena engages the knowledge that they are equal to space; that is the bodhisattva's striving toward the acceptance that phenomena are unborn.

Passing completely beyond the sphere of infinite space in every way, thinking "Consciousness is infinite," the bodhisattva perfects and abides in the sphere of infinite consciousness, thinking thus: "Perceiving space is a turning into consciousness." And the bodhisattva thinks: "All this is consciousness only, and that infinite consciousness, too, is neither existent nor nonexistent." Being absorbed in this way is the acceptance that phenomena are unborn, but it is not yet its full accomplishment. Passing completely beyond the sphere of infinite consciousness in every way, thinking "There is nothing at all," the bodhisattva perfects and abides in the sphere of nothingness.

As for "nothing at all," "anything" means attachment, aversion and delusion; thought, conceptualization and imagining; all conditioned phenomena; and whatever arises from the imagination of what is unreal: these are called "anything." "Nothing at all" means becoming smaller than all those just described; having become subtle is called "nothing at all." For the bodhisattva abiding in it, the acceptance that phenomena are unborn is exceedingly blazing and exceedingly luminous. Because of that cause, that condition and that basis, and because of having attained it, the bodhisattva is not attached to the sphere of nothingness and seeks higher. Seeking higher, the bodhisattva thinks thus: "This is where, in the subtlety of perceptions, one has thoroughly examined them as nothing at all; it is not neither perception nor non-perception." Here, "without perception" is so because of emptiness, and "not without perception" is so because of dependent origination. The sphere of neither perception nor non-perception becomes manifest, and passing completely beyond the sphere of nothingness in every way, one perfects and abides in the sphere of neither perception nor non-perception.

Abiding there, the bodhisattva thinks, "What joy is there in what is neither perception nor non-perception?" and gives rise to an understanding higher than that. Having given rise to an understanding higher than that, the Dharma of non-arising and non-cessation becomes manifest. One sees all phenomena as unborn and unceased. Having seen all phenomena as ceased by their very nature, they neither perish nor are impermanent. Therefore the acceptance that phenomena are unborn is fully purified. Beyond that, one enters into the spontaneously accomplished without interruption. These are the nine successive meditative attainments of a bodhisattva.

From the Vimalakīrtinirdeśa: After the Licchavi youth Ratnākara had asked the Buddha about the purification of a buddha-field, and it had been explained that the perfections, the ten virtues, the awakening mind and so on are the buddha-field, the Buddha said: "Therefore, son of good family, if a bodhisattva wishes to purify a buddha-field, the bodhisattva should purify their own mind. Why is that? To the extent that a bodhisattva's mind is pure, to that extent the buddha-field becomes pure."

Then, through the power of the Buddha, the venerable Śāriputra thought: "If a buddha-field becomes pure just as one's own mind is pure, then when the Blessed One Śākyamuni was practicing the conduct of a bodhisattva, was his mind not pure? Why does this buddha-field appear so impure?"

Then the Blessed One, knowing Śāriputra's thought, said: "Śāriputra, what do you think? Why is it that the blind do not see the sun and moon? The fault lies with the blind, not with the sun and moon. In the same way, Śāriputra, that sentient beings who lack merit and are afflicted by the eye of ignorance, that no sentient being at all sees the many ornaments of qualities of the buddha-field, is not the fault of the Tathāgata. Śāriputra, the buddha-field of the Tathāgata is pure, but you do not see it."

Then Brahmā Śikhin said to Śāriputra: "Do not say that the buddha-field is impure. The buddha-field is pure. Śāriputra, just like the adorned abode of the gods of the Heaven of Mastery over Others' Emanations, we see this buddha-field of the Blessed One Śākyamuni as adorned with qualities."

Then Śāriputra said to Brahmā Śikhin: "Brahmā, I see this great earth as utterly filled with highs and lows, thorns, cliffs, ravines, peaks, chasms, filth and stones."

Brahmā said: "Venerable Śāriputra, it is because your mind is not pure, and you conceive of highs and lows, and because your intention toward the Buddha's wisdom is not pure, that you see the buddha-field in that way. Śāriputra, those sentient beings whose mental intentions are all pure, and whose intention toward the Buddha's wisdom is pure, see this buddha-field as pure."

Then, knowing that the assembly were wondering what this could be like, the Buddha touched this great earth with his big toe. At that moment this great trichiliocosm appeared adorned with many jewels and with a host of qualities; it appeared like, for example, the field of the Tathāgata Ratnavyūha. And everyone in the assembly knew themselves to be seated on jeweled lotuses.

Then he said to Śāriputra, "Do you see?" He replied, "I see such as I have never seen before." He said: "My buddha-field is always pure like this, but in order to bring inferior beings to maturity, I show it as having many harms and faults. For example, the sons of the gods eat their food from a single vessel, yet each experiences it according to their merit. In the same way, though born in a single buddha-field, sentient beings see the praiseworthy qualities of the buddha-field according to how pure their intentions are." Then, when the buddha-field appeared in this way, eighty-four thousand living beings gave rise to the mind directed toward unsurpassed awakening.

From the Inconceivable Light: King Utter Vanquisher of Armies questioned the Buddha, and he declared: "Ninety-one eons ago, when the Buddha Vipaśyin appeared, there were in his teaching two bodhisattvas called Excellent Deva and Earth. Excellent Deva had few desires and few activities, and was irreversible in the practice of meditative concentration and the superknowledges. Earth was not endowed with such great qualities. While serving as Excellent Deva's attendant, he went far too often to towns, to districts and to the surroundings of the royal palace, and having gone there, he had many activities. Excellent Deva said, 'You have far too many activities. Strive a little at giving them up through your practice.' He urged him again and again, and Earth, taking these as angry words and displeased, said with harsh words, 'You bastard, son of a harlot! Your father and mother were adulterers, so how could you have moral discipline and wisdom?' And afterwards he did not repent, but showed his resentment lying dormant."

Excellent Deva, recognizing that he was simply not a vessel, dealt with him and let him go. But Earth, seething with fury, reviled Excellent Deva at every meeting. After he died, for sixty eons he was born as a bastard; as soon as he was born he was cast onto the footpath of the street and eaten by foxes and other creatures, and many beings reviled him as "bastard, son of a harlot." Because he was held by the awakening mind, that being was not born in hell. Excellent Deva is practicing pure conduct in the buddha-field of the Tathāgata Unshakable, and is called the bodhisattva Śrīsambhava. Inconceivable Light, too, having in the past practiced pure conduct in the presence of nine hundred and forty million buddhas and pleased them, became such a great being. Therefore, even at the cost of one's life, one should restrain sinful actions of body, speech and mind. One should rely steadfastly on a spiritual friend, for from that every host of good qualities will be accomplished.

At that time the Buddha was staying at Jetavana. When the Buddha went for alms, he saw a little boy who had been left in an empty house on a street of the Jeta market. And he prophesied that the boy would, after a hundred countless eons, become the Buddha Inconceivable Light.

From the Ratnarāśi Sūtra: "The stains of an ascetic are thirty-two. What are they? Thoughts of sensual desire, malice, harmfulness, praising oneself, disparaging others, seeking improper gain, and the stain of wanting gain by means of gain; belittling what is given in faith, concealing downfalls, relying on householders, relying on those who have gone forth, delighting in bustle, considering one's gains incomplete, wanting others' gains, being unsatisfied with one's own gains, envy of others' gaining merit, being stingy, seeking out others' errors, not restraining one's own errors through the prātimokṣa and the Vinaya, chattering like a crow, disrespect, excitement, distraction, outbursts of views, arising from the gathering of conditions and company, attachment to contradictory and extreme views, delighting in saṃsāra, not delighting in nirvāṇa, holding the secret spells of worldly society, being enveloped by obscurations, contradicting the maturation of karma, being frightened of the three doors of liberation, rejecting the profound Dharma of the Buddha, abiding in what contradicts the Dharma, and disrespect for the Three Jewels.

"Kāśyapa, it is many sinful monks who will bring about the decline of this teaching of mine. Kāśyapa, the ninety-five kinds of non-Buddhists and all opponents are unable to bring about the decline of my teaching. Rather, deluded persons will arise from within this very teaching of mine, and they will bring about the decline of my teaching. It is like this: Kāśyapa, even when the lion, king of beasts, has died, neither beasts nor birds are able to eat its flesh; rather, worms arise from the lion's corpse itself and eat the flesh of the lion, king of beasts. It is like that.

"Kāśyapa, one who has four qualities should be known as a sinful monk. What four? Being conceited, being clamorous, being like a vulture, and corrupt speech. And again four: haughtiness, swaggering, wanting gain, and wanting what is not Dharma.

"Kāśyapa, I permit two monks to receive offerings. What two? The pure monk who sees fear in wrongdoing with respect to the world beyond, who follows the maturation of karma, and who is possessed of shame and remorse; and one who has become an arhat."

From the Ratnakūṭa: "Through the power of a mighty nāga king's thought for the earth, he sends forth from a single mass of cloud garlands of lightning whose nature is heat, and he also sends forth streams of rain whose nature is to make things grow.

"Ānanda, in the time to come, at the end of the last five hundred years, when the true Dharma is perishing, the monks in my teaching will mostly come with their lower robes and upper robes not properly worn but hitched up, with broken alms bowls, without vigilance, with unsightly deportment, carrying staffs in their hands, flat-nosed, one-eyed, lame, crippled, of poor physique, and afflicted by various diseases. Why is that? Because at that time the monks will mostly be bound up with various activities, wanting service, gain and honor; the prātimokṣa, the discipliner, will be torn apart. Intent on this life, they will not look to the next. Not for the sake of the Dharma but for the sake of service, they will give the going forth and full ordination to everyone from the one-eyed to those of poor physique. Through such as that, my teaching will become unfit to be looked upon and unfit to be heard. The gods will become dismayed, and the māras will become joyful and at ease.

"Through two qualities one fully and perfectly awakens to unsurpassed awakening: diligence and conscientiousness. In brief, all the bodhisattva's actions of skillful means, however many they may be, are diligence, and all actions of wisdom there are, are conscientiousness. Therefore, for one endowed with method and wisdom there is no turning back from unsurpassed awakening."

From the Vikurvaṇarājaparipṛcchā: "What is a sūtra of definitive meaning? Because the ultimate is inexpressible, all the sūtras are of definitive meaning; that being so, one relies on the sūtras of definitive meaning. Those who do not rely on these sūtras of definitive meaning are called 'to be led.' Why are they called 'to be led'? Because they abide in phenomena that contradict the definitive meaning, they need to be led. By what are they led? They are led by words. Those who rely on the sūtras of definitive meaning lead by words. Why is that? Because they realize the inexpressible ultimate, and because they know all phenomena of the three times without omission. King Vikurvaṇa, if in this mode of meaning one relies in this way on this nature of reality, all the sūtras become of definitive meaning; if one does not rely on it in this way, all the sūtras become of provisional meaning."

In the buddha-field of the Tathāgata Devarāja, the food and drink of the people, too, appear merely by their thinking of them.

From the Ugraparipṛcchā: "When a householder bodhisattva sees a beggar, three perceptions should be generated: that this is a spiritual friend; that, once one has changed lives, it will turn into wealth; and that it is close reliance on the path of awakening. And again three, which are freedom from the three poisons. Why is that? Because one gives the thing away, because one is compassionate, and because one dedicates the gift to unsurpassed awakening. Living in a household is torment, is bondage, is of little purpose, is a stairway to the lower realms, is the decline of the true Dharma, is entangled with material things, and is the basis of suffering. Going forth is the reverse of these. A householder bodhisattva should be skilled in the conduct of ascetics. One should reconcile ascetics who are at odds. When the true Dharma is perishing, one should protect it even at the risk of one's life. On seeing a sick monk, one should nurse him even with one's own flesh and blood. The four purities of moral discipline are: abiding in the four lineages of the noble ones; delighting in the qualities of austerity and in having few possessions; not mixing with householders or with those who have gone forth; and dwelling in the wilderness without hypocrisy."

In the Lion's Roar of Maitreya it is said: "Four are robbers in the guise of ascetics: one who abides in the sign of a monk, in its color and shape, yet practices sinful things and has corrupt moral discipline; one who has gone into solitude yet entertains unwholesome thoughts; one who, not having attained the fruit, claims to have attained the fruit for the sake of gain and honor; and one who praises oneself and disparages others.

"For example, there were two people skilled in mantras and medicines for curing poison. One of them, in order to win over many people, ate poison and once cured himself by that method. Then, saying, 'I shall do so again,' he ate poison, but there was no chance for treatment and he died. The other said, 'Even if I do not win over many people, it is better so; I will neither eat poison nor administer medicine,' and he lived at ease and reached the end of his life. Likewise, in later times, some who have gone forth will say, 'One can purify oneself after committing a fault,' while others restrain themselves from the outset; these two are like those two.

"A bodhisattva should flee a hundred leagues from these four: gain, honor, fame, and sinful friends and bustle; one should flee from them. Wherever someone of the bodhisattva vehicle is dwelling, if there is even a chance that some thought of anger toward them might arise, one should flee a hundred leagues. Maitreya, set beside a bodhisattva's reviling, beating or striking with weapons all the sentient beings of a trichiliocosm, it is giving rise to some anger toward another bodhisattva that brings ruin; for if one gives rise to anger, one must put on the armor anew for as many eons as there were moments of that mind. Just as iron is cut by iron but cannot be cut by stone, wood or grass, so a bodhisattva's roots of virtue are exhausted by nothing other than giving rise to anger toward a companion. Therefore one should regard even one who has just given rise to the first resolve as the Teacher.

"If one who lives in a household
Were to fill the whole trichiliocosm
With jewels, and offer it
As a gift to the protector of the world,
And if a wise one, weary of the world,
Knowing its faults, were to forsake the household,
And, training in the footsteps of the Buddha,
Were to seek to go forth,
And, having sought going forth,
Without slackening, not looking back
At desires or at the household home,
Were to take but seven steps,
Then to that heap of merit
The former's rain of jewels would not compare."

In the time of the Tathāgata Variegated Crest, led by the universal monarch Nemi, all the people of the four continents went forth. Rice that was neither ploughed nor sown appeared, and wish-fulfilling trees as well, and their service was done by the gods.

From the Teaching on the Indivisible Nature of the Dharmadhātu: "In the world system Array of Joy, the food and drink of the people come to them merely by their thinking of them. The buddhas teach the Dharma through the very indivisibility of the nature of the dharmadhātu; they do not teach the Dharma of non-arising, non-cessation and so on for the sake of passing into nirvāṇa. That is how the Blessed Ones teach the Dharma."

From the Offering of Lamps: "If, with the intention of making offering to the Tathāgata, one makes lamplight shine even for as long as a flash of lightning, or even over as little as a single step of a stair, the ripening of it cannot be conceived by śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas. If one offers a lamp to a stūpa of the Tathāgata, at the time of death one attains four lights. What are they? The disk of the sun rising before one, the moon rising, a retinue of gods present, and seeing the Tathāgata."

Five things are supremely rare to find: birth as a human being, going forth in the teaching of the Tathāgata, delighting in it, perfecting moral discipline, and the exhaustion of the defilements.

From the Five Perfections: "A bodhisattva never enters the attainment of cessation, and the Tathāgata has not permitted entering it. The time and occasion for a bodhisattva to enter the attainment of cessation will come: when one sits at the heart of awakening, that is its time and occasion. In order to turn back from all perceptions and feelings, one enters it, and having fully and perfectly awakened to the unsurpassed, one turns the wheel of Dharma with its twelve aspects. As for the eight successive meditative attainments, one both enters them and emerges.

"Suppose that some beings, wishing to realize the fruit of arhatship, give gifts to sentient beings, or to śrāvakas or pratyekabuddhas, for as many eons as there are grains of sand in the Ganges. If a bodhisattva, considering that gift, rejoices in it and dedicates the root of virtue so that bodhisattvas newly entered into the vehicle may become irreversible, and the irreversible may swiftly attain omniscience, then the former merit does not come close to it, either as a comparison or as a fraction.

"When a man is in danger from robbers, it is better for him to arrive later; to be plundered and arrive quickly is not so. Likewise, it is better to fully and perfectly awaken after a long time; to fall to the level of the śrāvakas or the level of the pratyekabuddhas is not so."

Pūrṇa said: "Not knowing is dependent origination; it is produced by no one."

"It arises from the assembling and coming together of causes, and when the causes are absent it ceases. Since phenomena cease of their own accord, there is no one at all who makes them cease. Because one does not know these things to be like illusions, one clings to them strongly and comes to have a mind that will not give them up; and by not giving them up one holds to stinginess. Through that stinginess one is born in the lower realms and remains bound up with that thing."

The Blessed One did not permit bodhisattvas to enter the four meditative concentrations and to abide in them. Why is that? Out of concern that they would be born among the long-lived gods; whereas if they are born in the desire realm, they swiftly come to perfect omniscience.

From the Sarvadharmaguṇavyūharāja: He declared: "There is a dhāraṇī called Jayavatī. It was received from the Tathāgata Victor's Melody at the time of being a bodhisattva, and at the very moment it was obtained, the ten grounds were attained, and many thousands of living beings also attained acceptance of the Dharma." Then those bodhisattvas said, "Blessed One, please teach the dhāraṇī of great power." Then the Blessed One spoke this dhāraṇī:

tadyathā jaya jaya jayavaha khavaha hulu hulu padmabhe avamavame sarā sarāṇe dhiri dhiri dhiri dhiri devatā anupālaṇa yuttottāraṇi paracakranivarṇi puraya bhagavāna sarva āśana mama sarvakarma kṣapaya buddha atiśaneṇa svāhā

"Son of good family, this is the dhāraṇī called Jayavatī. It exhausts all wrongdoing, makes wealth and grain increase, turns back the armies of enemies, fully pacifies the harm of famine, and prolongs life. This is a Dharma discourse that is like the father and mother of the tathāgatas."

Then the bodhisattva Vajrapāṇi spoke these words of dhāraṇī-mantra:

namaḥ sarvatathāgatānāṃ namo amitabhāya tathāgatāya namaḥ sarvabodhisatvānān namaḥ sarvamahartekebhyaḥ tadyathā huhu hehe mati mati mahāvajramati dṛḍhaparavajramati tathāgata anuparivaleṭi sarasara ayokhi bhṛkuṭi bhṛkuṭimukhi kripi kripaluke satyapācana manusmara bhagavān vajrapāṇi sarvakarma āparaṇi kṣavaya sarva artha maparipūrāya dehi me varam buddhasatyen bodhisatva satyena svāhā

"Whoever wishes to see me, to become a universal monarch through power, to attain meditative absorption, to retain what has been heard, to have the elements ripen, to make the body invisible, or to travel through the sky, should, on the eighth day of the waxing moon, keep a fast day and night and have an image made. One should make an image of Vajradhara, the color of arrow-bamboo, adorned with all ornaments, peaceful in manner, and containing relics. In the center one should make the Tathāgata Śākyamuni, seated. On the right side one should place the noble Avalokiteśvara, four-armed, adorned with all ornaments, and containing relics. To the right and left one should place the Four Great Kings making offerings.

"The son of good family who is the practitioner should keep clean, bathe at the three times, take barley gruel as food for five periods, and recite at the three times. As incense, one should offer aloeswood, sandalwood, pok resin and guggulu. One should recite up to three hundred thousand times. This is how the practice with the image is to be done. At a stūpa containing relics, or in a wilderness place, one should also make offerings of scents, incense, various flowers and various banners. One should also offer a deity-cake of white flour. One should also have this Dharma discourse written down in letters and make offerings to it of clothes, ornaments, streamers, parasols and various kinds of conveyances.

"Then, until the fifteenth day, the full moon, one should be possessed of a mind of compassion toward all sentient beings, bathe at the three times and change one's clothes at the three times. Smearing a square maṇḍala with cow dung and covering it with various flowers, one should set out sixteen full vases and sixteen lamps. One should also adorn that place with parasols, victory banners and various banners. Setting down a vast food offering blended of many flavors, and sprinkling that place with scented water, one should recite until the image and the ground shake and one oneself becomes joyful. Then one should know, 'This will certainly and without fail be accomplished.' After that, I shall show myself in a dream and converse with that person. I shall fulfill all their wishes. I shall protect them like an only son. I shall grant whatever excellent thing they desire, except to one who lacks faith in this Dharma discourse and rejects it."

Then the bodhisattva Vajrapāṇi said to the Blessed One, "Blessed One, he is called Avalokiteśvara, Avalokiteśvara. Why is he called that?" He declared: "Because he looks upon the whole world in this way, considers it, satisfies it, fully protects it and truly gives it relief; because he is possessed of a mind of compassion, a mind of happiness, a mind of love and a mind of tender affection; and because he fulfills all wishes, therefore he is called Avalokiteśvara."

From the Gaganagañja: The bodhisattva Puṇyālaṃkāra asked, "Blessed One, how much merit will be generated by those sons or daughters of good family who fully comprehend such a profound sūtra as this, spoken by the Tathāgata, of definitive meaning, hard to see, taught and praised by all the buddhas, and who teach it extensively to others as well?" Then the Buddha declared that the merit is immeasurable.

For one who holds the characteristic of cognizance, all phenomena are without real entity.

Though in the Dharma body the Victors, fully discerned, are equal in body,
They also display a body endowed with beauty and of excellent form;
Having given rise to compassion that benefits sentient beings,
They also show the supreme body with the marks of a hundred merits.

All these phenomena are born from causes and conditions;
Without agent, without essential nature, they are born of imagination.
The Sugata, knowing such modes of phenomena,
Attained unchanging, peaceful and unsurpassed awakening.

"Here, son of good family, how is a bodhisattva skilled in the wisdom of dependent origination and free from all extreme views? Son of good family, the bodhisattva thinks thus: 'Whatever is dependently originated is nothing at all. Whatever depends on conditions is without real entity. Whatever depends on conditions is not established. Whatever depends on conditions has no self, no sentient being, no life, no nourisher, no person, no individual, no one born of Manu, no son of Manu. In whatever depends on conditions, in reality nothing at all is born. A phenomenon that depends on conditions is free of elaboration; therefore of the unelaborated, the non-elaborated, it is said: "When it arises, it is just the nature of reality that arises; when it ceases, it is just the nature of reality that ceases." In it there is no self, or sentient being, or anything up to a son of Manu. Because those phenomena that arise from conditions have no owner, there is no arising whatsoever; therefore all phenomena are said to be like grass, trees, walls, dreams and mirages. Just as for external things there is no producer of their arising and no stopper of their ceasing, so too for internal phenomena, apart from their arising when conditions are present and ceasing when conditions are incomplete, there is no producer of their arising and no stopper of their ceasing.' One who applies oneself thoroughly to wisdom in this way thoroughly abandons all extreme views. What, then, are extreme views? 'Extremes' means permanence and annihilation. One who neither brings about arising in this nor brings about perishing; what has no arising and no perishing is without permanence and annihilation. Therefore it is pure; it is pure of all extreme views. In this way one is skilled in dependent origination and in the wisdom of engagement, and is free from all extreme views."

Wishing for the lesser vehicle is the work of Māra.

I shall give rise to the supreme mind of awakening;
I shall wholly take care of all sentient beings;
I shall practice what is the supreme conduct of awakening;
I shall make beings into unsurpassed buddhas.

"For example, just as there is no antidote for space, so there is no antidote for any phenomenon. Those who seek nirvāṇa by means of antidotes hold a view of arising and perishing. Because they engage in arising and perishing, thinking, 'These phenomena are to be produced,' they do not know the equality of nirvāṇa." Therefore the Blessed One, too, said this: "Understand it thus! Do not dispute! Through freedom from dispute, perfect the supreme qualities of an ascetic!"

Monks will appear who are firmly set on objects of focus,
Messengers of Māra, of Māra's party,
Who will disparage the true Dharma.

Of evil character and sinful ways,
They live relying on householders;
Greedy for honors and for gain,
They do not seek this way.

They mingle with the worldly Lokāyatas,
Praising themselves greatly
And disparaging those who are truly certain;
Unskilled and arrogant ones will appear.

Having forsaken wilderness dwellings and mountains,
They will always delight in bustle;
They strive at the secret words of the world,
And they firmly abide in the perishable collection.

Those unskilled ones are very zealous
In managing affairs for the Saṅgha,
But they do not examine the sūtras
In harmony with one another;
To what each of them says on their own
They are always devoted.

They swagger with their objects of focus
And dwell in every kind of divergent view;
Having heard the Dharma of emptiness,
They regard it as a precipice.

At such a time as this,
The well-spoken teachings,
Possessed of meaning and of letters,
Will utterly perish.

Rather than abiding in the teachings,
Over dharmas that merely resemble them,
Asking, "How do they exist or not exist?"
They will even fight like executioners.

"We are ascetics in name alone;
We have none of an ascetic's qualities":
When they receive true exhortation,
They will come to long for this sūtra.

As those whose ears and noses are cut off
Say, on looking in a mirror, "How unsightly!",
So, on hearing true exhortation,
They will come to long for the true Dharma.

Ascetics will appear
Who fully uphold the true Dharma;
Though others put on a pretense,
No one at all will listen to this Dharma.

They will deceive even kings,
And though most people will be divided,
Through the blessing of the Buddha
Those beings will listen to the Dharma.

At that time we,
For the benefit of sentient beings,
Giving up even body and life,
Shall uphold the true Dharma.

"The activities of a bodhisattva are four: cutting off all māras and afflictions; overpowering all opponents in accordance with the Dharma; bringing sentient beings to maturity; and upholding the true Dharma. And these four activities are included in a single activity. Which one? Namely, upholding the true Dharma."

Through the blessing of the bodhisattva Gaganagañja, many verses teaching the profound Dharma resounded from the sky, and the bodhisattva Possessed of Wonder said to Gaganagañja, "I am amazed, wondering where these sounds have come from." Gaganagañja said, "Son of good family, what do you think? Where does the sound of an echo come from?" He replied, "It comes from conveying the sound of another." He asked, "Does an echo have any body or mind, or any form, color or shape?" He replied, "It has none." "Just so: although an echo is not established, it conveys sound; in the same way, space, though not established, comes to convey sounds and speech."

He asked, "Who conveys this sound? From where did this voice come?" He replied, "The conveying power of wisdom is inconceivable. When I think with the intention of my mind, although it comes from the world Blissful Appearance, the mind does not go there, nor does it come here; yet the echo of the sound arises. Son of good family, see how profound dependent origination is! Although the result arises from the cause, the cause does not pass over into the result, nor does a result arise from no cause. That is, both of these, the phenomena by which a result is accomplished from causes and conditions, are without movement and without agency. Those who know such a coming together are freed from it. Why is that? Because the Blessed One, too, said that knowing thorough affliction is itself its purification."

From the Ratnacūḍaparipṛcchā: "What are the eight complete purities of a bodhisattva's practice of the perfection of moral discipline? Not being hypocritical, with a mind free of material concern; not flattering, through casting off all possessiveness; not hinting for gifts, through being content by nature; fully perfecting the true upholding of the lineages of the noble ones, through the qualities of austerity and having few possessions; not caring for the body, through not giving up dwelling in the wilderness; not caring for one's life, through upholding the true Dharma; delighting in solitude, through forsaking bustle; and being devoted to heroism in the Dharma, through fear of the whole of the three realms.

"The Perfection of Wisdom is said to be a going to the far shore; therefore it is called a perfection, a gone-beyond. Because it has gone beyond all signs, all objects of focus and all the mental activities of sentient beings, it is therefore called a perfection. Its complete purities of wisdom are two. What are they? The complete purity of the characteristic of non-attachment, and the complete purity of the characteristic of non-obstruction."

He declared: "Son of good family, 'the superknowledges' is a synonym for undefiled wisdom. Through these five superknowledges the bodhisattva sports freely, and also abides firmly in undefiled wisdom. When a bodhisattva possesses four qualities, the bodhisattva is able to bring sentient beings to maturity. The four are: not growing weary; not being attached to one's own happiness; urging others on, in season and out of season; and fully knowing their dispositions. And again four: being easy to speak to, speaking pleasantly, being gentle in temperament, and having a smiling face without frowning.

"The bodhisattva's four applications of mindfulness, beginning with the body, are applications of mindfulness having two aspects. The two are discerning the body as unattractive and as attractive. Here, 'unattractive' means thinking, 'Alas, this body is full of impurity, short-lived, and a deceiver of the childish.' 'Attractive' means thinking, 'Through this body I shall attain the body of a Tathāgata, the Dharma body, the body of merit, the body of wisdom, the vajra body, the indestructible body, the undefiled body, on seeing whose form all sentient beings will be satisfied.' And again two aspects: as impermanent and as permanent. Here, the aspect of impermanence is: 'Alas, this body is impermanent and subject to destruction; so one does no wrongful harm for the sake of the body.' The permanent is that all the roots of virtue of abiding in contemplation of the body are fully dedicated to unsurpassed awakening, so that the lineage of the Three Jewels may not be cut off and in order to protect all sentient beings. By abiding in the four applications of mindfulness, one abandons the four inversions: one abandons the inversion that takes the body as clean, feeling as bliss, the mind as permanent and phenomena as a self."

From the Suvarṇaprabhāsottama:

If the body has no bones or blood,
How could there be relics?
For the benefit of sentient beings,
He left relics as a skillful means.

"That it is said in other contexts that he passed into nirvāṇa and left relics in the world is the skill in means of the Tathāgata. The offerings and services that sentient beings make should be seen as the power of the Tathāgata's compassion and roots of virtue. Whoever makes such offerings and services will, in future times, completely avoid the eight unfree states, will meet the tathāgatas and have spiritual friends, will not lose the awakening mind, will increase immeasurable merit, and will swiftly pass beyond saṃsāra and not be seized by the bonds of saṃsāra. Since this is the sublime practice, you should accomplish it earnestly and without negligence.

"What abides solely as suchness and as true wisdom itself is called the Dharma body. The former two bodies are mere designations. The Dharma body is real, and it serves as the basis of those bodies. Why is that? Because apart from the suchness of phenomena and nonconceptual wisdom, there is no quality of a buddha whatsoever. Son of good family, for example, just as many kinds of afflictions, karma and results are shown in dependence on the conceptualizing mind, so the various teachings of the three vehicles are shown in dependence on the nature of phenomena and the wisdom of suchness. For example, the sun and moon have no conceptualization, water and mirrors have no conceptualization, and light has no conceptualization, yet from the coming together of the three a reflection arises. In the same way, suchness and the wisdom of suchness have no conceptualization, yet by the power of aspiration the enjoyment body and the emanation body arise for fortunate beings from the coming together of causes and conditions, like reflections of the sun and moon. Son of good family, again, for example, waters and mirrors immeasurable and countless, in dependence on light, show empty reflections with differing characteristics. 'Empty' means without characteristic. In the same way, by the power of the aspiration that the enjoyment body and the emanation body should show the reflection of the Dharma body to the assemblies, those two bodies appear; and though they appear with various signs, the abiding of the Dharma body has no differing characteristics."

"Son of good family, it is with reference to those very bodies that the buddhas, the blessed ones, are said to be in nirvāṇa with remainder of the aggregates. With reference to the Dharma body, they are said to be in nirvāṇa without remainder of the aggregates. On the basis of these three bodies, the buddhas are explained as being in non-abiding nirvāṇa. Son of good family, the emanation body of the Buddha is called 'many,' because, in accordance with the minds of ordinary individuals, it shows various signs in many forms. The enjoyment body is called 'one,' because, the intention of the assemblies being one, its characteristic is one. The Dharma body has passed completely beyond various signs and is not the domain of apprehension through signs; therefore it is called neither one nor different. Son of good family, the emanation body appears in dependence on the enjoyment body; the enjoyment body appears in dependence on the Dharma body; the Dharma body is real and depends on nothing at all. Son of good family, there is a way of explaining these three bodies as permanent, and there is also a way of explaining them as impermanent.

"The powers, the fearlessnesses, the specific perfect knowledges and so on, the eighty unshared qualities: just as gold, water and space, when free of stain, turbidity and cloud, do not thereby cease to be gold and so on, so the Dharma body, through the exhaustion of all afflictions, does not become nonexistent as the very body that is called 'pure.' Although conceptualization appears to exist, in its essential nature there is no conceptualization. Although they are counted as three, there are not three entities; that which neither increases nor decreases is like a dream, an illusion and a mirage, and there is neither grasped object nor grasping subject.

"Śāriputra, I have declared that among gifts the gift of the Dharma is supreme.

The body is without movement and without agency,
Without essence, arisen from conditions;
Arisen from the imagination of what is unreal,
It stands like a karmic machine in an empty village.

All these phenomena are empty;
They arise from the condition of ignorance.
These great elements do not greatly arise;
From what has not arisen they arise, and yet they do not arise.
Because the elements do not arise,
Therefore I call them the great elements.

They are not existent, nor ever existent;
They arise from the condition of ignorance.
This ignorance does not exist;
Therefore I have explained this ignorance."

From the Dhāraṇī of the Stacked Pinnacle Chambers of Great Vajra Meru: "Because it is imputed, the imputed is empty. Because it is dependently originated, the dependent nature too is empty. Because it arises from causes and conditions, dependent origination too is empty. Because it is adventitiously produced by causes and conditions, it is empty of the nature of arising. Here dependent origination is empty of its own characteristic, and whatever is empty of its own characteristic is without characteristic."

What is called the presentation of the three bodies is the characteristic of what is emanated through the two accumulations.

From the Tathāgatotpattisaṃbhava: "There dwell together bodhisattvas bound to one more birth, as numerous as the atoms of ten buddha-fields, such as the bodhisattva Samantabhadra, all of whom, with a single and undifferentiated body whose nature is wisdom, display physical bodies through infinite doors.

"With a single breath of Subhadrā, a goddess of Vaśavartin, king of the gods, there arises the melody of divine music with a hundred thousand parts, and that sound, once it has arisen, resounds as the melodies of a hundred thousand different sounds. In the same way, although the Tathāgata utters but a single speech, it resounds as immeasurable sounds in accordance with the intentions of sentient beings.

"For example, the entire great trichiliocosm, from the mass of water below that supports it up to the peak of existence, the sphere of neither perception nor non-perception, abides in space. All the abodes of beings who dwell in the formless realm and all the abodes of beings who dwell in the desire realm abide in space and arise from the expanse of space.

"For example, suppose there were a great canvas the size of a great trichiliocosm world system, and on that canvas the whole great trichiliocosm were completely drawn: the celestial palaces of the gods who dwell in the desire realm, the celestial palaces of the gods who dwell in the form realm, the celestial palaces of the gods who dwell in the formless realm and so on, drawn to their true dimensions. And suppose that canvas were placed inside a single atom, so that no being whatsoever saw or knew of it, but someone with a miraculous eye saw it, split the atom open, showed it to beings and so benefited them. Just so, the wisdom of the Tathāgata, which sustains all sentient beings, abides fully contained within the minds of all sentient beings, but the childish do not know it. For those who do not know it, the Tathāgata unties the knots of childish perception, so that they comprehend wisdom and realize equality; that too is like this. By this example one should understand the arising of the Tathāgata's wisdom."

From the Saddharmapuṇḍarīka: "Pūrṇa, son of Maitrāyaṇī, will in the future fully awaken in this very Sahā world system. At that time there will be no lower realms in this buddha-field, nor any women. All those beings will be born miraculously, practicing pure conduct, with mind-made bodies, luminous of themselves, possessing miraculous powers, travelling through the sky, golden in color, and adorned with the thirty-two marks of a great being."

The sixteen excellent men: Bhadrapāla, Ratnākara, Susārthavāha, Naradatta, Guhagupta, Varuṇadatta, Indradatta, Uttaramati, Viśeṣamati, Vardhamānamati, Amoghadarśin, Susaṃprasthita, Suvikrāntavikrāmin, Anupamamati, Sūryagarbha, and Dharaṇīṃdhara.

The tathāgatas of the ten directions came to this Sahā world system, each together with his own attendants, with one or with two. At that time this whole Sahā world system became thoroughly adorned with jewel trees. Its ground of beryl was covered with a net of the seven precious substances and of gold, adorned with nets of bells and tinkling bells, and strewn with various flowers, and it was marked out in a checkerboard with golden cords. There were no villages, towns, market towns, provinces, kingdoms, or royal capitals. There were no black mountains. There was no Mucilinda, no Great Mucilinda, no Cakravāḍa, no Great Cakravāḍa, and no Sumeru. There was no Great Sumeru and no other mountain. There were no oceans. The rivers and the great rivers were no longer present. There were no hosts of gods, humans, or asuras. There were no hell beings, no animal births, and no world of Yama. For at that time all the beings born into the six destinies of the round of existence in the Sahā world system were placed in other world systems, except for those who had gathered in that assembly.

"... as arising from distorted perception. Mañjuśrī, a bodhisattva mahāsattva who abides in this way, constantly viewing all phenomena thus and dwelling in this, abides in the domain of a bodhisattva mahāsattva."

"The Dharma teacher who wishes to expound this sūtra
Should give up entirely deceit, pride, and disparagement.
So too the wise one should not envy others.
Never should he speak ill of anyone.
Never should he quarrel over views.
Never should he give grounds for doubt,
Saying, 'You will not attain unsurpassed wisdom.'

"Mañjuśrī, this is the supreme teaching of the Dharma of the tathāgatas; it is the ultimate among Dharma discourses. Among all Dharma discourses this Dharma discourse is profound; it is at odds with the whole world."

Many bodhisattvas who had come from other world systems said, "Blessed One, if we should wish to teach this Dharma discourse and so forth here in Sahā after the Tathāgata has passed into nirvāṇa, please instruct us." The Blessed One declared, "What need is there for you to do so? In this very world system there are thousands of bodhisattvas whom I have brought to maturity, as numerous as the sands of sixty Ganges rivers; they will teach it and so forth."

No sooner had he said this than many bodhisattvas emerged from beneath the earth and paid homage to the Tathāgata Prabhūtaratna, to Śākyamuni, and to those who had come from the ten directions. Then those bodhisattva mahāsattvas, having emerged from beneath the earth, paid homage to the tathāgatas and praised them with many kinds of bodhisattva praises, until fifty intermediate eons had fully passed. Throughout those fifty intermediate eons the Blessed One Śākyamuni remained silent, and the four assemblies too sat in silence throughout those fifty intermediate eons. Then the Blessed One performed a miraculous display of such a kind that, by his performing it, the four assemblies perceived the time as no more than a single afternoon.

"Such, always, is my power of blessing:
For thousands of koṭis of inconceivable eons
I do not move away from this Vulture Peak,
And elsewhere too I have koṭis of resting places.
When beings see this world system
Being burned up by the eon and so conceive it,
Then this buddha-field of mine
Is completely filled with gods and humans.
They play and enjoy all kinds of delight;
There are parks, mansions, and great celestial palaces;
It is adorned with mountains of jewels, and likewise
With trees laden with flowers and fruit.
Above, the gods beat great drums
And let fall a rain of māndārava flowers
Upon the other wise ones who have entered awakening here,
And upon me and the śrāvakas.
Though this field of mine always remains thus,
Others conceive of it as burned up by the eon.
They see the world system as utterly terrifying,
Filled with and tormented by hundreds of sorrows.
For many koṭis of years they never hear
Even the names of the tathāgatas,
Of the Dharma, or of my community of the Saṅgha:
Such is the fruit of evil deeds.
But when gentle and tender beings
Are born here in this human world,
As soon as they are born thus through virtuous deeds,
They see me now expounding the teachings.
The Tathāgata's lifespan is immeasurable."

After explaining that his passing into nirvāṇa is entirely a display, the Blessed One declared: "Ajita, when a son or daughter of good family hears this Dharma discourse that teaches the measure of the Tathāgata's lifespan and has faith in it with superior intention, then this should be known as the mark of that person's superior intention: namely, that he sees me dwelling on Vulture Peak, surrounded by a host of bodhisattvas and faced by a host of bodhisattvas, sitting in the midst of the Saṅgha of śrāvakas and teaching the Dharma; that he sees this Sahā world system, my buddha-field, with its ground made of beryl and level, marked out in a checkerboard with golden cords, and brilliantly adorned with jewel trees; and that he sees bodhisattvas dwelling here in multistoried mansions made for their enjoyment. Ajita, this should be known as the mark of superior intention of a son or daughter of good family who has faith with superior intention."

"Sons of good family, in brief, all the qualities of the Buddha, all that is the Buddha's lordly majesty, and all the profound points of the Buddha's teaching have been explained by me in this Dharma discourse."

"A son or daughter of good family who desires this unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening should set a big toe alight before the stūpas of the Tathāgata. One should set alight a finger, or a single toe, or one limb, or a hand. A son or daughter of good family who has entered the bodhisattva vehicle in this way generates a far greater store of merit; giving away entirely a kingdom, children, wife, or a trichiliocosm is not equal to it. Among all the sūtras taught by the Tathāgata, this Dharma discourse, the Saddharmapuṇḍarīka, is supreme."

The bodhisattva Samantabhadra said, "In order to protect whoever practices this Dharma discourse, I will take a white elephant with six tusks and go there."

At that time the Blessed One, surrounded by the four assemblies and gazed upon by them, honored and revered, taught the Dharma discourse called the Great Exposition, a great and most extensive sūtra, an instruction for bodhisattvas, embraced by all the buddhas.

From the Eye of Space: The Blessed One declared, "Great King, in the future such conditions will arise that the Tathāgata will lay down training rules for the śrāvakas: in order to subdue harmful persons who show no respect, in order that those with a sense of shame may dwell in the experience of ease, in order to abandon the defilements of this life, in order to restrain the defilements of the next life, and in order that my teaching may long endure. For some persons who have strayed onto a wrong path, the penalty of bodily exhaustion is imposed. For some, the penalty of meeting with what is unpleasant. For some, the penalty of being expelled outside the boundary and made to live apart. For some, the penalty of being placed at the lowest rank of monks. For some, the penalty is imposed of being deserving of censure and reproach by the whole of the Buddha's teaching. And I entrust the training rules of the śrāvakas into the hands of you, the kṣatriyas of the present, and of all those of the future.

"Why is that? Because in the present and in the future there will appear within my teaching persons who have strayed onto a wrong path, who are hard to tame, who have cast off shame and embarrassment, and whose conduct is impure. There will be those who, by the power of relatives, the power of wealth, the might of householders, the power of much learning, or the power of their tongues, behave faultily toward the trainings, and who, showing the marks of one who lives in a household and the enjoyments of a household, gather possessions about them. Since monks who have few concerns cannot swiftly expel them from the Saṅgha or punish them according to the trainings as laid down, I entrust them into the hands of the kṣatriyas, to be dealt with according to the training rules as laid down. Those kṣatriyas will punish such persons according to the rules as laid down.

"Those kṣatriyas who, in the present and in the future, up to the final five hundred years, by such diligence and by overpowering opponents, protect and guard my teaching, make the way of the Dharma blaze, and keep the lineage of the Three Jewels unbroken, are protectors of the Buddha's teaching, and the gods too will protect and guard them. Those kṣatriyas will also hold their kingdoms for a long time. Their merit too will greatly increase. And thereafter, for a hundred eons, they will practice the conduct of awakening.

"Those kṣatriyas who, in the present and in the future, live neglecting my teaching are forsaken by all the gods and abandoned by all the śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas; such a kṣatriya, having gone on to the next world, will not even be born among gods or humans. Whoever neglects my teaching has set about cutting off the lineage of the Three Jewels, has set about extinguishing the lamp of the Dharma, has set about drying up the water of the Dharma, has set about plucking out the Dharma eye of all beings.

"Great King, what do you think? If some person were to pluck out the eyes of all beings, how much demerit would that person incur?"

King Bimbisāra shed tears and said, "That person would increase in demerit that is incomparable, incalculable and immeasurable."

The Blessed One declared, "Great King, those kṣatriyas who, in the present and in the future, while my teaching blessed by all the tathāgatas still blazes, do not protect persons who abide by the Dharma and do not discipline according to the Dharma persons who dwell on a wrong path, increase in demerit far more than that. Even if such a kṣatriya had earlier, for a hundred eons, given gifts, kept moral discipline, and so on up to cultivating wisdom, and had entered by any one of the three vehicles, still, because of neglecting the holy Dharma, all the roots of virtue he had previously gathered will be entirely exhausted and used up. In his land three things will arise: famine, strife and disease. The gods too will neglect and forsake him. In his land his commands will not hold, and deceit, trickery and lies of every kind will arise. Foreign armies will plunder his heaps of wealth. Or it will be burned by fire. Or it will be carried off by water. Before long that kṣatriya will be struck by a grave illness and will be parted from all that is dear and pleasing. And after death he will be born in the hells. If, rarely, once in a hundred times and after a long while, he obtains a human existence through good conduct, he will soon become blind and be deprived of all wealth and all enjoyments. He will dwell in misery and live long in it. And after death he will again be born in the hells."

"... When this is examined, it is impossible for him to obtain a human body even at a time when a buddha appears. As with the kṣatriyas, so too with the brahmins and the vaiśyas: whether one is chief of a city, chief of a district, chief of a mountain retreat, or chief of a province, whoever, out of heedlessness or for the sake of wealth, does not protect to the best of his ability the persons who abide by the Dharma within my teaching, and does not punish according to the Dharma and the Vinaya the persons who dwell on a wrong path, will all be possessed of such an obscuration of karma."

King Bimbisāra said, "Blessed One, in the present and in times to come it will be hard for kṣatriyas to obtain the human state, since they mostly live intoxicated and heedless. Those who, for the sake of the long endurance of the holy Dharma and of keeping the lineage of the Three Jewels unbroken, do not take as objects of veneration those who abide by the Dharma, who possess moral discipline and are rich in good qualities, and who do not subdue with punishment in accord with the Dharma those who dwell on a wrong path and have cast off shame and embarrassment, will come to ruin."

The young god Aruṇa, the Dawn, was born through the power of aspiration among the gods of the realm of the Four Great Kings, and he travels ahead of the sun at a distance of ten thousand yojanas. His celestial palace measures twenty yojanas. In a single day and night, in the four continents and the eighty thousand intermediate continents, the sixty thousand continents of the nāgas, the forty thousand continents of the asuras, the hundred continents of the garuḍas, the five thousand two hundred continents of the kinnaras, the four thousand six hundred continents of the mahoragas, the eight hundred continents of the kumbhāṇḍas, the eighty-four thousand continents of the pretas, and the three thousand continents of the piśācas, pūtanas and kaṭapūtanas, in each and every continent he brings to maturity many hundreds of thousands of koṭis of niyutas of living beings.

Within this Jambudvīpa, in the great ocean of its eastern quarter, there is a mountain made entirely of beryl, called Arṇapa, which rises twenty yojanas up out of the water. On that mountain there is a cave called Most Variegated, where the bodhisattvas of former times have dwelt, one yojana in height and in breadth; in that cave dwells the jantu. It has thoroughly trained itself in the loving-kindness held in common with the śrāvakas. There is a second cave, called Amara, one yojana in height and breadth; in that cave dwells the horse. There is a third cave, called Well Established, one yojana in height and in breadth; in that cave dwells the sheep. On that mountain dwell a tree deity called Victorious Intelligence and a great rākṣasī called Lovely Throat. Both of them, each with a retinue of five thousand, act as patrons of those three who belong to the animal birth, and day and night they constantly strive to honor them.

In this Jambudvīpa, in the ocean of its southern quarter, there is a mountain made entirely of crystal, called Glory of Beings. It rises twenty yojanas up out of the water. On that mountain, in a cave called Fine Color, one yojana in breadth and height, dwells the monkey. In a second cave, called Excellent Adornment, one yojana in breadth and height, dwells the rooster. In a third cave, called Dharmapaha, one yojana in breadth and height, dwells the dog. On that mountain dwell the rākṣasīs worthy of offerings called Radiance and Near Radiance, with a retinue of five thousand, and they act as patrons.

In the ocean of the western quarter of Jambudvīpa there is a mountain made entirely of gold, called Moon of Awakening, which rises twenty yojanas up from there. On that mountain, in a cave called Milantra, one yojana in height and breadth, dwells the pig. In a second cave, called Fragrant Glory, one yojana in height and breadth, dwells the rat. In a third cave, called Udmaśrī, one yojana in height and breadth, dwells the ox. On that mountain the great rākṣasī called Well Guarded and one called Calf Surākṣa, with retinues as large as before, and a wind god called Attendant, with a retinue as large as before, act as patrons as before.

In the ocean of the northern quarter of Jambudvīpa there is a mountain made entirely of Jambu-river gold, called Mark of the Crest's Glory, which rises twenty yojanas up out of the water. On that mountain, in a cave called Arunagengola, of the same size as before, dwells the lion. In a second cave, called Laṃkara, of the same size as before, dwells the hare. In a third cave, called Arising in Joy, of the same size as before, dwells the snake. On that mountain the rākṣasī called Conch Bearer and one called Śata the Sage, with retinues as large as before, and a water god called Bringer of Rain, with a retinue as large as before, act as patrons in the same way as at the earlier places.

These twelve who have gone to the animal birth have great merit and great fortune. Through the power of their aspiration they bring beings to maturity every day and night. They are the rat, the ox, the tiger, the hare, the elephant, the jantu, the horse, the sheep, the monkey, the rooster, the dog and the pig; these have attained the quality of being worshipped by gods and humans.

In the first instant, the seed consciousness abides in the mother's womb in the jelly-like stage, small, the size of a grain of white mustard seed. In the sixth week, from the jelly-like stage it becomes curd-like, small, the size of a juniper seed. After another week, from the curd-like stage it becomes fleshy, the size of a walnut. In the eighth week, from the fleshy stage it becomes firm, the size of a bilva fruit. Then, as the weeks pass, up to the thirteenth week, the signs of the head, the hands, the thighs and the private parts appear. In the twentieth week the signs of male and female appear. In the twenty-first week the bones are complete. In the thirtieth week all the limbs, the blood, the skin and the pores of the body hair are complete. Then it dwells in the mother's belly amid blood and foul smells.

While it dwells in the mother's belly it remembers its former lives and becomes utterly weary, thinking, "When will I get out of this mother's belly? Afterwards I shall apply firm diligence and vigor to such virtues that I will never again be born in a mother's womb." But through being oppressed by the mother's body, through the touch of the ground, the touch of hands, and the suffering of being washed of impurities, which weigh heavily on body and mind, it forgets its former lives entirely.

Then all, from the lord of the gods down to the lord of the piśācas, those who move in the sky and those who move on the earth, said with one voice: "Blessed One, this is our wish. Sugata, this is our wish: in times to come, as long as the way of the Dharma blessed by the Tathāgata blazes, and as long as the śrāvakas of the Blessed One, holding on to no possessions in any way, delight in the Dharma, for that long we will set aside our own affairs and protect the Dharma and Vinaya taught by the Tathāgata. We will also protect and care for the persons who abide by the Dharma, rich in moral discipline and perfect in good qualities, together with their retinues and all their belongings.

"Why is that? Blessed One, as long as the śrāvakas of the Blessed One, holding on to no possessions in any way, abide by the Dharma and practice the Dharma in accordance with the Dharma, for that long the vitality of the earth, the vitality of beings and the vitality of the Dharma will remain without declining. For that long the gods, nāgas and yakṣas will delight in this human world. For that long beings, when they die and pass on, will be born among gods and humans in states renowned for great power. For that long the gods will be filled with beings renowned for great power. For that long the bodhisattvas who abide by the Dharma will remain. For that long our strength and endeavor will prevail in crushing the side of what is not Dharma. For that long beings will be capable of faith in the Tathāgata's teaching.

"Venerable Blessed One, in times to come there will appear śrāvakas of the Blessed One who hold possessions, who have abandoned the dwellings in hermitages, forest outskirts and remote places where one abides by the practice of yogic attention, who are jealous and the like, who live in crowds, who have cast off the trainings, shame and embarrassment, who mix with householders, and who delight in gain and honor. Proud toward one another through the backing of householders and the strength of their heaps of wealth, and delighting in strife, they will quarrel even in front of householders and will not guard against harsh words and lies. At that time the householders, from the kṣatriyas and brahmins down to those who live in the provinces and districts, will lose faith in the Blessed One's teaching in great numbers, and even the faith of those in whom faith in the Sugata's teaching had arisen will be cut off. When such a time, the exhaustion of the bright side, has drawn near, the bodhisattva mahāsattvas will gladly go to other world systems. The gods and yakṣas who abide by the Dharma and are renowned for great power will remain in equanimity, or will come away from there. At that time we will be unable to repel the evil yakṣas, the kaṭapūtanas and so on down to evil humans, and at that time we will be unable to avert the exhaustion of the bright side or the many kinds of danger."

From the Saṃdhinirmocana: "Beings superimpose the imputed nature onto the dependent nature and the perfected nature, and then they conventionally designate the dependent nature and the perfected nature in terms of the imputed nature. In whatever ways they conventionally designate, in those ways their minds are thoroughly imbued with the conventional designations, and through being bound up with conventional designation, or through the latent tendencies of conventional designation, they cling to the dependent nature and the perfected nature as having the character of the imputed nature. In whatever ways they cling, in just those ways, through that cause and that condition of clinging to the dependent nature as the imputed nature, they bring forth the dependent nature again in the future. On that basis they are thoroughly afflicted by the three kinds of affliction, and they circle and wander through the five classes of destiny and do not pass beyond saṃsāra.

"If a single eye consciousness arises at one time, then a single conceptual mental consciousness, sharing the field of activity of the eye consciousness, also arises at that one time. If two, three, four, or all five collections of consciousness arise at one time, then a single conceptual mental consciousness, sharing the field of activity of the five collections of consciousness, also arises at that one time.

"Maitreya, that the dependent characteristic and the perfected characteristic are in every way utterly free of the imputed characteristic of affliction and purification, and that in this even that is not apprehended: this is called, in the Great Vehicle, the complete characteristic of emptiness."

From the Sūryagarbha: "In this Jambudvīpa there is this stūpa, the Support of All, the abode of sages and munis, where many buddhas, bodhisattvas, pratyekabuddhas and śrāvakas of the past have dwelt, and where in the future too they will dwell. So that the great teaching may long endure, the perfectly complete buddhas of the past entrusted this Support of All, the abode of sages and munis, into the hands of Varuṇa. I too entrust it into his very hands. He too will rejoice, and the persons who abide by the Dharma and earnestly apply themselves to yogic attention will be brought to maturity. And those givers and patrons who earnestly apply themselves to serving the persons who abide by the Dharma will also be protected."

The nāga king Varuṇa said, "So be it."

In the continent of Godānīya, on the Mountain of Dust, the Variegated Abode of the Sages and Munis was entrusted to the nāga king Increase of Jewels. In the continent of Videha, on the Cattle Meadow Mountain, the Source of Sages was entrusted to the nāga king Moon Guardian. In the continent of Kuru, on the Mountain of Victorious Joy, the Heap of Incense was entrusted to the nāga king Needle That Pierces the Body. In the continent of Godānīya, the Source of Light was entrusted to the nāga king Cow Head. In the eastern continent, the Lotus Tree like a Nyagrodha was entrusted to the nāga king Thousand Forms. In the continent of Kuru, the Light of the Heap of Incense was entrusted to the nāga king Who Roams in Dwellings. Within the great ocean, the Radiance of the Jewel Essence was entrusted to the nāga king Sāgara. On the summit of Sumeru, in the abode of the Thirty-Three, the Essence of Fully Blossomed Flowers was entrusted to the nāga king Airāvaṇa. In Jambudvīpa, at the place called Increase of Joy, the Cave of the Elder was entrusted to the nāga king Great Purifier. In Vaiśālī, the Most Stable was entrusted to the nāga king, the son of Vāsuki. In Kapilavastu, the Golden Lamp of Sweet Fragrance was entrusted to the nāga king Anavatapta. In the land of Magadha, the Great Victor was entrusted to the nāga king Given by the Mountain. In Mathurā, the Lovely Cloud was entrusted to the nāga king Who Roams Like a Jackal. In the kingdom of Kosala, the Pure Victor was entrusted to the nāga king Worm. Beyond the cave, the Mucilinda Fragrance of Truth was entrusted to the nāga king Mucilinda. In the kingdom of Dharaṇīṃdhara, the Provision for the Path of Seeing was entrusted to the nāga king Elāpattra. In the land of Kashmir, the Saffron Peak was entrusted to the nāga king Huluhulu. In the land of Water Taking, the Essence of Koṭis of Light Rays was entrusted to the nāga king Pendant. In China, the Light of Nārāyaṇa was entrusted to the king Given by the Ocean. In the land of Kaśa, at the abode of the Earth's Breast, near Mount Gośṛṅga, on the bank of the Gomatī River, the Gomasālagandha was entrusted to the nāga king Given by the Householder.

If he looks in every direction, he will see the Tathāgata everywhere, throughout all of space. If he looks at him as small, he sees the Tathāgata as small. If he looks at him as immeasurable, he sees him as immeasurable. He thinks, "From where has this Tathāgata come?" And he thinks, "The Tathāgata has not come from anywhere. He goes nowhere." He does not apprehend the Tathāgata going anywhere or coming from anywhere. Again he thinks, "These three realms, the cause of the body, are mind only; therefore, however anyone conceives them, so they are seen. I make the Buddha with my own mind. With my mind I see the Buddha. My mind itself is the Buddha. My mind itself is the body. Mind itself is also space. Through the conceptualizing of my mind I see the Buddha. Mind does not see mind. Those who perceive things as mind are without knowledge. Those who perceive things as mind are bound up with it. Its reversal is without essence. Because these phenomena are without knowledge, they have not truly arisen." Whoever has first generated the mind set on awakening, and thinks, "The wisdom by which emptiness is realized is itself empty of intrinsic nature," obtains, through that attention to the object, the samādhi in which the buddhas of the present stand face to face. And yet this is also the śrāvaka vehicle: by meditating thus on all forms as signless, there is the purification of penetrating insight, the purification of freedom from desire, the purification of ignorance.

From the Sūtra of Thorough Analysis: "Śāriputra, this Jambudvīpa will become filled with such people, who ruin both themselves and others through the livelihood of those who are not excellent persons, through laxity, and through taking up quarrels.

"Furthermore, Śāriputra, the Blessed One Kāśyapa too made this prophecy: 'The scripture of the Tathāgata Śākyamuni will swiftly decline through gain and honor.' Śāriputra, this Dharma and Vinaya will swiftly decline through gain and honor. Śāriputra, a monk who teaches an impure Dharma, not knowing what the Tathāgata taught with a hidden intent, teaches a Dharma taken from others. Where the Tathāgata has taught the Dharma that there is no sentient being, no life principle, no nourisher and no person, he teaches about these things, saying, 'It is a self,' or 'It is a sentient being,' or 'It is a nourisher,' or 'It is a person.' By this he disparages me, abandons the Dharma, and speaks ill of the Saṅgha. The karmic maturation of that cannot be known by the world with its gods; only the Tathāgata knows it. Śāriputra, you should believe this and understand it: how much unwholesome effort is possessed by that monk who teaches an impure Dharma.

"Śāriputra, what do you think? Are the beings of Jambudvīpa many?"

He said, "Blessed One, they are many."

He declared, "Śāriputra, if someone were to kill the beings of Jambudvīpa, would he not on that basis amass many roots of unwholesomeness?"

He said, "Many, Blessed One. Many, Sugata."

He declared, "One who, not knowing the Buddha's awakening, teaches an impure Dharma to others amasses still more unwholesome qualities than that. Why is that? Whoever rejects the Buddha's awakening disparages the buddhas, the blessed ones, of the past, the future and the present. Why is that? Because all the tathāgatas of the past, the future, the present and the ten directions teach only ultimate emptiness, and teach the Dharma that there is no self, no sentient being, no life principle, no nourisher and no person. Śāriputra, this teaching of the Dharma of ultimate emptiness by the buddhas, the blessed ones, is the very finest: namely, the qualities of the Buddha are perishing of their own accord, without essential nature, without any object; they are emptiness.

"In the past there appeared a tathāgata called Great Array. After he had passed into parinirvāṇa his relics were widely spread, just as mine will be in the future."

His true Dharma remained for five hundred years, just as mine will in the future. Śāriputra, while the Tathāgata is present, there is one Buddha Jewel, one Dharma Jewel and one Saṅgha Jewel. After he has passed into parinirvāṇa, the teaching will become fivefold.

Śāriputra, in this teaching there will come such times when Māra the Evil One, emanating in the form of monks, will teach wrong views of many kinds and establish many people in them. There he will teach wrong views such as these: the basis of the mind, the basis concordant with the five, the basis concordant with the momentary, the basis of those who hold that everything exists, the basis of the person, and the basis of the object of focus. Śāriputra, they will teach such wrong views, which neither the buddhas nor the śrāvakas have taught.

He declared: Śāriputra, I would gladly stay, in any posture whatever, in a place filled with rotting snake corpses, rotting dog corpses or rotting human corpses. But, Śāriputra, with monks whose moral discipline is broken, whose conduct has fallen away, whose livelihood has fallen away and whose view has fallen away, I would not gladly stay for a single night, a single day, a single instant, not even for as long as a snap of the fingers.

Śāriputra, when those fools hear a sūtra such as this, spoken by the Tathāgata, they will be afraid, they will be terrified, they will be utterly dismayed; they will think of it as a precipice, they will think of it as a great precipice. Why is that? Because this sūtra explains the faults of broken discipline without leaving any out.

Śāriputra, if one asks by what cause and what condition someone is called "one of broken discipline": it is because they practice a broken discipline that they are called one of broken discipline. They are called one of broken discipline because of indulging in words and speech, indulging in the three times, indulging in many kinds of occupation, indulging in much talk, delighting in sleep, delighting in bustle, delighting in elaboration, speaking unreasonable, improper talk; grasping at a self, at a sentient being, at a life principle and at a person; failing to honor father, mother, monks and brahmins; practicing as a physician; corrupting households; going as a messenger; delighting in household occupations; mixing with householders; acting as servants to householders; destroying leaves, flowers and fruit; giving full ordination to those not yet twenty years old; giving full ordination to those with incomplete limbs; accepting raw grain; accepting raw meat; accepting gold and silver; lying; lax deportment; little patience; ingratitude; little faith; torpor; much sleep; much regret; anger; much deceit and guile; great jealousy; lacking shame and lacking conscience; conceit, arrogance, haughtiness and pomposity; being bloated with food; being puffed up by an unabashed mind; the seven kinds of pride; hypocrisy, flattery, hinting, coercion, and seeking gain through gain; having no faith in the Three Jewels; not believing in the maturation of karma; holding this life to be paramount; not holding the next life to be important; and much doubt and indecision.

Śāriputra, those who possess impure actions of body, speech and mind and an impure livelihood are like filth. When their bodies break up, after death they will be born among the hell-beings. They are to be shunned by the buddhas, by the śrāvakas of the buddhas, and by those who seek what is virtuous, just as when red sandalwood is put into a vessel of filth, it too becomes filthy.

From the Hundred Thousand: Śāriputra, there are also bodhisattva great beings practicing the six perfections who, with the very first generation of the mind, enter the flawless state of a bodhisattva and attain the irreversible ground. Śāriputra, there are also bodhisattva great beings who, with the very first generation of the mind, fully awaken to unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening, become manifestly enlightened, turn the wheel of Dharma, accomplish the benefit of countless, immeasurable sentient beings, and pass into nirvāṇa in the realm of nirvāṇa in which no aggregates remain; and after they have passed into parinirvāṇa, their true Dharma remains for an eon or for more than an eon.

From the commentary on the Hundred Thousand: The twelfth is one who, endowed with those very qualities, generates the power of a buddhafield, has purified their own mind-stream with good qualities, and attains the first ground with little effort; then at once, without needing great exertion, they become a buddha, turn the wheel of Dharma, and cause the teaching to remain for as long a time as it lasts.

Śāntideva explained:
If something can be remedied,
why be unhappy about it?
And if it cannot be remedied,
why be unhappy about it?
One overcome by unhappiness and anger
becomes confused about the remedy.
Being discouraged, one has no enthusiasm
and is seized by great decline.
Brooding on it, even in a moment,
one's life is cut short.
Therefore, through habituation, give up
this pointless thing as you would a harm.

If one asks how the abandoning of unhappiness is achieved: it is by casting off a mind that is light and overly pliant. As the Questions of Ugra says, "casting off a mind like a tuft of cotton," and as the Gaṇḍavyūha says, "In order to vanquish all afflictions, one should generate an invincible mind."

Through habituation, nothing at all remains difficult. For example, just as all sentient beings, under the power of the afflictions, perceive what is suffering as pleasure, so too, if one sets up the perception of pleasure toward everything from which suffering arises, through habituation it abides as the very perception of pleasure. By this one attains the samādhi called Proceeding Happily in All Phenomena, and even when struck by the sufferings of the hells, the perception of pleasure remains present, as is found in the Sūtra of the Meeting of Father and Son.

From the Sūtra of Nanda's Going Forth:
Even in a thousand koṭis of eons,
in hundreds and in koṭis of niyutas,
one may not even hear the word "buddha";
how then could one see a buddha?
Just as the udumbara flower
is extremely hard to find upon a tree,
so too a buddha is hard to find.
Since you have found one,
Nanda, you will go forth.
You have found the freedom so very hard to find.
Nanda, having come to see the Teacher,
do not be displeased with me.

That yoke with a hole in it is driven by the wind and runs about in every direction. Nanda, by some chance the neck of the blind turtle might enter the hole of that yoke, but, Nanda, attaining a human existence is not like that. So too is the perfect endowment of freedom.

From the Dispeller of Darkness in the Ten Directions: In the northwestern quarter dwells the buddha called Subduer Who Vanquishes All Doubt. Seated at the foot of the tree of awakening, he made all the gods of Māra's realms throughout the trichiliocosm irreversible from unsurpassed awakening. Such was the valor of that great person. In all your activities, by day and by night, call that buddha to mind, and do not give up the mind of awakening; then no fear will arise for you.
