Bharadvāja ruled as king. Gautama took leave of his parents and went forth under the sage Kṛṣṇa. He did not adopt the sage's dwelling in the wilderness, but stayed in a hut near the city of Potala.

The courtesan Bhadrā, who lived by her beauty, consorted with a great scoundrel called Mṛṇapa and slept with him. Then, hearing talk that she was dallying with someone else, Mṛṇapa killed Bhadrā. Bhadrā's servant raised the alarm, and when everyone gave chase, Mṛṇapa left the bloodied sword at the door of the sage Gautama's hut and fled. When everyone saw the sword, they cried, "The renunciant is a murderer!" and led him away, and King Bharadvāja, without investigating, had him impaled on a stake.

Then the sage Kṛṣṇa saw this, questioned him and spoke with him. Gautama asked, "Where will I be reborn?" The sage replied, "The brahmins say that for one without offspring there is no good destiny. Do you have a son or not?" He said, "I do not." Then the sage, by his blessing, made rain and a cool wind strike his body, and said, "Call to mind your past enjoyment of desire." Two drops of semen mixed with blood fell, and from them came two eggs. They ripened and grew in the sun, and when they burst open two sons came forth and went into a sugarcane grove. The father died. Then the sage took the two and raised them. Because they had ripened in the sun, because they lived in the sugarcane grove, and because they were the sons of Gautama, they became known by three names.

Then Bharadvāja's line came to an end. When he died, the ministers said, "Let us make the elder brother, Gautama the renunciant, king," and they asked the sage Kṛṣṇa, who told them he had been killed. The two sons remained. The elder was made king, but his line came to an end. The younger was installed and became known as Ikṣvāku. From his line descended one hundred and one kings of the city of Potala. The last of these was the Ikṣvāku king Virūḍhaka. He had four sons: Talalagarṇi, Karakarṇi, Hastini Yangpa, and the youngest, Roka.

Then, when Virūḍhaka's wife died, he took the daughter of another king. That king gave her only after he had extracted a promise that, should he have a grandson, the boy would be installed as heir. Later a son was born, and when he was not installed as heir, that king threatened him, saying, "Is this right?" Virūḍhaka was afraid and asked what he should do, and the ministers said, "Banish the other sons and install this one." Then the king and the ministers, by a stratagem, banished all the sons. Because everyone loved them, people, their sisters among them, streamed out unceasingly through the gate of the city of Potala for seven days; afterwards the gate was shut and the rest were held back.

The exiles went near the Himalaya, to the bank of the river Bhāgīrathī, in the vicinity of the hermitage of the sage Kapila. There they built huts and lived by hunting deer. Then, tormented by desire, they all grew pale and gaunt, and the sage said, "Since you have fallen from the royal ways of the kṣatriyas, it is lawful for you to live with your sisters of the other household." Then the people multiplied. They all received laws from the sage, marked out a boundary on the ground with water from a golden vase, built a city and settled there. Because the sage Kapila gave the site, it was the city of Kapila, Kapilavastu. Then Virūḍhaka, hearing that they had proved able, named them the Śākyas, the Able Ones.

Then, after Virūḍhaka died, from the regent and the banished sons onward, apart from those who were installed one after another as kings of the mountain, the lines of all the others died out. Through the descent of one called Nupura Kolabulaka, there came to be one thousand and fifty-five kings of Kapilavastu. The last of them, Dhanuḥsthira, had two sons: Siṃhahanu and Siṃhanāda. Siṃhahanu had four sons: Śuddhodana, Śuklodana, Droṇodana and Amṛtodana. His daughters were Śuddhā, Śuklā, Droṇā and Amṛtā. Śuddhodana had two sons, Siddhārtha and Nanda. Śuklodana had two sons, the venerable Tiṣya and Bika. Droṇodana had two sons, the venerable Mahānāman and Aniruddha. Amṛtodana had two sons, the venerable Ānanda and Devadatta. The son of Śuddhā was Fully Aware. The son of Śuklā was Patramali. The son of Droṇā was Tali. The son of Amṛtā was Śebala. The son of the Buddha was Rāhula. The mother of the Buddha was Mahāmāyā. The younger mother was Māyā; both were daughters of King Double Measure. The mother was the elder.

When the bodhisattva departed from the abode of Tuṣita, by the nature of things he looked upon five matters: family, country, clan, time and woman. As for family, he considered which of the two, the kṣatriyas or the brahmins, was held to be foremost, for if he did not take birth there, it would not benefit the world and would not serve the welfare of beings; the rest are likewise. As for country, it is one where there is much sugarcane, many cattle and buffalo and many mendicants, where many beings are of high station, and where there are no border peoples. As for time, when the lifespan of people is above eighty thousand years, their weariness with existence is slight and they are dull; and when it has declined below a hundred, the five degenerations are strong. As for clan and woman, respectively, like the Śākyas and Mahāmāyā: a lineage honored by many and of great kin, faultless back through seven generations, wise and virtuous; and the woman must be able to carry the bodhisattva in her womb for ten months.

The six teachers are Pūraṇa Kāśyapa and the rest. The six later teachers are the brahmin Kutatildo and the rest. The six sons of ignorance are Udraka and the rest.

Because from the time the Buddha entered the womb every aim was accomplished, he is Sarvārthasiddha, Accomplisher of Every Aim. When he was led to pay homage to Śākyavardhana, the common deity of the Śākyas, the god came out to meet him and bowed to him, and so he is God above the Gods. When he set out to pay homage to the god, all the haughty and unruly Śākyas living in that city sat as though subdued, and so he is Śākyamuni, the Able One of the Śākyas.

The seven raised places are the two hands, the two shoulders and the rest.

Of the seven precious things, the wheel is made of gold, with a round hub and a thousand spokes. The jewel is a beryl that brings forth whatever is needed and desired. The elephant is ash white. The horse is blue necked. The woman is blissful to the touch. The minister offers wealth such as gold. The general is skilled in the arts of war.

By the nature of things, seven days after the bodhisattva was born his mother passed away. At twenty-nine the bodhisattva left home, and after he had practiced austerities for six years, the sage Asita perceived that the nectar was about to be won. The sage Asita lived on the mountain Kingi, and when he was dying he sent his disciple to seek the nectar; the one who went forth was Mahākātyāyana.

Just as those with little provision for the road
Set out on the way and travel in hardship,
So beings who have not done good deeds
Go to the lower destinies.
Just as those with ample provision for the road
Set out on the way and travel in ease,
So beings who have done good deeds
Go to the higher destinies.

Though life is taken through five causes, one is not stained by wrongdoing. They are these: one traveling along a road unknowingly deprives tiny creatures, ants, beetles and the like, of life; one aims a weapon at something else and, without intending it, strikes another, who loses his life; a physician wishing to help a patient, seeing no harm in it, gives medicine, and the patient dies of it; likewise a mother beats her son in order to discipline him, and he loses his life; and creatures fall against one's will into a fire one has lit. Though life is taken through these five kinds of cause, one is not stained by wrongdoing. Taking what is not given, when one has not taken the vow against taking what is not given, has a slight ripening.

The seven well-formed features of an elephant are: the four limbs and the tail being fine, the testicles lying within the groin, and the trunk being well formed.

The secondary precious thing, the sword: it goes of itself into other realms, and without striking, merely at the sight of it, all obey the king's command.

The hide is the hide of a nāga of the ocean, five yojanas wide and ten yojanas long, which the nāgas offer to the king. When it is cold it makes one warm, and when it is hot it makes one cool. It keeps unlovely women from sight.

The bed, in short, is such that if one lies on it the mind becomes workable.

The grove is one where, when the king enters it, divine birds and the like gather.

The house is moderate in cold and warmth, and at whatever hour one is to do virtue, one wakes at that time.

The garment is not harmed by fire and the like, and takes no smoke.

The boots do not sink even in water, and even walking slowly one goes a hundred yojanas.

Taking no delight in kinsfolk,
Longing to see the learned,
Renounced from the faults of the household:
Such a one is a monk.
Not watching song and dance,
Free of attachment to the hosts of faults,
Those who delight in the charnel ground:
Such a one is a monk.

Among these, through unwholesome karma one is born among the hell beings, through virtue among the gods, and through a mixture among humans.

When one takes what is not given under the pressure of the king's punishment, or for the sake of one's teacher, one's parents, or noble ones who are sick or hungry, the result is slight. Even of what is taken without being given from the Three Jewels, what belongs to the Buddha and to the Dharma is purified if it is given back in a matching share; but what is stolen from the Saṅgha is not purified unless its feeling is undergone. This taking of what is not given by one who has not taken the vow against taking what is not given has a slight ripening.

Its karma, in three ways,
Sustains one through the various states,
And forty ripenings in all
Are stirred into violent forms.

The root of all that is pointless is liquor:
It makes one the laughingstock of the hells,
All one's faculties are ruined,
And it becomes the cause of what is pointless.

Excessive delight in words,
Attachment and destruction,
Faults of speech and conceit,
And the four harsh words are likewise.

A person whose mind is utterly distracted by liquor,
Who knows not what to do and what not to do,
Is like the cattle.
Therefore give up liquor!

People distracted by liquor,
Though alive, are like the dead.
Whoever wishes to live on always
Should always give up liquor completely.

Liquor is the root of every wrong;
It always brings every aim to nothing.
It is the stairway to all the lower realms,
A great abode of black darkness.

Through liquor one goes to the hells,
And likewise to the world of hungry ghosts;
Deceived by the faults of liquor,
People go among the animals.

Among poisons, the poison is liquor,
Like the hell among the hells;
The supreme sickness among sicknesses
Is liquor, so the learned have said.

It ruins the intellect and the faculties,
It exhausts the precious Dharma,
It destroys the pure life;
Liquor is the one unfailing breeding ground.

If kings and those learned in the treatises
Are made dull by liquor,
What need is there to say that ordinary people
Who drink liquor are deceived?

For people who depend on liquor
It is the axe to every Dharma;
Supreme in making one shameless,
It is taught as a thing to be given up.

The mind that has been seized by liquor
Knows not and does not discern
What is to be done and what is not;
Such a person knows nothing at all.

Whoever depends on liquor
Is at times joyful,
At times tormented,
At times doing evil.

It breeds deep confusion in the mind
And destroys both worlds;
Like a fire to the Dharma of liberation,
It is liquor alone that burns it away.

Those people who give up liquor,
Settled evenly in the way of the Dharma,
Go to that supreme abode
Where there is no death.

Those who are deceived by drinking liquor,
Their deeds are base;
They fall into the unbearable hells.
Why be tormented to no purpose?

When it is drunk, liquor is sweet;
When it ripens, it burns fiercely.
The poison fruit among poison fruits,
So the learned have called liquor.

Thinking, "This will not harm me,"
Let the wise not put their trust in liquor.
Though cool to the taste, its ripening burns;
Liquor makes one go to hell.

Those whose minds are distracted
Take delight in doing evil deeds;
Later, in the burning of the ripening,
The foolish are tormented.

One does not set one's mind on sense pleasures;
Sense pleasures deceive above all else.
They are the fierce bonds of saṃsāra,
The cause of all the hells.

Whoever delights in sense pleasures,
His suffering has no end.
For one seized by the snake of desire,
How could there ever be happiness?

You, through unbearable desire,
Have come to the fierce hells,
Whose harm is utterly unbearable.
Why be tormented to no purpose?

When you, deceived by desire,
Committed evil,
Why did you not grow angry then?
Why are you tormented today?

The fruit appears
Of deeds done and stored up and made firm.
Since the feeling that evil brings is unbearable,
Therefore one should do no evil.

Evil, the ripening of evil,
Always ripens in the lower realms.
For one who does no evil there is no evil;
Therefore give up evil.

For one who has wholly given up evil
There is no ripening of evil.
The evil one has done oneself brings harm;
It does not come to be undergone by another.

From the noble Mahāparinirvāṇa Sūtra, on the tending of the Tathāgata's relics and the building of stūpas: The Blessed One said to Nanda and the great assembly, "After I have passed into nirvāṇa, whether in the abodes above or in the world of humans, if beings obtain my relics in their hands and, with sorrow and with joy, bow down reverently and make offerings with a mind of faith, they will gain merit that is measureless and incalculable. Nanda, whoever sees the relics of the Tathāgata sees the Buddha. Whoever sees the Buddha sees the Dharma. Whoever sees the Dharma sees the Saṅgha. Whoever sees the Saṅgha sees nirvāṇa. Therefore, Nanda, you should know that the Three Jewels are permanent, stable, unchanging and unmoving, and that they become the protector and the place of refuge of all sentient beings."

Nanda said to the Blessed One, "When the Blessed One has passed into final nirvāṇa, by what rite should we offer the Tathāgata's body to the fire, so that the relics may be obtained and honored with faith?"

The Blessed One said to Nanda, "After I have passed into nirvāṇa, you should burn me in the manner of a universal monarch."

Nanda asked again, "What is the rite for burning a universal monarch?"

The Blessed One said to Nanda, "When a universal monarch has died, he is kept for seven days, and only then is he placed inside an iron coffin. When he has been placed in the coffin, the inside of the coffin is filled with fragrant sesame oil, the opening is sealed well, and he is kept for another seven days. Then he is taken out of the coffin and washed with scented water. When he has been washed with scented water, he is honored and filled with incense, and the whole body is wrapped in cotton. Over the cotton wrapping he is wrapped, layer upon layer, in a thousand bolts of fine white cloth. When he has been wrapped in this way, the inside of the coffin is filled with fragrant sesame oil, the king's remains are put back into the coffin, and the opening of the coffin is sealed well. He is drawn onto a chariot of fragrant wood and the seven precious substances; ornaments are hung on the four sides of the chariot, and the chariot is adorned with all kinds of jewels. He is surrounded and honored with countless flowers, pennants, parasols of the seven precious substances, victory banners, all kinds of fine incense, and divine music. Then the remains of the universal monarch are burned with fragrant wood bearing designs and with fragrant oil. When he has been burned in this way, the relics are gathered, and at the crossroads of a great city a stūpa of the seven precious substances is built with four doors set out upon it; the relics are placed within it, and the whole world is allowed to behold it and rejoice."

"Nanda, a universal monarch gains the station of a king through a little merit, yet he is not freed from existence. He indulges in the five sense pleasures, together with his queens and the women of his palace; he is possessed of wrong views and the three poisons; he has not given up the afflictions and the thoroughgoing afflictions by so much as the tip of a hair. Yet when he dies, the world performs such a rite for him and builds a stūpa, and all rejoice and gaze upon it. Nanda, what need is there to speak of the Tathāgata?

"For measureless, endless, incalculable, countless eons he wholly gave up queens, the women of the palace and the five sense pleasures. Seeing the things of the world to be like frost or hail, he pressed on through arduous effort, practiced what is extremely hard to practice, and carried out the hard world-transcending practice of all bodhisattvas. Of the deeds of all the buddhas who dwell in the ten directions throughout the three times, there is nothing he did not accomplish: completely pure discipline, samādhi, wisdom, liberation, and the knowledge and vision of liberation, and the six perfections. Moreover, the ten powers of a tathāgata, great compassion, the four fearlessnesses, the three doors of liberation, the eighteen emptinesses, the six superknowledges, the five eyes, the thirty-seven factors of awakening, the eighteen unshared qualities, the thirty-two major marks, the eighty excellent minor marks, the lifespan of all buddhas, the complete purification of all buddha fields, the bringing of all beings to full maturity, all the hard practices, the discipline of gathering virtue, the discipline of working for the welfare of beings, the discipline of restraint, all good qualities, all wisdom, all ornaments, all great aspirations, all skill in means, and the rest: all merit and wisdom, inconceivable, he perfected spontaneously and completely. He cut off all nonvirtue completely, he cut off all afflictions completely, and he cut off all the habitual tendencies of the afflictions completely. Having fully realized the four truths and the twelve links of dependent origination, he subdued the four māras at the foot of the tree of awakening and became the wisdom of omniscience. Because he had diligently practiced all such sublime Dharma, all the buddhas spoke with one voice, saying, 'Excellent, excellent!' and with one accord they empowered the crown of his Dharma body with the water of the wisdom of the nature of reality, and they proclaimed, 'He has now become fully and manifestly awakened into unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening.'

"Therefore he is now the teacher of gods and humans, fully awakened throughout the ten directions, gone to the ultimate end; he is called the Blessed One, without peer among gods and humans. Because he looks upon all sentient beings as he looks upon Rāhula, he is called the Tathāgata, the perfectly complete one, endowed with knowledge and conduct, the Sugata, knower of the world, the unsurpassed, the tamer and guide of beings to be tamed, the teacher of gods and humans, the Buddha, the Blessed One. When he has done all the deeds of loving care for the world and passes into nirvāṇa for the sake of beings, then, in keeping with the ways of the world, it should be arranged that all beings make offerings to him as to a universal monarch.

"Nanda, when I pass into nirvāṇa, keep me for seven days as is done for a universal monarch, and only then place me in an iron coffin. When I have been placed in the iron coffin, fill it inside with fragrant oil and seal the opening well. Decorate and adorn the coffin richly with the seven precious substances, and all around the coffin make offerings of jewel banners, incense and flowers. When you have made offerings in this way for seven days, take me out of the coffin and bathe me well with scented water. When the body of the Tathāgata has been bathed well with scented water in this way, wrap the body well in cotton, and over that wrap it in a thousand bolts of priceless white cloth. When it has been wrapped in this way, then place it in the iron coffin, fill it with fragrant oil, and seal the opening well. Then, together with gośīrṣa sandalwood, aloeswood, and every fragrant wood, set the body upon a chariot of the seven precious substances, adorn it with every kind of jewel, and draw it to the place of burning. Make offerings with countless jewel banners, countless jewel parasols, countless jewel garments, countless kinds of music, countless flowers, and incense enough to fill the sky, and cense it. All the gods, humans and the assembly should burn it with the fragrant woods, sandalwood and aloeswood, and with fragrant oil, and set it ablaze. When it has been burned in this way, the gods, humans and the fourfold assembly should gather the relics and place them in a vase of the seven precious substances; and if they build a stūpa of the seven precious substances at the crossroads of a great city and make offerings to the relics, beings will gain great merit, give up the suffering of the three realms of existence, and attain the bliss of nirvāṇa.

"Nanda, when the fourfold assembly has built a stūpa of the seven precious substances for the relics of the Buddha, they should build three other stūpas as well. What are they? They are these: the stūpa of a pratyekabuddha, the stūpa of an arhat, and the stūpa of a universal monarch, so that the world may know the places in which to go for refuge."

Nanda said, "Blessed One, when the Tathāgata comes into the world, out of love for sentient beings he proclaims throughout the three realms, with the eightfold voice of Brahmā, the ten powers, great compassion, the ten fearlessnesses, the twelve links of dependent origination, the four truths and the three doors of liberation, and with the light of loving-kindness in its five colors he illuminates the six paths of destiny. Then, according to the dispositions of beings, some come to practice the four fruits and the two vehicles. Some attain the undefiled, unconditioned awakening of a pratyekabuddha. Some tread the ground of awakening that is cessation and non-arising. Some attain the measureless dhāraṇīs. Some attain the five eyes and the six superknowledges. Some are freed from the three lower realms. Some become free of the eight unfree states. Some become free of the suffering of the three worlds of gods and humans. Through the pure force of the Tathāgata's loving-kindness and the inconceivable body of the Tathāgata's Dharma of liberation, right up to his passing into nirvāṇa, if the world with its gods and humans and the fourfold assembly build a stūpa of the seven precious substances and make offerings to the relics, they will gain great merit, and ordinary beings will be freed from the suffering of the three realms and will enter true liberation. Therefore, when the Buddha passes into nirvāṇa, it is fitting that the world with its gods and humans and the whole assembly build a stūpa of the seven precious substances and make offerings to the relics, in order to hold in mind the great kindness of what the Buddha has done and his measureless loving-kindness. But, Blessed One, what benefit comes to beings from building the three kinds of stūpa, that stūpas should be built and offerings made to them?"

The Blessed One said to Nanda, "Pratyekabuddhas, having realized the most profound Dharma of dependent origination, are freed from all existence; free of every fault of wrongdoing, they have become a field of merit for gods and humans. Therefore, if one builds a stūpa for them and makes offerings, one gains merit, because, next after the Tathāgata, they bring many beings to attain the supreme fruit. Nanda, arhats have exhausted the course of birth; they take no further existence, they have perfected the pure life, and they have become a field of merit for the world. Therefore the merit gained by building a stūpa for them and making offerings is great, next after that of the pratyekabuddha, for they too bring beings to liberation. Nanda, a universal monarch, though he is not freed from the afflictions of the three realms, rules the four continents by the force of his merit, and because he keeps beings in the practice of the ten virtuous actions for as long as he can, he is an object of reverence for those beings. Therefore, if the fourfold assembly build a stūpa for him and make offerings, they will gain measureless merit."

Again Nanda said, "Blessed One, when the Buddha has passed into nirvāṇa, where should we, the fourfold assembly, offer the Tathāgata's body to the fire so that the relics of the Tathāgata may be obtained? Blessed One, please teach us this."

The Blessed One said to Nanda, "When the Buddha has passed into nirvāṇa, if the fourfold assembly burn me inside the city of Kuśinagara, the people of that city will suffer the penalties of royal action, and measureless disputes will arise, and not all will gain merit equally. Nanda, if the fourfold assembly burn me outside the city, the world will gain merit equally."

Nanda said, "Blessed One, after the Buddha has passed into nirvāṇa and been offered to the fire, when the fourfold assembly have gathered the relics and placed them in a jeweled vase, where should the stūpa of the seven precious substances be built so that all will give rise to minds of faith and make offerings? Blessed One, please teach us this."

The Blessed One said to Nanda, "When the Buddha has passed into nirvāṇa and been burned, the fourfold assembly should gather the relics and place them in a vase of the seven precious substances. At the crossroads within the city of Kuśinagara they should build, of the seven precious substances, a stūpa with three terraced tiers and its rings, adorned and decorated with every kind of jewel, richly adorned with the banners that all the world holds supreme, its cornices on the four sides also made of the seven precious substances and decorated everywhere with ornaments. On one tier of that stūpa a door is set on each of the four sides, and within, where the windows shine straight in, the jeweled vase is placed; and the fourfold assembly should make offerings to it. Nanda, the stūpa of a pratyekabuddha should have eleven tiers and be decorated with various jewels. Nanda, the stūpa of an arhat has four tiers, and it too is decorated with various jewels. Nanda, the stūpa of a universal monarch is also made of the seven precious substances, but it has no tiers. Why is that? Because he is not freed from the suffering of existence in the three realms."

Then Aniruddha said to the Blessed One, "When the Buddha has passed into nirvāṇa and been offered to the fire, how should the gods, humans and the fourfold assembly divide the relics of the Tathāgata, so that all may make offerings?"

Then the Blessed One said to Aniruddha, "After the nirvāṇa, the gods, humans and the rest should gather the relics of the Buddha and, with a perfectly impartial mind, divide them among all the three realms and the six classes of beings, so that they may make offerings to them."

Then Śakra (Indra), lord of the gods, said to the Blessed One, "Blessed One, I ask the Tathāgata for half of the Tathāgata's relics."

The Blessed One said to Śakra, lord of the gods, "The Tathāgata looks upon all sentient beings as upon his son Rāhula. Therefore do not say, 'I want half of the relics.' Why is that? Because he works equally for the benefit of all sentient beings."

The Blessed One said to Śakra, lord of the gods, "I will give you one canine tooth from my right jaw. Build a stūpa for it in the world of the gods and make offerings to it, and you will gain merit that knows no exhaustion."

At that time all the gods and humans lamented and wept. Then the Blessed One said to the four assemblies, "Gods, humans and all of you, do not grieve over the Buddha's passing into nirvāṇa. Why is that? Even though the Buddha passes into nirvāṇa, the relics remain to receive offerings, and there remain besides the unsurpassed jewel of the Buddha, the sūtras, the Vinaya and the Abhidharma. Therefore the Three Jewels and the four truths are always present in the world, and there is a place where beings may make offerings with faith. Why is that? To make offerings to the relics is the jewel of the Buddha. Whoever sees the Buddha sees the Dharma body. Whoever sees the Dharma sees the noble ones. Whoever sees the noble ones sees the four truths. Whoever sees the four truths sees nirvāṇa. Therefore you should know that the Three Jewels are permanent, abiding, unchanging and unmoving, and that they become the dwelling place and the support of the world."

The Blessed One said to the great assembly, "Do not give way to great grief, tormented by suffering. It is certain that I shall go to nirvāṇa. If you have any doubt concerning discipline, or abiding, or the permanent or the impermanent, or the Three Jewels, or the four truths, or the six perfections, or the twelve links of dependent origination, ask quickly. This questioning is the last questioning, so that there may be no regret after the Buddha has passed into nirvāṇa." Three times he said this.

At that time the four assemblies and the rest, their minds tormented by grief, stifled their cries; it weighed upon their minds almost to despair, and they swooned with grief. Through the blessing of the Buddha they wiped away their tears and said nothing at all, nor did they ask anything. Why was that? Because all these four assemblies had directly realized discipline, abiding, the Three Jewels and the four truths, and were free of doubt.

Then the Blessed One, knowing in his heart that the four assemblies had no doubt, said, "Excellent, excellent, most excellent! You four assemblies have fully realized the Three Jewels and the four truths, and to be without doubt is like washing away stains with clear water. You too must exert yourselves with diligence, and you will quickly attain the path of release. Do not grieve to the point of swooning."

At that time the Blessed One, still seated on the lion throne, set aside his outer robe with his golden hand and bared his lion-like chest with its śrīvatsa mark, the color of refined gold. He showed it to the great assembly and said to the gods, humans and the great assembly, "Look at my body, the color of refined gold!" Then the four assemblies and all present gazed without blinking at the body of the Tathāgata, the color of refined gold, and they became supremely blissful, like monks who are versed in the threefold restraint.

At that time the Blessed One, having shown the great assembly his body the color of refined gold, sent forth hundreds of thousands of trillions of light rays of the great nirvāṇa, measureless and incalculable. He illuminated all the world systems of the ten directions and outshone even the light of the sun and moon. Having sent forth this light, he said to the great assembly, "For your sake the Tathāgata practiced what is extremely hard to practice for countless eons. He cut off the limbs of his body and even his feet, and he practiced every austerity that is extremely hard to practice. Through the power of his aspiration of great compassion, he became fully awakened into unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening in this world system of the five degenerations, and he attained a body indestructible like a vajra, the color of refined gold, possessed of the thirty-two major marks and the eighty excellent minor marks, illuminating everything with measureless light. There is no one who is not liberated merely by being touched by its light or by seeing that body."

Again the Blessed One said to the great assembly, "The coming of a buddha into the world is extremely rare, like the udumbara flower: it is very hard to see. You must not let this, your very last sight of my body, be in vain. By the power of my former aspirations I came to this Sahā world system, and now that I have accomplished the welfare of those to be tamed, I am close to passing into nirvāṇa. You and all the rest, gaze well with one-pointed mind at my body the color of refined gold. If you too apply yourselves diligently to pure deeds, you will attain this fruit."

At that time the Blessed One spoke in the same way again and again, three times, and showed the great assembly his body like refined gold. Then he rose from the golden throne of the seven precious substances, ascended into the sky above to the height of seven palm trees, and spoke again: "I am close to passing into final nirvāṇa. You and all the great assembly, with thoroughly pure intention, look at my body the color of refined gold!" Seven times he said this. Then he descended from the sky and sat upon the lion throne, and again he said to the great assembly, "I am close to passing into final nirvāṇa. You and all the rest, with thoroughly pure intention, look at my body the color of refined gold!" Then once more the Blessed One rose from the lion throne, ascended into the sky to the height of one palm tree, and said to the great assembly, "I am close to passing into final nirvāṇa. You and all the rest, with pure intention, look at my body the color of refined gold!" In the same way, ascending to the height of seven palm trees, seven times he said, "I am close to passing into final nirvāṇa. You and all the great assembly, look at my body the color of refined gold!"

From the Ratnakaraṇḍa:

Those whose minds are stripped bare, without shame and without scruple,
Those who lack such good qualities as these, and lack truthful words,
Go about the world more naked than the naked.

Better to live on dead creatures' remains;
Not so a wicked livelihood.
Better it is to die in the face of battle;
Not so to live on in defeat.
It is not by the former host that one conquers;
It is by the host of heroes that one conquers.

So it says.

In the noble Perfection of Wisdom in Eighteen Thousand Lines it is declared: "Subhūti, if sentient beings were entities and not non-entities, then those sentient beings for whose sake the Tathāgata, the Arhat, the perfectly complete Buddha turned the wheel of Dharma would not pass into final nirvāṇa in the element of nirvāṇa without remainder of the aggregates. Subhūti, it is because sentient beings are non-entities and not existent entities that those sentient beings for whose sake the Tathāgata turned the wheel of Dharma pass into final nirvāṇa, have passed into final nirvāṇa, and will pass into final nirvāṇa in the element of nirvāṇa without remainder of the aggregates."

And again: "Subhūti, if the thirty-two marks of a great being of the Tathāgata, the Arhat, the perfectly complete Buddha were entities and not non-entities, then the light, the complexion, the splendor and the glory of the Tathāgata, the Arhat, the perfectly complete Buddha would not outshine the world with its gods, humans and asuras, and would not be beautiful, radiant, resplendent and blazing. Subhūti, it is because the thirty-two marks of a great being of the Tathāgata, the Arhat, the perfectly complete Buddha are non-entities and not existent entities that the light, the complexion, the splendor and the glory of the Tathāgata, the Arhat, the perfectly complete Buddha outshine the world with its gods, humans and asuras, and are beautiful, radiant, resplendent and blazing."

And again: "Subhūti, if the wheel of Dharma of the Tathāgata, the Arhat, the perfectly complete Buddha were an entity and not a non-entity, then the Tathāgata, the Arhat, the perfectly complete Buddha would not turn the wheel of Dharma that no monk, brahmin, god, māra, Brahmā, or anyone at all in the world is able to turn in accordance with the Dharma. Subhūti, it is because the wheel of Dharma of the Tathāgata, the Arhat, the perfectly complete Buddha is a non-entity and not an existent entity that the Tathāgata, the Arhat, the perfectly complete Buddha turns the wheel of Dharma that no monk, brahmin, god, māra, Brahmā, or anyone at all in the world is able to turn in accordance with the Dharma."

And again: "Subhūti, if the vajra-like wisdom of a bodhisattva great being were an entity and not a non-entity, then, by this Great Vehicle, the bodhisattva great being would not realize the habitual tendencies, rebirth-linking, and all the afflictions to be non-entities, would not attain the wisdom of omniscience endowed with the supreme of all aspects, and would not outshine the world with its gods, humans and asuras and go forth. Subhūti, it is because the vajra-like wisdom of a bodhisattva great being is a non-entity and not an existent entity that the bodhisattva great being realizes the habitual tendencies, rebirth-linking, and all the afflictions to be non-entities, attains the wisdom of omniscience endowed with the supreme of all aspects, outshines the world with its gods, humans and asuras, and goes forth."

Subhūti said, "Venerable Śāradvatīputra, when this perfection of wisdom is being explained, if a bodhisattva great being does not hesitate and is not bewildered, then that bodhisattva great being is one of whom it should be known that, in order to protect all sentient beings and never to abandon any sentient being, he abides by this very abiding; and he abides by way of not abiding. That bodhisattva great being, namely, should be known as never separated from attention to great compassion."

Śāriputra said, "Venerable Subhūti, if you hold that whoever abides never separated from that attention, the attention to great compassion, is a bodhisattva great being, then, venerable Subhūti, all sentient beings would be bodhisattvas. Why is that? Because, venerable Subhūti, all sentient beings are never separated from attention."

Subhūti said, "Excellent, excellent, venerable Śāriputra! Having spoken true words, the venerable Śāriputra has fully grasped that very meaning, and he has hit the mark. Why is that? Venerable Śāriputra, because sentient beings do not exist, attention should be known as nonexistent. Because sentient beings are empty, attention should be known as empty. Because sentient beings are isolated, attention should be known as isolated."

"Because sentient beings have no essential nature, attention should be known as having no essential nature; and because sentient beings do not become fully and manifestly awakened, attention should be known as not becoming fully and manifestly awakened." So it says.

From the noble Buddhāvataṃsaka, the Gaṇḍavyūha, the coming forth of the qualities of the bodhisattva Avalokiteśvara:

Go, Sudhana! Amid the glorious king of waters
Stands Potalaka, fair king of mountains of the wise,
Made of precious substances, with trees and groves of many flowers,
With orchards, pools, and streams that flow.
Dwelling there amid that supreme fragrance, Avalokiteśvara
Moves about for the benefit of beings.
Go to him, Sudhana, and he will proclaim to you
The Protector's vast qualities and the way to enter them.

Circling him to the right and showing his reverence,
Sudhana, most gentle, went on his way to the south.
Avalokiteśvara, the sage who lives by compassion,
He too saw, dwelling in a valley of the jewel mountain.
In a meadow of herbs on the vajra mountain of many jewels,
He sat upon a lion throne whose heart was a lotus,
Surrounded by gods, asuras, nāgas, rākṣasas, kinnaras
And heirs of the Victor, and to them he taught the Dharma.
Seeing him, Sudhana felt measureless joy arise.
He approached and bowed at the feet of that ocean of qualities:
"So that I may attain the excellent conduct,
Noble one, rouse your compassion, and counsel and teach me, I pray."

Stretching out his stainless hand, adorned by a hundred merits,
Sending forth a great, fine net of a hundred clouds of light,
He laid it on Sudhana's crown; and that utterly pure being,
The wise Avalokiteśvara, spoke these words:

"Son of the Buddhas, I know a single door of liberation,
The heart of wisdom, made lovely by the compassion of all the Victors.
It comes forth to protect and wholly gather in all beings.
Everywhere I move, loving them as I love myself,
And I protect the many beings, all of them, from every suffering.

"Those bound in fetters, fallen into the hands of enemies,
Tormented in body, and likewise cast into prison,
Hearing my name, though bound and seized, will be set free.
Those who have done wrong against kings and been condemned to death,
If at that time they call my name to mind,
The arrows that are shot will not strike their bodies;
Though struck with sharp weapons, these will not pierce them, and they will come through.
Those who go before a ruler's court and those who fall into disputes
Will prevail over all their foes and gain renown;
Their fame, friends, family and wealth will increase,
Whoever keeps my name in mind.

"Those who fear thieves and dread enemies, whenever they set out,
Whether lions, bears, leopards, beasts of prey, wild yaks or snakes,
Will prevail over all their foes and be without fear.
If some call my name to mind,
Even if those of hostile mind should cast them into a chasm of the great earth, they will escape;
And though coals and dung fire blaze up fiercely,
It will turn into a brimming treasury of water.
If one calls my name to mind for a single moment,
Thrown into the waters of the ocean, one will not die there;
Rivers will not sweep one away, and amid fire one will not be burned;
Nothing will be in vain, and all one's aims will be accomplished.

"From the wooden stocks, the binding rods, the iron chains, and likewise falls,
From contempt and humiliation, likewise terror and deceit,
From abuse and threats, from reviling and slander of every kind,
Merely by calling my name to mind one gains release.
Those who scowl and look for every kind of opening, however many,
And those who always speak harshly out of anger,
Hearing my name and calling it to mind, will speak pleasantly,
And the moment they see you they will give rise to a loving mind.

"If someone calls merely my name to mind,
The enemies who for harm's sake work with vetālas and with mantras
Will always be overpowered by him,
And no poison at all can take hold in his body.
If someone calls merely my name to mind,
All the nāga lords, the hosts of clouds, the garuḍas, the piśācas,
The kumbhāṇḍas, the rākṣasas, the harmful ones of savage mind,
The stealers of vitality and the terrifiers, will become like things seen in a dream.

"Even if one calls my name to mind for a single instant,
One will not be parted from father and mother, from kin, friends and all who are dear,
Nor likewise be thrown together with foes;
One's wealth will not run out, and one will not fall into poverty.
If some keep my name in mind,
When they die and pass from here they will not go to the Avīci hell,
Nor to birth as animals, nor among the hungry ghosts, nor to the unfree states;
Those pure beings will be born in the abodes of gods and humans.

"If some call my name to mind,
They will not be blind from birth, one-eyed, deaf or lame;
They will not become crippled or blind; they will speak clearly and be fair to see,
And for many tens of millions of eons they will have all their faculties complete.
If some keep me in mind,
Those pure beings, when they die and pass from here, will be born
In the world systems of the ten directions, in the very presence of the buddhas;
They will see the buddhas and will hear the Dharma too.

"By this and by other means, however many they may be,
And by other powers that never run out, I surely tame sentient beings;
Yet I, son of the Buddhas, have cultivated only a single liberation,
And all the qualities of those who possess good qualities I do not know."

The qualities of the noble Avalokiteśvara.

The Precious Narratives of the Scriptures, compiled from the various noble sūtras, together with the Lineage of the Śākyas, composed by the preceptor Paltsek, is complete.

Translator's notes

In the Akṣayamati passage the line on outsiders reads literally "that of those who know outer signs and higher knowledges becomes manifest through ..."; it is taken, with the parallel clauses, to mean that the discipline of non-Buddhists with the higher knowledges (abhijñā) ends with those knowledges, reading "becomes manifest" as a slip for "is exhausted." The list of givings opens with sbyin pa bzhin, "like giving," evidently a slip for sbyin pa bzhi, "four kinds of giving," matching the four items listed and the following "four kinds of patience." That list of four patiences names only three, and is translated as it stands. The four items beginning "Giving is of the Dharma" are the four means of attracting disciples (saṃgrahavastu). Mañjuśrī's closing words read literally "then ba dag will attain buddhahood"; ba dag is taken as a slip for bdag, "I." In the sentence about the body offered as a lamp, mar me 'bud ("blows a lamp") is read as mar me 'bul, "offers as a lamp." The run of buddha names (evidently from the list of the thousand buddhas of the Good Eon) is written without separators, so its division into separate names is tentative. Apart from Maitreya and Roca, they are translated by meaning, since their Sanskrit forms could not be identified with confidence. In the sentence on Brahmā Śikhin, lta ("view") is read as lha, "god." Samantadarśin is the name under which Mañjuśrī is to attain buddhahood in that sūtra. In the Collection of Flowers passage, bye ba phrag brgyad is eight koṭis, that is, eighty million. Several excerpts are abbreviated and are translated as they stand. In the Bhadrapāla passage, zla ba 'byor pa is taken as a name or epithet ("Moon of Prosperity"); its referent is uncertain. The sentence on internal and external form reads oddly as written, as if part of it were missing. The sentence from the Irreversible Wheel speaks of "these two" without saying who they are. In the passage on confession, the sentence about something happening "up to a hundred times in a single day" is elliptical, and its sense is uncertain. The sentence "By the four garuḍas, kings of the birds" stands alone in the source, without a verb, and is translated as it stands. In the Sāgara passage, par mdor is read as bar mdor, "untimely." The list of the "eight qualities" of going forth names seven in the form given. In the verse beginning "Knowing the world is no vessel," the word rendered "of other realms" (khams pa) could also mean "weary," and the line is uncertain. The Garland Bearers are a class of gods within the realm of the Four Great Kings. "Forbearance" in the stages of loving-kindness refers to the forbearance regarding the nonarising of phenomena (anutpattikadharmakṣānti), attained by advanced bodhisattvas. In the lists of gain and honor, "praise" renders ཚིགས་སུ་བཅད་པ, which ordinarily means "verse"; it reflects Sanskrit śloka, which also means fame or praise. The phrase རྒྱལ་པོས་བཀྲབ་པར་འགྱུར, rendered "is empowered by the king," is obscure and translated from context; the word འཐིང་སླད at the end of that list is likewise rendered loosely as "mistreats." In "will become likewise" the text reads ད་ལྟར ("now"), evidently for དེ་ལྟར ("likewise"); conversely the sūtra title reads དེ་ལྟར་གྱི, evidently for ད་ལྟར་གྱི ("of the present"), giving the Pratyutpanna-buddha-saṃmukhāvasthita-samādhi. In "stirred into the great ocean" the verb སྦྱངས is taken in the sense of mixing. The passage on women reproduces a traditional misogynist trope of the source and is translated as it stands. "Everlasting" renders གཡུང་དྲུང (svastika), used as an epithet of the unchanging. In the list of virtuous dharmas the text reads བརྙེས ("find"), evidently for བརྙས ("despise"), which is how it has been translated. "Praise" again renders ཚིགས་སུ་བཅད་པ in the sense of Sanskrit śloka, fame or praise, as in the earlier list of gain and honor. The ten matters of the Dharmasaṃgīti are phrased as things the bodhisattva never neglects; for the deeds of Māra, beings in turmoil, and the destruction of the Dharma, the sense is that the bodhisattva does not neglect to counter or attend to them. The rare words ཁམ་ནམ་པ ("feeble") and སྐྲུ ("cawing") are rendered from context. In the Purification of Karmic Obscurations passage, ལས་ཀྱི་སྒྲིབ་པ་དག་འཐོབ་པར་འགྱུར could also be read as "will incur karmic obscurations," taking དག as a plural marker; the purifying sense has been preferred in keeping with the title, but the reading is uncertain. The sūtra title written དཔལ་སྤོང is taken as དཔལ་སྦས, the Śrīgupta Sūtra, whose story turns on Śrīgupta confessing his attempt on the Buddha's life; likewise ཆོས་འདུས་པ is read as ཆོས་འདུལ་བ ("the Dharma and Vinaya"), and དེས་བས as དེས་པས, the Sūratapariprcchā. The five aggregates of śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas are the five pure aggregates: discipline, concentration, wisdom, liberation, and the vision of liberation. In the Ratnadatta passage the text announces five purifications but lists only four as it stands; it is translated as written. The title རྣམ་པར་དག་པ་བསྡུས་པ is rendered literally as "Compendium of Purity"; its content (the monk who rejected the true Dharma, and the rejection of the claim that awakening comes by a single mode of practice) matches the Sarvavaidalyasaṃgraha, and the title may be a scribal variant of རྣམ་པར་འཐག་པ་བསྡུས་པ. In the same passage བསམ་གཏན་བཞི་ཐོས ("heard the four concentrations") is read as ཐོབ ("attained"). The words མ་ལྡོང་བ ("unfitting") and འགྱེད་པ ("things that incite strife") in the Bodhisattva Prātimokṣa list are obscure and rendered from context, as is the construal of the preceding sentence on guile and the rest. The passages on women reproduce the misogynist rhetoric of the source and are translated as they stand. In the list of the twenty situations only nine are given, as in the source. The phrase བུད་མེད་ཉི་ཤུ་མ་ཚང་བ could also mean "a woman under twenty years of age"; the reading "fewer than twenty" is a choice. The obscure གཉན་འདབ is read as ཉེ་འདབས ("neighbor"). The titles ཟུང་གི་མདོ ("Pair Sūtra") and ཕ་རོལ་ཏུ་ཕྱིན་པ་ལྔ་པ ("Five Perfections") are rendered literally; the second may be the Pañcapāramitānirdeśa. "A nun's wisdom is two fingers wide" reflects a stock Indian disparagement of women's understanding and is translated as it stands. The Pair Sūtra quotation breaks off at "into the sky above" in the source before the next citation begins. In the quotation from The Meeting of the Father and the Son, the verb read as "understand" is written འཆལ; it is taken as a slip for འཚལ, in line with the well-known saying that beings understand the Buddha's single utterance each according to their dispositions. In the line read as "does not make a living by wrong means," the Tibetan ཡང་བས་འཚོ is unclear as written; it is taken as wrong livelihood (ལོག་པས་འཚོ), which fits the series of vows. "The teacher's closed fist" renders the stock image (ācāryamuṣṭi) of a teacher who withholds the Dharma. This piece is a chain of short excerpts, several of them cut off mid-thought by the compiler; they are rendered as they stand. Subhūti's dream report breaks off after its opening words. The two sentences ending "is not like that" set someone with the aspiration to awakening against a śrāvaka; the word written གླིང ("island, continent") in the first is unclear and is passed over, and ཆོས་འཕགས is taken as "exalted in the Dharma" rather than as the name Dharmodgata. "In this Dharma" renders དར་མ, which may stand for a short title. The line about the householders going to "the Supreme Puṇḍarīka Flower" is fragmentary, and the line "the eighteen unshared qualities have come forth as peace" is uncertain in sense. In the Kṣitigarbha passage, ངན་ཕྱིར is read as ངའི་ཕྱིར, "for my sake," which the sense requires. The Ākāśagarbha excerpt says "four" for the kings' downfalls although five items follow; it is translated as written. The Saṅgharakṣita excerpt and the line on leftovers from The Application of Mindfulness of the True Dharma are terse and their exact sense is uncertain. In the Mañjuśrī's Play excerpt, the Tibetan plays on the word for anger (ཁོང་ཁྲོ), explaining it as what destroys virtue. The Ratnolkā verses are paradoxical by design, "not recollecting" the buddhas meaning recollection free of conceptual grasping. In the Upāli passage, མཛེས་པ ("beautiful") makes no sense and is read as འཛེམ་པ, "to shrink from, have scruples." In the Compendium of Trainings excerpt, the third of the Three Heaps is written as exhorting "the pratyekabuddha stage" (རང་སངས་རྒྱས་ས་ལ); it is read as requesting the buddhas (སངས་རྒྱས་རྣམས་ལ), the well-known third member of the triad. In the Laṅkāvatāra passage, སྨིན་པ ("ripening") is read as སྨོན་པ ("aspiration"), as the sentence that follows requires. The Ugra remark on weapons is terse, and its exact point is uncertain. "Icchantika" renders འདོད་ཆེན་པོ, literally "one of great desire," the technical term for those cut off from liberation. Several readings follow the evident sense of the Laṅkāvatāra where the Tibetan as written makes no sense. In the second verse, དེ་བཤད ("that is explained") is read as དེ་ཆད ("their cutting off"). རྒྱལ་པོའི་རིན་སྲེད ("the king's...") is read as རྒྱལ་བའི་རིང་བསྲེལ, "the relics of the Victor." In the Akaniṣṭha verse, ཁྱོད ("you") is read as ཁྲོད ("among"). "Should purify" renders རྣམ་པར་སྦྱར, taken as རྣམ་པར་སྦྱང. The sentence on attaining realization is elliptical; "could perish" supplies the point implied by the surrounding talk of imperishability. Lokāyata is the materialist school, used here for worldly views in general. The first block continues a quotation begun in the preceding piece, and the ocean simile breaks off unfinished ("however many realms of sentient beings there are...") before the next quotation begins; it is rendered as it stands. In the Ratnakoṭi quotation the text reads mtshungs pa med pa ("without equal"), evidently a slip for mtshams med pa ("act of immediate retribution"), which the rest of the passage uses; translated accordingly. In the list of four from Rousing the Superior Intention, yongs sa dag pa is read as yongs su dag pa. The sense of a few items in the "I do not call" list is compressed in the Tibetan: "one who looks to holy recipients of offerings" (yon gnas dam pa la) and "one who has never been put to the test" (ma bcos pa) are best-sense renderings. The quotations from The Analysis of Karma are abbreviated excerpts in the Tibetan, giving only the first item or two of each list; they are rendered as they stand, and the ellipses mark where the anthology breaks off. In the list of the drawbacks of alcohol, mi ma thong ba is read as mi thob pa ("does not gain"), and in the Karmavibhaṅga verse rtsa nas phyin is taken in the sense of "torn out by the root". In the Tathāgata-qualities quotation, lho bal is taken as barbarian or tribal raiders; the exact sense is uncertain. In the Upāli quotation the Tibetan reads "that is the impure (yongs su ma dag pa) moral discipline of a śrāvaka," but the argument requires "pure"; the evident sense is translated. In Jñānottara's question, lag dung bar is read as gdung bar ("tormented"). The brahmin youth's name, 'od zer phreng, could not be confidently identified with a Sanskrit form and is translated literally as "Garland of Light Rays". The passage names the buddha of that episode first as Dīpaṃkara and then as Kāśyapa; both are kept as the Tibetan has them. In "I declare him without fault, unlike other beings," the phrase sems can gzhan pa la is compressed and its exact force is uncertain. The dhāraṇī is given letter for letter as the Tibetan spells it. The sūtra titled sangs rgyas mi spang ba is translated literally as Not Forsaking the Buddha, and the world realm smig rgyu can as Endowed with Mirages; Sanskrit forms for these could not be confidently identified. The name dpal dbyig is rendered Śrīvasu (dbyig for vasu), a probable but not certain reconstruction. The quotation from The Way of the Dharma consists of paradoxical imperatives and is translated as commands exactly as they stand; the anthology gives no framing for them. In the Bhavasaṃkrānti verses, line eleven repeats "the world does not fathom it," as the Tibetan has it. In the Two Truths quotation, bkris is read as bkres ("hungry"). In Mañjuśrī's answer about the ocean, mi lom pa is taken in the sense of "not flowing into". In the explanation of apparent diversity, yongs su rdzogs pas is read as yongs su rtogs pas ("fully conceives"), matching the negative form yongs su mi rtogs na that follows. The eon names (སྙན་པ་ཆེན་པོ, སྐར་མ་ལྟ་བུ, ཡོན་ཏན་བཀོད་པ) are translated by meaning ("Great Renown," "Like the Stars," "Array of Qualities"); plausible Sanskrit forms such as Mahāyaśas, Tārakopama and Guṇavyūha are not given because they could not be confirmed with certainty. The title མ་འདྲེས་པའི་མདོ ("Sūtra of the Unmixed") could not be identified with confidence and is rendered literally. In that quotation the Tibetan reads literally "the obstacles ... are two: being without deceit and guile"; it has been taken as naming deceit and guile as the two obstacles, with their absence enjoined. In the ten obstacles, དེ་བ་ལ་མགྲོན་དུ་འབོད་པ is obscure; "inviting such people in as guests" is a tentative rendering. In the Chapter of Truth, ཀུན་རྫོབ་ཏུ་འཇིག་པ ("perishing conventionally") has been read as ཀུན་རྫོབ་ཏུ་འཇུག་པ ("entry on the conventional level"), an evident one-vowel slip; the clause about the "setting out of persons ... as lesser and immeasurable" is terse and its exact sense uncertain. "Certainty of the right state" renders ཡང་དག་པ་ཉིད་དུ་ངེས་པར་ཞུགས་པ (samyaktvaniyāma), the fixed attainment of arhat-type disciples, who on this account have cut off the continuum of rebirth. འདོད་ཆེན་པོ ("those of great desire") is the standard Tibetan rendering in the Mahāparinirvāṇa Sūtra of icchantika, a being cut off from the roots of virtue. The phrase རྒྱུ་སོགས་པའི་སྲོག་གཅོད་པ is obscure; it is rendered tentatively as "to take life for any reason whatsoever." The sentence beginning "This world is not an emanated body" is terse in the Tibetan and its exact sense is uncertain; it has been rendered literally. In the list of improper livelihoods, སྣབས་སུ་སྦྱོར་བ ("prepare nasal remedies") and ལྷ་བྱེད་པ ("practise the cult of the gods") are tentative. ཐོག་མནས is read as ཐོག་མ་ནས, "from the beginning." The closing question uses humble speech and is put to the Blessed One by an unnamed interlocutor of the sūtra. འདོད་ཆེན་པོ renders icchantika, as in the preceding piece. In the eclipse simile, གཟའ ("planet") refers to Rāhu as the eclipse-causing graha. The phrase གནམ་སྟོང་གཉིས་པར is taken as the new-moon juncture between the two halves of the month; གཟའ་མཇུག་རིངས ("long-tailed planet") is rendered "comet." སྒོམ་བཏོད་པས in the silkworm simile is obscure and rendered "having sealed itself in." གཏམ་སྙེད and དཀྲིགས་སྙེད in the passage on women are obscure numerical expressions (དཀྲིགས is a very large number) and are rendered loosely as "countless multitudes" and "myriads beyond count." The line ས་རྣམས་ལོག་པར་རབ་ཏུ་གནས in the Dense Array verse is obscure; "it abides, inverted, in the grounds" is tentative. The isolated phrase "The Blessed One, the pratyekabuddha" after the Golden Colour quotation stands so in the Tibetan. མཐོས་ཀྱང་བླའོ in the simile of hunger and poisoned food is read as a contraction of མ་ཐོས་ཀྱང་བླའོ, "it is better not even to have heard," which the simile requires. In the Avataṃsaka verses, མོད་པའི་ཡིད་ལ་གནས is obscure and rendered loosely as "abide right within the mind." The names of the continent (Banner of Awakening), the town (Infinite Treasury) and the sūtra Array of the Ornaments of Eloquence are translated by meaning, since their Sanskrit forms could not be confirmed. The sentence ལྷ་མ་ཡིན་གྱི་དབང་པོ་སྒྲ་གཅན་ཞེས་བྱ་བ་ཡོངས་སུ་བསྔོའོ is abrupt; it has been taken as naming the preceding vast dedication after Rāhu, whose enormous bodies are then described, but it could also be read as "Rāhu, lord of the asuras, dedicates." "Leagues" renders དཔག་ཚད (yojana). "One million" renders བརྒྱ་སྟོང་ཕྲག་བཅུ (ten hundred-thousands). In the list of obscurations, the Tibetan reads "not seeing bodhisattvas," which has been kept as written. The fourth river's name is given as the Tibetan spells it, Pakṣu (the Vakṣu or Oxus). The bodhisattva's name དེ་བཞིན་གཤེགས་པའི་རིགས་སུ་བྱུང་བའི་དཔལ is translated by meaning ("Glory Born in the Family of the Tathāgata"), since its exact Sanskrit form could not be confirmed. The section that begins "The expression of the speech of the tathāgatas" repeats, almost word for word, the preceding sentences about the sun's even rays and the blind; it is translated as it stands. In the passage on the hidden wisdom, འདུན་པ་ཆེན་པོ ("great longing") is read as མདུད་པ་ཆེན་པོ ("great knots"), which the verb "untie" requires, and the closing ཞེ་ན is taken as marking the Tathāgata's thought. In the simile of the water vessel, ཆུ་སྣོད་གཅིག་ཅིག་གམ is read in the light of the following "broken vessels" as "a water vessel is broken." The list introduced as the "ten" works of Māra actually contains thirteen items in the Tibetan; it is translated as it stands. In the list of deviant paths, དཔགས་པས་འབྱེད་པ་བྱེད་པ is obscure and rendered tentatively as "parting from the conduct of bodhisattvas through calculation." ཁྱུ་མཆོག ("supreme bull") is an epithet of the Buddha. The short simile beginning "Like someone who owns everything" is irregular in metre in the Tibetan and is rendered with the verse that follows. The list introduced as ten waves of Māra contains twelve items in the Tibetan; it is translated as it stands. In it, གཅིག་ལས་མི་བཟོད་པ and སྨོན་ལམ་ལ་ལྷུང་བར་མི་དམིགས་པ are obscure and rendered tentatively. In the twelve qualities, བགེད་པའི་ངང་ཚུལ is taken as a form of འགྱེད་པ, "to distribute," hence "a generous disposition." In the benefits of concentration, བསམ་གཏན་ཟློག་པ ("turning back the concentrations") and ཚད་ཡོད་པའི་ཏིང་ངེ་འཛིན ("a samādhi that has measure," perhaps an error for ཚད་མེད་པ, "immeasurable") are given literally; ཅང་ཤེས་པ is rendered "well trained." ཆོས་ཀྱི་གཡུང་དྲུང ("the svastika of the Dharma") is kept as written, though the context of speech that follows may point to an original "Dharma conch" (ཆོས་དུང). གྲོག་པ in "listens to ... reads" is read as ཀློག་པ. The last line of the Transference of Existence verse (དེ་ལ་འཇིག་རྟེན་མི་དགོས་སོ) is rendered literally. The sentence on the afflicted and purification (སྣྲེལ་ཞིའི་རྣམ་གྲངས, "the permutations of the cross-classification") is very compressed and its exact sense is uncertain. གནོད་སེམས་ཀྱི་ཐ་བ is rendered "the barb of malice"; ཚུལ་ཁྲིམས་མཆོག་ཏུ་མི་འཛིན་པ follows the Tibetan literally. In the verse on beings not fit as vessels, རབ་ཏུ་གཞེས is taken as "fully attends to." In the list of impossibilities, འཆགས་པའི་ཚུལ་ཁྲིམས is read as འཆལ་པའི་ཚུལ་ཁྲིམས ("corrupt moral discipline"), and བྱང་གི་སྒྲ་མི་སྙན is the continent Northern Kuru; the final item, "rest in equipoise upon the Tathāgata," is given literally and is obscure. གཟུངས་ནི་དྲི་བ་བརྗོད་དུ་མེད་པའོ ("Dhāraṇī is questions beyond expression") is also obscure. The names of the eon, world system and tathāgata in the story are translated by meaning. In the Eight Maṇḍalas verse, ནམ་མཁའ ("space") is taken as an abbreviation of Ākāśagarbha, one of the eight great bodhisattvas, with ཐུབ་པ་སྐྱེས་མཆོག ("sage, supreme being") and བློ་ལྡན ("the wise") read as epithets, which gives the eight names the verse requires. ཟ་མ and མ་ནིང are both terms for eunuchs or persons of neuter sex (paṇḍaka). The worm names ཁ་རྫོགས ("Complete Mouth") and ཨ་བ་རོ་ཕེན ("Abarophen") are given as translation and transliteration respectively, since neither could be identified. The list of features of the female faculty (fractions, "the triangular," མོའི་སྤ་བ "feminine demeanour") is obscure and rendered literally. These anatomical and misogynist claims are reproduced as the source states them. བསྟན་པར་མི་བྱའོ in the passage on śrāvaka companions is read as བསྟེན་པར་མི་བྱའོ ("should not associate with"), and གཞག་པར་མི་ནུས as གཞིག་པར་མི་ནུས ("cannot destroy"), as the reasoning requires. The size of Jambudvīpa is given without a unit in the Tibetan. In the passage on inference from action, the clause about the Tathāgata and the word དཔའ་བར (read as དེ་བར, "to that destiny") are uncertain. The Prophecy of Bhadra the Illusionist announces four paths but lists three, and announces four sources of eloquence but lists five; both are translated as they stand. Personal names in the past-life stories (Moon Brow, Teacher of the Three Worlds, Beauty Illuminating the World, Unripened Fame, Supreme in Bodhisattva Conduct, Good Eyes) are translated by meaning. ཐོས་ནས་བྱ་བ་མི་བྱེད་པ is rendered tentatively as "gives up busy activity"; བ་གླང་ཅང་ཤེས is rendered "a noble bull elephant." གག་ལས is read as གང་ལས ("from whom"). ཆོས་ཐམས་ཅད་ཀྱི་གོ ("the import of all phenomena") is terse and its sense uncertain. In the story of the monk, ཡ་མཚན་ཅན ("strange ones") is taken as a term for the heterodox ascetic schools, here numbered ninety-six; ཕྱུགས་བདག is taken as the Paśupatas, and ཤིང་ཐུན as "woodcutter." The brahmin's words (ངུར་བ་ལྟ) are elliptical and rendered loosely. The buddha's name ཀོ་སན་ཏ་བཟང་པོ is given as it is spelled, Kosanta the Excellent; it could not be identified. བསྟན་ལས་དེ་རིང in the verse is taken as "far from the teaching." The quotations from the Questions of Mañjuśrī and the Dharmaskandha repeat, with small differences, passages quoted earlier in this text. The answer to Śāradvatīputra is paradoxical but is translated as the Tibetan stands. In Akṣayamati's list, འདུས་བྱས་དང་འདུས་མ་བྱས་ལམ་ཆགས་པ is rendered tentatively as "linking the conditioned and the unconditioned as a path." In the Questions of Śrīvasu, གནོད་པ་བྱེད་པ ("who do him harm") qualifies the first and last members of the list as written. འཁོར་བ་འཇིག is the Tibetan rendering of Krakucchanda. In the Transference of Existence, སྟོད་པ is read as སྟོང་པ ("empty"); བཟོ་སྦྱངས is Śreṇya, an epithet of Bimbisāra. In the Teaching of the Four Qualities, ཆོས་བཞིན is read as ཆོས་བཞི ("four qualities"), as the list requires. The Four Qualities passage announces four things never to be given up but lists five; it is translated as it stands. The dhāraṇī is transcribed letter for letter as the Tibetan spells it. In the Teaching of the Inconceivable Light, འདུས་བྱས་སུ་མཐོང ("seeing as conditioned") has been read as འདུས་མ་བྱས་སུ་མཐོང ("seeing as unconditioned"), which the parallel with the Buddha seen as the nature of reality requires. In the Questions of Siṃha, སྙིང་པོ་ལྔ ("the five essentials") is obscure. The names of the world systems and buddhas in the Ten Buddhas are translated by meaning, since their Sanskrit forms could not be confirmed. "Non-revealing form" renders རྣམ་པར་རིག་བྱེད་མ་ཡིན་པའི་གཟུགས (avijñaptirūpa). The word rendered "whitewash" is uncertain; the context (worn by sun and rain, rebirth with a white body) suggests a coating of plaster or lime. The "third outer terrace" refers to the terraces of Mount Meru inhabited by gods of the Four Great Kings' realm; the exact identification is uncertain. "The Tong region" translates an unidentified place name. The names of the former Śakras other than Ānanda are rendered by meaning ("Good Fortune," "Deathless") because their Sanskrit forms cannot be identified with confidence. In the third of the four qualities, the Tibetan has a word meaning "meeting" where the following clause plainly requires "effort"; the evident reading "without effort" is translated, and the clause is taken, as in Asaṅga's parallel argument, as a counterfactual against real external objects. In the Saṃdhinirmocana quotation this text reads that consciousness is "not" divided off by its object, whereas the sūtra as usually transmitted says it is distinguished by its object being mere representation; the text is translated as it stands. In the definition of the faculty of complete knowledge (ājñendriya), this text repeats "persons in training who have not yet attained clear realization," the same wording as for the preceding faculty; the standard doctrine assigns this faculty to trainees who have attained realization, so the negative is probably a copyist's repetition, but the text is translated as it stands. "What is" in that list renders a syllable written ཞན, an evident slip for ཞེ་ན. "The faultless state of rightness" renders the technical term for the certainty of the noble path (samyaktvaniyāma). The final sentence names Maitreya where the sūtra context would lead one to expect a generic bodhisattva; it is translated as written, and the sentence continues in the next piece. "The Establisher" renders the name of the addressee and title of the sūtra on the eightfold fast; the Indian form of the name cannot be identified with confidence, so it is translated by meaning. "Solitary awakening" renders the term for a pratyekabuddha's awakening. The passage continues into the next piece with the closing formula of the vow. The list of the "sixteen great countries" names only fifteen; it is translated as it stands. Several of its names are given here as the Tibetan spells them (Puntra, Srakpa, Kama, Kampo), since their standard Sanskrit forms cannot be identified with full confidence (Puṇḍra and Kamboja are possible). In the list of terms for a self, "the one possessed of power" and "the master of power" translate two terms whose Sanskrit equivalents are uncertain. "Is not apprehended" follows the evident reading of a phrase written with a dropped syllable, and "the vehicle" in Subhūti's last question follows the evident reading of a word misspelled as "rope." "Nanda" follows the house rendering of the name as written; in parallel versions of this entrustment the addressee is Ānanda. In the avoidance of familiarity with nuns, the last clause (rendered "does not let himself become tainted on her account") is uncertain; the Tibetan could also be read as "does not obtain robes on her account." "The fixed lawfulness of phenomena" renders the term for the invariable nature of things (dharmaniyāmatā), here spelled with a word meaning "faultlessness." "Each comes to abide on his own ground" follows the evident reading of a phrase missing the word for "ground." "Coarse sixty-day rice" renders a word meaning "the sixtieth," the name of a quick-ripening, inferior rice (ṣaṣṭika). In the arhat's reply, the opening phrase "though there are arhats" is literal; the point is that arhats do not sleep and so have no dream-cognition to grasp. "The meditative absorption of the single array" renders the samādhi named ekavyūha in the Seven Hundred Lines. The question "Why is that which is not nonexistent called existent?" is translated literally; its double negative is as the Tibetan has it. "Ghana" ("Dense") is the name of the buddhafield of the Dense Array Sūtra, and the verses play on the word, which also means "dense." In the verse on life, warmth and consciousness, the Tibetan reads "vigilance" where the well-known parallel and the sense require "like a log" (a word differing by one letter), and that reading is translated. "By twos or by threes" renders words that can also mean "two or three finger-widths"; the reference appears to be to the combination of atoms into dyads and triads. The two lines on dwelling "from abode to abode in the realms of the gods" are obscure, and their rendering is tentative. Udraka is Udraka Rāmaputra, the meditation teacher whose absorptions did not uproot the notion of self. The buddha "King of Masses of Splendor" and "King Steadfast Gift" are rendered by meaning, since their Sanskrit forms cannot be identified with confidence. Kṣemadatta, Ghoṣadatta, King Śūradatta and the monk Supuṣpacandra are figures of the King of Meditative Absorptions (Samādhirāja) Sūtra. In "the true Dharma of the supreme among men," the Tibetan has an unexplained syllable ("water") before "supreme," which has been passed over. "Separate folk" brings out the word-play on the term for ordinary beings, literally "individual beings." In the list of god kings, "the Caravan Leader" (king of the gods of Māra's class) and "the Foremost Brahmā" (of the Ministers of Brahmā) are rendered by meaning. The phrase introducing Prasenajit's offering appears to name its source as "the Journey of the Buddha"; the title is uncertain. The householder "Victory Guard" and the Śākya son "Dark Gift" are rendered by meaning, since their Sanskrit forms cannot be identified with confidence; they appear to be cited as examples of those who found the four causes of happiness. In the second list of four qualities, the final phrase rendered "making it one's preparation for death" is uncertain. "Gentleness" follows the evident reading of a word written as "certainty," as in the parallel phrase "patience and gentleness" earlier in this text. In the Laṅkāvatāra quotation, "evolving consciousnesses" follows the evident reading of a word written as "perishing consciousnesses," which differs by one letter; and "would be no different from the tīrthikas' teaching" follows the evident sense of a sentence that, as written, lacks the negative. "Differences of pleasure, of shape" follows the evident reading of a word written as "sphere" for "shape." The opening of the Cloud of Jewels quotation ("one who is not yet skilled in the mind") is translated as written, though it may originally have read "one skilled in the mind." "Stream of the appearances of one's own mind" follows the evident reading of a word written as "cause," and "purifies all at once" follows the evident reading of a word written as "engages"; both are one-letter differences, and the parallel in the Laṅkāvatāra supports the readings adopted. In Mahāmati's final list, "the Lord" follows the evident reading, matching the list at the start of this piece, of a word written as "Indra." "A mat from grass" renders a doubtful word for the material. The sentence on "without intrinsic nature" is obscure, and its rendering is tentative. "An illusory display" renders a word that can also mean "confused" or "bewildering." "Shape" follows the evident reading of a word written as "sphere," as in the preceding piece. In the passage on setting questions aside, the Tibetan is compressed; the rendering follows the parallel in the Laṅkāvatāra, in which the Buddha leaves certain questions unanswered only for the time being, according to the capacity of the hearer. The verse line "Conception is called the conceived" is obscure and is translated literally. In the final reply, "his" refers to the Buddha, of whom the narrative speaks in the third person. "For the childish" follows the evident reading of a word written as "made," which differs by one letter. In the verse beginning "Having that as its cause," the second line follows the evident reading "in which mentation moves," where the Tibetan has a negative ("does not enter") that does not fit the parallel in the Laṅkāvatāra; likewise "The mind is not divided by habitual tendencies" follows the parallel where the Tibetan verb reads "does not make." "The assembly of minds" follows the evident reading of a word written as "past." The question "how is it that mental consciousness is turned back" is compressed in the Tibetan; it asks why only the turning back of mental consciousness is spoken of. The two lines on "the middle" are obscure and are translated tentatively. The phrase "yogins who have no wish for striving" is translated as written. The second verse is the well-known Laṅkāvatāra verse listing synonyms of mind; "stirrings of the three realms" renders a term corresponding to Sanskrit tribhavaceṣṭā. In the Dharmasaṃgīti list, the word rendered "fame" is literally "verses" (Skt. śloka), which in the stock compound "gain, honor and śloka" means fame or renown. The rainbow sentence is terse and its sense (each color being due to the dominance of an element) is somewhat uncertain. In the passage on the yogin's mastery, "solidity" translates the evident reading sra ba where the text has srab ("thin"). The sentence "Such is the cessation of perception and feeling" renders a repeated heading-like phrase whose exact construction is unclear. "This Dharma text" renders a transliterated "dharma," apparently referring to the sūtra just cited (the Dharmasaṃgīti). The names of the world system ("Happiness") and the universal monarch ("Array of Virtue") are translated literally, as their Sanskrit forms cannot be fixed with confidence; Sumerukalpa renders "Like Meru." The opening "up to" marks an abbreviated quotation leading into the verses. In the fourth verse the comparison between the householder's gift and the renunciant's single night of contemplation is left without an explicit conclusion in the Tibetan; it has not been supplied. In "when it was granted," the text's gnad nas is read as gnang nas ("having been granted"). "The view of the perishable collection" renders the term for satkāyadṛṣṭi, and "the imputation of what is unreal" renders the term for abhūtaparikalpa. "The acceptance that phenomena are unborn" renders the term for anutpattikadharmakṣānti throughout. In the third-concentration formula the Tibetan is elliptical ("from that joy, attachment and ..."); it has been read, in line with the standard formula, as "through detachment from that joy." The phrase rendered "so full of what is neither suffering nor bliss" translates an awkward "mang ba" ("much, abundant") whose construction is uncertain. The triad "exceedingly blazing, luminous and radiant" may echo the names of the second-concentration heavens. In Śāriputra's thought the Tibetan reads "just as one's own all sentient beings are pure," evidently a corruption of "just as one's own mind is pure," which the sūtra's argument requires; the latter is translated. The source titled here "the Inconceivable Light" is presumably the Acintyaprabhāsanirdeśa. The names of the king ("Utter Vanquisher of Armies") and of the two bodhisattvas ("Excellent Deva" and "Earth") are translated literally, since their Sanskrit forms cannot be fixed with confidence. The text reads "Excellent by the deva's attendant" where "Excellent Deva's attendant" is plainly meant. The admonition "You have far too many activities" is headed by Excellent Deva's name; the context, in which Earth is the busy one and then reviles Excellent Deva, shows that Excellent Deva is the speaker rather than the one addressed. "The Tathāgata Unshakable" translates a buddha name that may stand for Akṣobhya, whose buddha-field is famous; since the Tibetan form here is not the usual one for Akṣobhya, it has been translated literally. "Inconceivable Light" is here the name of a person (and of the buddha he is to become), matching the title of the sūtra being cited. In the list of the ascetic's stains, the text's "thoughts of desire-god" is read as "thoughts of sensual desire"; the items "arising from the gathering of conditions and company" and "the secret spells of worldly society" are obscure and rendered literally; the list as given exceeds thirty-two items. In the Ratnakūṭa passage, "the prātimokṣa, the discipliner, will be torn apart" renders an awkward clause of uncertain construction. In the Vikurvaṇarājaparipṛcchā the text reads des pa'i don for nges pa'i don ("definitive meaning"); and the sentence "those who rely on the sūtras of definitive meaning lead by words" takes drang ba in its active sense ("to lead"), which suits the reason that follows. "The Lion's Roar of Maitreya" takes the heading "Maitreya Lion said" as a citation of the Maitreyamahāsiṃhanāda Sūtra, which is addressed to Maitreya, as the vocative "Maitreya" later in the passage confirms.
