In the list of things to flee, "fame" renders a word literally meaning "verses" (śloka, in the stock phrase "gain, honor and fame"), and the text's bzhin before "gain" is disregarded as a slip. The sentence beginning "set beside a bodhisattva's reviling" is elliptical in the Tibetan; the comparative sense is drawn from the context and the iron simile that follows. "The Tathāgata Variegated Crest" and the world system "Array of Joy" are translated literally, their Sanskrit forms being uncertain; "Nemi" renders mu khyud ("rim"). "One both enters them and emerges" renders a terse, possibly defective clause. The opening sentences continue the speech of Pūrṇa begun in the preceding piece. In "to enter the four meditative concentrations and to abide in them," the text's gnad la is read as gnas la. "Victor's Melody" renders the name of the Tathāgata (perhaps Jinaghoṣa or Jayaghoṣa), whose Sanskrit form is uncertain. "The color of arrow-bamboo" renders a rare color term for Vajradhara's image whose exact sense is uncertain; "pok resin" names an aromatic resin incense whose identification is uncertain. "This is how the practice with the image is to be done" renders a terse sentence that may instead conclude the preceding instructions on making the image. The first line of the opening verse is obscure ("rab phyed sku mnyam"); "fully discerned, are equal in body" is a tentative rendering. In the sentence closing the passage on dependent origination, the text reads "connected with ('brel ba) all extreme views," evidently for "free from (bral ba)," as in the question that opens the passage. In the prophetic verses, "dharmas that merely resemble them" renders the obscure "ded la 'dra ba'i chos," and "fight like executioners" renders a line whose sense is uncertain. "Lokāyatas" renders the term for the worldly materialists. "Possessed of Wonder" translates the name of a bodhisattva whose Sanskrit form is uncertain. "Blissful Appearance" translates the name of a world whose Sanskrit form is uncertain. "Son of good family" reads rigs kyi bu where the text has rig sa kyi bu. In the Suvarṇaprabhāsottama passage, the text reads "conceptual wisdom" (rnam par rtog pa'i ye shes), but the argument and the parallel phrase "the wisdom of suchness" require "nonconceptual wisdom," which is translated. "Heroism in the Dharma" renders a phrase (chos kyi dpa' ba) of somewhat uncertain sense. The play on words in the verse on the great elements turns on the Tibetan 'byung ba, which means both "element" and "arise." "Subhadrā" renders bzang mo, the name of the goddess, whose Sanskrit form is not certain. In the Saddharmapuṇḍarīka passage the text reads song med, evidently for ngan song med ("no lower realms"), which is translated. The passage is a chain of extracts from the Lotus Sūtra (Saddharmapuṇḍarīka), the anthology joining them with brief linking phrases; the clause "as arising from distorted perception" opens mid-sentence as the extract does. In the verse on the rain of flowers the Tibetan reads "las mngon par 'thob" ("attain from"), which makes no sense in context; it is translated by the evident reading "la mngon par 'thor" ("scatter upon"), in keeping with the sūtra. Btang bzung is taken as the mountain Mucilinda, which the sūtra lists here beside the Cakravāḍa ranges. The first part continues the extracts from the Lotus Sūtra (the chapters on Bhaiṣajyarāja and Samantabhadra, and the opening scene with the Great Exposition). "The Eye of Space" (nam mkha'i spyan) renders the title of the next sūtra quoted, which I could not identify with confidence. In the Blessed One's last speech the Tibetan "mi srun" is read as mi srung, "do not protect," which the parallel with "do not discipline" requires. The phrase "harmful persons who show no respect" (gnod mi bkur ba) is unusual in this stock list of reasons for the training rules, where "shameless persons" is expected, but it is translated as written. The piece opens mid-sentence, continuing the sūtra quoted before it ("the Eye of Space"); "does not protect" reads the Tibetan "mi slu" ("does not deceive") as mi srung, as the sense and the parallel earlier require. The account of the twelve animals in the caves of four ocean mountains resembles the one in the Sūryagarbha section of the Great Collection (Mahāsaṃnipāta), but this text's list differs from it, and its own items are kept. "Jantu" (Tibetan dzan du) stands where a serpent might be expected; "the ox" in the closing list reads the obscure "ga ba" by the sense; the twelve-animal list here has an elephant where other versions have a dragon. Several cave and deity names are uncertain: Arṇapa, Amara, Dharmapaha, Milantra, Udmaśrī, Arunagengola and Laṃkara are given as the Tibetan spells them; "Excellent Adornment" (spra ba'i han bzang po), "Radiance and Near Radiance," "Calf Surākṣa," and "Śata the Sage" are tentative renderings of the Tibetan. The embryological stages follow the terms used in the lexicon earlier. In the speech of the gods, "the stone to repel" (bzlog pa'i rdo) is read as bzlog pa'i rngo, "the ability to repel," as in the clause that follows. "Gladly go" renders "thugs dgyes shing" as written, though a negative ("displeased") might be expected. In the Saṃdhinirmocana extract, "ris ldar" is read as ris lngar, "the five classes of destiny." "The Support of All" (kun rten) is taken as the name of the stūpa; I could not identify its Sanskrit form. The first paragraph continues the Sūryagarbha's list of holy places entrusted to nāga kings. Most of the places and nāga kings are given only by Tibetan translated names that I cannot identify with confidence, so their meanings are translated; only Sāgara, Airāvaṇa, Vāsuki, Anavatapta, Mucilinda, Elāpattra and Nārāyaṇa are standard. Several of these Tibetan forms look corrupt: "ba long spyod" is read as ba lang spyod (Godānīya); the name after "Increase of Jewels" ends in garbled syllables; "the king Given by the Ocean" may stand for a nāga king. "Rgya yul" is taken as China, and the land of Kaśa with "the Earth's Breast" as the Khotan region with its Mount Gośṛṅga, as in the Sūryagarbha. The middle paragraph, on seeing the buddhas through the mind, follows the Pratyutpannabuddhasaṃmukhāvasthitasamādhi; two of its short sentences on "those who perceive things as mind" read obscurely in the Tibetan and are translated as written. "The Sūtra of Thorough Analysis" (mdo sde rab tu rnam par 'byed pa) is a title I could not identify. The six "bases" of wrong view (the basis of the mind, the basis concordant with the five, and so on) are obscure, and the list of six does not obviously match the "fivefold" division of the teaching just mentioned; they are rendered literally. "The flawless state of a bodhisattva" renders the Tibetan skyon med pa, which in this context translates bodhisattvaniyāma, the bodhisattva's certainty of fixed destiny. "Like filth" reads phyi sa (excrement, filth) for the written phyis, which makes no sense here and fits the image of the vessel of filth that follows. The Śāntideva verses are a rendering of Bodhicaryāvatāra 6.10 and following that differs from the standard canonical translation; they are translated as they stand here. "Hypocrisy, flattery, hinting, coercion, and seeking gain through gain" are the five wrong livelihoods. The buddha's name ཡིད་གཉིས་ཀུན་འཇོམས་རྣམ་པར་གནོན་པ is translated by its meaning ("Subduer Who Vanquishes All Doubt"), since its Sanskrit form could not be identified with confidence. In the third of the four thieves, the written las 'chi ba makes no sense; it is read as khas 'che ba, "claims", which the context plainly requires. The last four lines of the verse on the perfections are repeated in the source after the words "by the great elder Kāśyapa", evidently a scribal repetition anticipating the following sentence; it is translated as it stands. The passage draws on the Mahāyāna Mahāparinirvāṇa Sūtra. The fourth of the four qualities (self, permanence, bliss, purity) is written སུག, which is not a Tibetan word here; it is read as "pure", the quality the sequence requires. The latter part glosses the four reliances (Dharma not the person, meaning not the letter, definitive not provisional meaning, wisdom not consciousness). In "those persons are not authoritative" the source reads ཚད་མ་ཡིན་ནོ, "are authoritative"; this is taken as a slip for ཚད་མ་མ་ཡིན་ནོ (one of two consecutive མ syllables dropped), since the reliance on the Dharma rather than the person requires the negative. Likewise "a synonym for the Tathāgata" reads tshig bla dags for the written tshul bla dags. The names of the king and queen of Vaiśālī are given in English translation, as their Sanskrit forms could not be identified with confidence. "Saindhava" (Tibetan སིནྡྷ་པ) is the classic example of a word with several senses (salt, water, horse, and in this sūtra a sword, all "things from the Indus"), so it is left untranslated. "True man" renders སྐྱེས་པ (Skt. puruṣa), in the Mahāparinirvāṇa Sūtra's teaching that knowing one's buddha-nature is what makes one a "man". In the Questions of Ugra passage, the wife images are uncertain: ཁོག་ཚེ (rendered "trap") is unclear, and ཁྲི་མོན (rendered "jailer") is obscure. ཕམ་པ་བྱེད་བྱད་པས is read as ཕམ་པ་བྱེད་པས, and ཞིབ as ཞི་བ, "at peace". "Rāhu" renders གཟས, the eclipse demon who seizes the sun and moon. The Three Heaps is the Triskandhaka confession liturgy. The titles རིན་པོ་ཆེའི་མཐའ (taken as the Ratnakoṭi) and འཕགས་པ་སྒྲ་ཆེན་པོ ("the Noble Great Sound") are identified with limited confidence. The names "Victorious Host", "Excellent Gold" and "Golden Color" are translated by meaning, since their Sanskrit forms could not be identified with confidence. In "disparage the followers of the śrāvaka vehicle" the written སྨོན (aspire) is read as སྨོད (disparage), which the context requires. The Buddha's reply to Mañjuśrī ("thereby incurs a karmic obscuration") is translated as it stands, though a negative ("does not incur") may have dropped out. "Comes to nothing" reads སྟོངས་པ for the written སྟོན་པ. The names Precious Parasol and Steadfast Intelligence are translated by meaning. The verse on "the Essence of true beings" refers to the bodhisattva Kṣitigarbha arriving in the guise of a monk. The first of the ten faults (འཚོ་བའི་ཡོ་བྱད་ཀྱིས་དཔའ་ཡང་ཐང་བ) is obscure; "having one's resolve worn down by the requisites of livelihood" is a tentative rendering. Udraka is the Tibetan ཨུ་ཏྲ་ཀ (Udraka Rāmaputra); ཨར་ཀ and དེ་ཤ་ཀུ་པ་ལི་ཀ are given as the Tibetan spells them, since they could not be identified with confidence. The name of the last buddha of the Fortunate Eon, སྣང་མཛད, is translated by its meaning ("Illuminator"). "Whatever is elaborated, that is awakening" is translated as written; the reasoning that follows ("because awakening is free of elaboration") suggests the paradoxical style of the sūtra, though a dropped negative ("whatever is not elaborated") is possible. In the passage on overweening pride, "knowledge" (རིག་པ) in "what arises from knowledge, craving and existence" may be a slip for "ignorance" (མ་རིག་པ); it is translated as it stands. "Parasol" reads གདུགས་ཆེ for the written གཏུགས་ཆེས. "Offering seats" reads འབུལ་བ for the written འདུལ་བ, which does not fit. Among the qualities of irreversibility, the source reads "speaking ill of a monk who upholds the true Dharma, even for the sake of his life"; a negative has evidently dropped out, since the list describes virtues, and it is rendered "not speaking ill". "How then could there be four" reads བཞི for the written ཞི. The future names of Avalokiteśvara and Mahāsthāmaprāpta and of the world-system are translated by meaning. The sentence "apart from the denigration of a bodhisattva, nothing other than a bodhisattva can bring about a wrongful downfall" is obscure in the Tibetan and is translated literally. The buddha names Aśoka, Candanaśrī, Ketuśrī, Aśokaśrī and Puṣpaśrī are standard renderings; the other buddha and world-system names in that list, and the tathāgatas of the Bhadrakalpika passage, are translated by meaning. "Even when it is not matched by conduct" takes the elliptical སྤྱོད་པ་དང་མི་ཡང as "not accompanied by conduct". The Jewel Torch dhāraṇī is the Ratnolkā. In the first passage the source reads "the six perfection of wisdom"; the stray "six" is set aside, since the argument concerns the perfection of wisdom alone over against the others. In the Sarvāstivāda passage, "below" reads འོག for the written འོད. "Even a stump" interprets the written འགལ་བ in light of the well-known saying that one should not harbor malice even toward a burnt stump, let alone a body that has consciousness. "Refuse" reads ལུད for the written ལུང. "One who views emptiness as a thing in itself" renders སྟོང་པ་ཉིད་ཁོ་ནར་ལྟ་བ, the one who takes emptiness itself as a view. In the passage on rejecting the Dharma, the written "speak" (སྨྲ, སྨྲས) makes no sense and is read as "reject" (སྤོང, སྤངས), which the surrounding sentences require. "The monk unlike the Tathāgata" renders དེ་བཞིན་གཤེགས་པ་དང་མི་མཐུན་པའི་དགེ་སློང as written; it may be a slip for "the monk who resembles the Tathāgata". The names Great Yellow, Blissful Conduct, Lamp of the Nāga Lineage, Manifestly Embraced (the written མངོན་པར་དང་བཞེས is unclear) and Light of the Source of Wisdom are translated by meaning. "Musāragalva" renders སྦུག, a gemstone in the standard list of seven precious things. "Composed in lines of eight syllables" is a tentative rendering of ཚེག་བར་བརྒྱད་པར་སྦྱར་བ. In "the Tathāgata, once passed away, no longer exists", the written རྨོངས་ཕན་ཆད is obscure; it is rendered in light of the following sentence, which denies that the unarisen can be called "dead and gone", and the reading is uncertain. "Keeping the Dharma in mind where great crowds gather" renders an obscure phrase (འདུས་པ་འཆིབ་རྗེས་སུ་དྲན་པ). The paradoxes in Mañjuśrī's dialogue (perfecting generosity "by not giving up", patience "by malice" and so on) are deliberate and explained in the passage itself. The opening sentences complete the explanation, begun in the previous piece, of how the perfections are accomplished "by forgetfulness" and "by being inert matter". The names of the hells and of the universal monarch are translated by meaning. "Eaters of excrement", "smells", "incense" and "blood" read ཟ་བ for the written ཟབ. The hell named by a cry, བྱ་ཛ་བ་བྷ, is given as a sound. The final story follows the Vinaya account of the origin of the Śākyas. The sage's name is written both ནག་སྦྱིན and ནག་པོ; both are rendered Kṛṣṇa, the Kṛṣṇadvaipāyana of the Sanskrit accounts. The names of Virūḍhaka's four sons are given as the Tibetan spells them, since they do not clearly match known forms. The city gate is written ཏ་ལ་ལ, read as Potala. "The rest were held back" renders the obscure ལྷག་མ་མ་བ་ཀྱེའོ. "Sisters of the other household" (གཡས་ཁྱིམ) presumably means half-sisters by another mother. "Although it is free of elaboration and of all movement" renders the elliptical སྤྲོས་པ་མེད་ཅིང་རྒྱུ་བ་མེད་པའི་རྒྱུ་ཡིན་ཡང. The three names of the sons, implied but not given here, are Sun-born, Ikṣvāku and Gautama. Several names in the genealogy are transliterations or renderings that could not be identified with confidence and are given as the Tibetan spells or means them: Nupura Kolabulaka, Bika (perhaps Bhadrika), Patramali, Tali, Śebala, Kutatildo, the mountain Kingi, Fully Aware (rab tu tshor ba) and King Double Measure (gnyi tshad). "Seng ge man kal" is taken as Siṃhahanu, father of the four -odana brothers in the standard genealogy. "Yum yar mo sgyu ma" is read as "the younger mother, Māyā," contrasted with the elder, Mahāmāyā. Mi dkar ba ("not white") is taken as the sage Asita ("black"). In the homage scene "lha sbyin" is not read as Devadatta; the deity is Śākyavardhana. "Chad pa" here means royal punishment, not annihilation. "Dge 'dun gyis brkus pa" is read as "stolen from the Saṅgha" (gyi for gyis), as the contrast with the Buddha's and Dharma's property requires. "Chur" in the hide's measurements is read as length. The final two verses are terse and rendered literally. In the verse beginning "Among poisons," the Tibetan reads chos ("Dharma") where the sense and the parallel line in the kimpāka verse require chang ("liquor"); the latter is translated. In "like a fire to the Dharma of liberation," srog byed is read as sreg byed ("that which burns"), and in "the snake of desire" sprul is read as sbrul ("snake"). The poison fruit is the kimpāka, a fruit sweet to taste but deadly. In the stūpa description, "sgo bzhi dod par" is rendered "with four doors set out upon it," and "phyin ri mo dang ldan pa" as "bearing designs"; both phrasings are uncertain. Where the Tibetan has "rnam par grol ba'i ye shes ston pa" ("teaching the wisdom of liberation") in a list of the five pure aggregates, it is taken as the standard "knowledge and vision of liberation" (ston pa read as mthong ba). "Gdong" before "bla na med pa" is read as gdod ("now, at last"). The epithet list is given as it stands, without the usual "arhat." The verbs "dugs shig" and "dus shig" in the cremation instructions are uncertain; they are rendered "cense it" and "set it ablaze." "Bsam pa bzhin dul la ni" is read as "bsam pa bzhin du la la ni" ("according to their dispositions, some"). In "byams pa tshad ma mchis pa," tshad ma is read in the sense of tshad med ("measureless"), not valid cognition. The text gives "ten fearlessnesses," not the usual four, and this is kept. "'Phang lo" on the Buddha's stūpa is rendered "rings," the stacked rings of the spire; the exact architectural sense is uncertain. "Brgyas pa tsam du mya ngan ma byed cig" is read with brgyal ba ("swoon"): "do not grieve to the point of swooning." "Rnyed pa'i 'jig rten gyi khams" is read as mi mjed pa'i, the Sahā world. Khrag khrig is a very large number, rendered here as "trillions." "Dkon mchog gi za ma tog" is taken as the Ratnakaraṇḍa Sūtra (The Jewel Casket). The phrase "sems bshus" in the first verse line is obscure; it is rendered literally as "minds stripped bare." The Tibetan lists "monk" (dge slong) where parallel passages usually have śramaṇa; the text's reading is kept. The passage is the verse account of Sudhana's visit to Avalokiteśvara from the Gaṇḍavyūha; the Tibetan here names that text "sdong po brgyan pa." Several lines are obscure and are rendered as closely as the wording allows: "chu rgyal dpung" (amid the king of waters), "sdang ba'i sems rgyun sa chen ltongs su thar gyur" (taken to mean escaping a chasm into which the hostile cast one), and "nges 'dul gzhan dang zad nus kyis," read as zad mi nus ("powers that never run out"). The verse "sems can rnam dag pa" is taken to describe Avalokiteśvara, though it could also refer to Sudhana.
